failure, normalized-\(\beta\), and half-circulant identities, all three
full Kummer-kernel memberships, and the selected-ancestry break in
\texttt{Ogdoad.Excess}.  The standard interpretation of the script's
nonzero Moore determinants as relative normality remains paper-level
linear algebra.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[self-dual inverse basis and determinant collapse]
\label{prop:cubic-inverse-determinant-collapse}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-inverse-completely-normal}.  For
\(k\geq1\), put
\[
 q=2^{3^{k-1}},\qquad E=L_k,\qquad F=L_{k-1},
\]
and write
\[
 x=\beta_k,\qquad y=x^q,\qquad z=x^{q^2},\qquad
 a=\beta_{k-1}.
\]
Then
\[
 x+y+z=a,\qquad xy+yz+zx=0,\qquad xyz=a.             \tag{\(\mathsf{CN}_4\)}
\]
Consequently the relative trace Gram matrix is diagonal:
\[
 \bigl(\operatorname{Tr}_{E/F}(x^{q^i}x^{q^j})\bigr)_{0\leq i,j<3}
   =a^2 I_3.                                         \tag{\(\mathsf{CN}_5\)}
\]
Thus \((x/a,y/a,z/a)\) is a self-dual normal basis of \(E/F\), and
the immediate Moore determinant contains only the lower selector:
\[
 \det\!\begin{pmatrix}x&y&z\\y&z&x\\z&x&y\end{pmatrix}
 =x^3+y^3+z^3+xyz=a^3.                               \tag{\(\mathsf{CN}_6\)}
\]

There is likewise no intermediate absolute determinant invariant.  Put
\(n=3^k\), \(b_i=\beta_k^{2^i}\), let
\(\mathcal C=(b_{i+j})_{i,j\in\mathbb Z/n\mathbb Z}\), and let
\(\mathcal M\) be the \(\F_2\)-matrix of multiplication by \(\beta_k\)
in the normal basis \((b_j)\).  Then
\[
 \det\mathcal C=1,\qquad
 \mathcal C\mathcal M
  =\operatorname{diag}(b_0,\ldots,b_{n-1})\mathcal C. \tag{\(\mathsf{CN}_7\)}
\]
If \(Q_k\) is the reciprocal minimal polynomial of \(\beta_k\), then
for every \(f\in\F_2[X]\),
\[
 \det f(\mathcal M)
 =\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}f(b_i)
 =\Norm_{L_k/\F_2}(f(\beta_k))
 =\operatorname{Res}(Q_k,f).                        \tag{\(\mathsf{CN}_8\)}
\]
In particular, for a current prime
\(\ell\mid\Phi_n(2)\) and \(t=(2^n-1)/\ell\),
\[
 \det(\mathcal M^t-I)=0
 \iff \beta_k^t=1
 \iff \text{the \(\ell\)-primary condition in \(C_k\) fails}. \tag{\(\mathsf{CN}_9\)}
\]
On failure \(\mathcal M^t-I=0\); on success it is invertible and its
determinant is one.  Hence rank, determinant, resultant, and dual-basis
coordinates provide no state strictly between the original selected Euler
test's zero and nonzero outcomes.

Finally this collapse persists for crossed Frobenius determinants.  Let
\(S(u)=u^q\), put \(\vartheta=\beta_k^t\), and let \(A\) be multiplication
by \(\vartheta\).  Since
\(\Norm_{E/F}(\vartheta)=1\), Hilbert 90 gives
\(\vartheta=v^{q-1}\), and therefore
\[
 M_v^{-1}SM_v=AS.                                   \tag{\(\mathsf{CN}_{10}\)}
\]
Thus every characteristic-polynomial or determinant invariant of the
twisted Frobenius \(AS\) is the corresponding invariant of \(S\), whether
or not \(\vartheta=1\).  The only untwisted discriminator
\(\det(A-I)\) is precisely \((\mathsf{CN}_9)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Equation~\((\mathsf{CN}_3)\) is the relative minimal polynomial
\[
 X^3+aX^2+a
\]
of \(x\), so its elementary symmetric coefficients give
\((\mathsf{CN}_4)\).  The diagonal Gram entry is
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{E/F}(x^2)=x^2+y^2+z^2=a^2,
\]
whereas every off-diagonal entry is
\(xy+yz+zx=0\).  Complete normality supplies the basis assertion.
In characteristic two,
\[
 x^3+y^3+z^3+xyz
 =(x+y+z)^3+(x+y+z)(xy+yz+zx)=a^3,
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{CN}_5)\)--\((\mathsf{CN}_6)\).  These two
polynomial calculations are independently kernel-checked in
\texttt{Ogdoad.Excess}.

The matrix \(\mathcal C\) is invertible because \((b_i)\) is a normal
basis.  Squaring every entry cyclically permutes its rows, so
\((\det\mathcal C)^2=\det\mathcal C\); nonvanishing over \(\F_2\) gives
\(\det\mathcal C=1\).  Evaluating multiplication by \(\beta_k\) in
each Frobenius embedding gives the second identity in
\((\mathsf{CN}_7)\), and taking determinants proves
\((\mathsf{CN}_8)\).  Since multiplication by a nonzero field element
is invertible, \(\mathcal M^t-I\) is either the zero matrix when
\(\beta_k^t=1\), or invertible otherwise.  Its nonzero determinant lies
in \(\F_2^\times=\{1\}\), proving \((\mathsf{CN}_9)\).

For the final assertion,
\(\ell\mid q^2+q+1\), so
\(\Norm_{E/F}(\vartheta)=\vartheta^{q^2+q+1}=1\).
Hilbert 90 and the direct calculation
\[
 v^{-1}S(vu)=v^{q-1}S(u)=\vartheta S(u)
\]
give \((\mathsf{CN}_{10})\).  Lean checks this generic conjugacy and
the corresponding norm-one order-three twist identity.  The
normal-basis, finite-field norm, Hilbert--90, and resultant
interpretations remain paper-level.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Singer-orbit sieve]\label{prop:singer-sieve}
Let $k\geq1$.  Put $n=3^k$, $q=2^{n/3}$, and
$N=q^2+q+1=|U_k|$.  If a prime $\ell\mid N$ satisfies
\[
  \ell>\frac{N}{n(n-1)+1},
\]
then $\eta_k\notin U_k^\ell$.  Consequently, if
$\ell^a\Vert N$, then
\[
  v_\ell(\ord\eta_k)=a.
\]
Unconditionally,
\[
  \ord(\eta_k)\geq n(n-1)+1,
  \qquad
  [U_k:\langle\eta_k\rangle]
  \leq\frac{N}{n(n-1)+1}.
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let
\[
  D=\{y\in U_k:y^{q+1}+y+1=0\}.
\]
This is the cyclic $(N,q+1,1)$ Singer difference set: every
nonidentity element of $U_k$ has a unique expression $xy^{-1}$ with
distinct $x,y\in D$.  Since the minimal polynomial of $\eta_k$ over
$\F_2$ has degree $n$, its Frobenius orbit
\[
  A=\{\eta_k^{2^i}:0\leq i<n\}
\]
has $n$ elements.  The equation defining $D$ is Frobenius-stable, so
$A\subset D$.

Suppose $\eta_k\in H:=U_k^\ell$.  The subgroup $H$ is characteristic
in the cyclic group $U_k$, hence Frobenius-stable, and $A\subset H$.
The $n(n-1)$ quotients $xy^{-1}$ for ordered distinct pairs
$(x,y)\in A^2$ are nonidentity elements of $H$ and are all distinct by
the Singer difference-set property.  Therefore
\[
  |H|-1\geq n(n-1).
\]
Since $|H|=N/\ell$, this contradicts the displayed strict inequality.
In a cyclic group of order $\ell^a m$, $\ell\nmid m$, lying outside
the subgroup of $\ell$-th powers is equivalent to having the full
$\ell^a$ part in one's order, proving the first assertion.

Finally the same distinct quotients all lie in
$\langle\eta_k\rangle$, whether or not an $\ell$ is fixed.  Thus
$\ord(\eta_k)-1\geq n(n-1)$, which gives the last two inequalities.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[why the translated orbit does not double the sieve]
The identity $y+1=y^{q+1}$ puts the translated Frobenius orbit
$A+1=A^{q+1}$ in the same subgroup as $A$.  It supplies no second
quotient family: modulo $N$ one has $(q+1)^2\equiv q$ and
$q(q+1)\equiv-1$, while the quotient set of $A$ is stable under both
$q$-th powers and inversion.  Hence the internal quotient sets of
$A$ and $A+1$ coincide.  Cross quotients already collide, for example
$(y+1)/y=y^q$.  Improving Proposition~\ref{prop:singer-sieve} therefore
requires cross-correlation or character information beyond the
$(N,q+1,1)$ difference-set axiom.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[partition-order bound]\label{prop:cubic-partitions}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:singer-sieve}.  Let
\(B(n)\) be the number of partitions into distinct positive parts not
divisible by \(3\) whose total is at most \(n=3^k\).  Then
\[
  \ord(\eta_k)\geq B(n).
\]
In particular, if \(p^{\rm dist}_{\not\,3}(n)\) counts such partitions
of \(n\) itself, then
\[
 \ord(\eta_k)\geq p^{\rm dist}_{\not\,3}(n)
 =\exp\!\left(\left(\frac{\sqrt2\,\pi}{3}+o(1)\right)\sqrt n\right).
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf B\)}
\]
If a prime \(\ell\mid N\) satisfies
\[
  \ell>\frac{N}{B(n)},
\]
then \(\eta_k\notin U_k^\ell\), so the order of \(\eta_k\) contains
the full \(\ell\)-primary part of \(N\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put \(r=3n\), \(t=\zeta^2\), and retain
\(\omega=\zeta^n\), so \(t^n=\omega^2\) and
\[
 \eta_k=M(t),\qquad
 M(X)=\frac{\omega+\omega^2X}{1+X}.
\]
The element \(2\) generates
\((\mathbb Z/r\mathbb Z)^\times\), of order \(2n\).  For every
positive \(j\) not divisible by \(3\), choose \(z_j\) with
\(2^{z_j}\equiv j\pmod r\).  Frobenius gives
\[
 \eta_k^{2^{z_j}}
 =\omega^j\frac{1+\omega^jt^j}{1+t^j}.                 \tag{\(6\)}
\]
For a finite set \(I\) of such \(j\), put
\[
 s_I=\sum_{j\in I}j,\quad E_I=\sum_{j\in I}2^{z_j},\quad
 A_I(X)=\prod_{j\in I}(1+\omega^jX^j),\quad
 B_I(X)=\prod_{j\in I}(1+X^j).
\]
Thus
\[
 \eta_k^{E_I}=\omega^{s_I}\frac{A_I(t)}{B_I(t)}.       \tag{\(7\)}
\]
All denominators are nonzero when \(s_I\leq n<r\).

Suppose \(s_I,s_J\leq n\) and
\(\eta_k^{E_I}=\eta_k^{E_J}\).  Cross-multiplication gives
\[
 F(X):=\omega^{s_I}A_I(X)B_J(X)
       +\omega^{s_J}A_J(X)B_I(X),\qquad F(t)=0.         \tag{\(8\)}
\]
Now \(M(t^{-1})=M(t)+1=\eta_k+1\), and the Singer equation
\(\eta_k+1=\eta_k^{q+1}\) shows that the assumed equality also holds
after replacing \(\eta_k\) by \(M(t^{-1})\).  Hence \(F(t^{-1})=0\).
Over \(\F_4\), the two elements \(t,t^{-1}\) have the distinct
degree-\(n\) minimal polynomials
\[
 X^n+\omega^2,\qquad X^n+\omega,
\]
because \(\ord_r(4)=n\).  Therefore
\[
 X^{2n}+X^n+1\mid F(X).                                \tag{\(9\)}
\]
But \(\deg F\leq s_I+s_J\leq2n\).  In the only possible equality
case \(s_I=s_J=n\), the two leading coefficients are both
\(\omega^{2n}\) and cancel in characteristic two.  Thus
\(\deg F<2n\), and (9) forces \(F=0\).

The constant coefficient first gives
\(\omega^{s_I}=\omega^{s_J}\).  Cancel that scalar and all factors
belonging to \(I\cap J\).  If \(j_0\) is the least remaining part,
comparison of the \(X^{j_0}\)-coefficients gives
\(\omega^{j_0}=1\): on one side \(j_0\) occurs in an \(A\)-factor,
with coefficient \(\omega^{j_0}\), and on the other in a \(B\)-factor,
with coefficient \(1\); larger parts cannot contribute.  This is
impossible because \(3\nmid j_0\).  Hence \(I=J\).

The \(B(n)\) powers in (7) are therefore pairwise distinct, proving
the order bound.  Meinardus's partition theorem~\cite{Meinardus54},
applied to the Euler product
\[
 G(x):=\prod_{3\nmid j}(1+x^j)
 =\frac{\prod_{j\geq1}(1+x^j)}{\prod_{j\geq1}(1+x^{3j})},
 \qquad
 \log G(e^{-t})\sim\frac{\pi^2}{18t},
\]
gives \((\mathsf B)\).  Finally, if
\(\eta_k\in U_k^\ell\), all \(B(n)\) displayed powers lie in the
subgroup of order \(N/\ell\).  The strict inequality rules this out;
the cyclic-group argument of Proposition~\ref{prop:singer-sieve}
then gives the full \(\ell\)-primary assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[ceiling of the height-budgeted amplification]
The preceding construction is scale-optimal within the following
precise class of degree arguments.  For
\(J=(\mathbb Z/3n\mathbb Z)^\times/\{\pm1\}\), assign an atom indexed
by \(j\in J\) the height
\[
 h(j):=\min\{|b|:b\equiv\pm j\pmod{3n}\}.
\]
Suppose a family of integer exponent vectors is certified pairwise
distinct only by bounding the Laurent span after cross-multiplication
with the triangle estimate
\[
 w(e-e'):=\sum_{j\in J}h(j)|e_j-e'_j|\leq2n
\]
and then invoking the two degree-\(n\) minimal polynomials as above
(including the verified leading-term cancellation at the boundary).
Then the family has cardinality \(\exp(O(\sqrt n))\).

Indeed, fix one vector \(e_0\).  Every difference \(e-e_0\) lies in
the displayed weighted \(\ell^1\)-ball.  Each positive height occurs
only \(O(1)\) times, so the number of such lattice points is bounded
by the coefficients through degree \(2n\) of a fixed power of
\[
 \prod_{m\geq1}
 \bigl(1+2x^m+2x^{2m}+\cdots\bigr)
 \preccurlyeq
 \prod_{m\geq1}(1-x^m)^{-2}.
\]
Colored-partition bounds give \(\exp(O(\sqrt n))\).  Polynomially many
Frobenius or cyclic shifts do not change that scale, whereas
\(|U_k|=\exp((2\log2/3+o(1))n)\).  Thus more triangle-budgeted subset
encodings cannot prove primitivity.  This does \emph{not} rule out
systematic factor cancellations or exact reduction modulo
\(\Phi_{3n}\); those require new arithmetic input rather than the
generic degree budget.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The exact remaining character test}

\begin{proposition}[no transfer and the exact character test]
\label{prop:character-test}
The kernel orders are pairwise coprime: for $1\leq i<k$,
\[
 \gcd\!\left(\Phi_{3^i}(2),\Phi_{3^k}(2)\right)=1.
\]
For each prime $\ell\mid |U_k|$, let
$\chi_{k,\ell}:U_k\to\mu_\ell$ be a character of exact order $\ell$.
Then
\[
 \operatorname{Hom}\!\left(
 \F_{2^{3^{k-1}}}^{\times},\mu_\ell\right)=1; \tag{\(\ddagger_0\)}
\]
no multiplicative character or power-residue datum from a previous
level can transfer an $\ell$-part to level $k$.  Moreover,
\[
 C_k\iff C_{k-1}\ \text{and}\
 \chi_{k,\ell}(\eta_k)\neq1
 \quad\text{for every prime }\ell\mid |U_k|.       \tag{\(\ddagger\)}
\]
Equivalently, with
\[
 \rho_{k,\ell}(A)=\chi_{k,\ell}(A^{q_k-1})
 \quad(A\in\F_{q_k^3}^{\times}),
\]
where $\rho_{k,\ell}$ is trivial on $\F_{q_k}^{\times}$, the missing
assertion is $\rho_{k,\ell}(\gamma_k)\neq1$ for every such $\ell$.
This is the smallest power-residue form of the cubic obstruction.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
If a prime $r$ divided two displayed cyclotomic values, then
$r\neq3$ and the standard cyclotomic-divisor criterion would give both
$\ord_r(2)=3^i$ and $\ord_r(2)=3^k$, a contradiction.  Thus every
$\ell\mid |U_k|$ is coprime to
$|\F_{2^{3^{k-1}}}^{\times}|=\prod_{j<k}\Phi_{3^j}(2)$, proving
\((\ddagger_0)\).  In the cyclic group $U_k$, the kernel of an exact
order-$\ell$ character is $U_k^\ell$.  If
$|U_k|=\ell^a m$ with $\ell\nmid m$, then an element has the full
$\ell^a$ in its order exactly when it is not in $U_k^\ell$.  Applying
this for every prime divisor and using \((**)\) proves
\((\ddagger)\).  The formula for $\rho_{k,\ell}$ and
$\eta_k=\gamma_k^{q_k-1}$ give the final equivalence.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[short cyclotomic-period form of the cubic obstruction]
\label{prop:cubic-short-period}
Let \(k\geq1\), put $n=3^k$, $q=2^{n/3}$, and let
$\ell$ be a prime divisor of $\Phi_{3^k}(2)$.  Let
$\rho=\rho_{k,\ell}$ be the exact
$\ell$-character from Proposition~\ref{prop:character-test}, realized
with values in the complex roots of unity.  In
$\F_{2^{2n}}$ let $\mu_{3n}$ denote the roots of unity of order
dividing $3n=3^{k+1}$, and set
\[
 \mathcal S_{k,\ell}
 :=\sum_{\substack{t\in\mu_{3n}\\t\ne1}}
       \rho(t+t^{-1}).                                    \tag{\(\mathsf S\)}
\]
Every argument of $\rho$ in this sum is a nonzero element of
$\F_{2^n}$.  If $\rho(\gamma_k)=\zeta_\ell^a$, then
\[
 \mathcal S_{k,\ell}
 =(n-1)+2\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\zeta_\ell^{a2^i}.              \tag{\(\mathsf P\)}
\]
Consequently
\[
 \chi_{k,\ell}(\eta_k)=1
 \iff \mathcal S_{k,\ell}=3n-1.                          \tag{\(\mathsf C\)}
\]
Thus the surviving datum is exactly a short Gauss period over the
order-$n$ subgroup generated by $2$ in
$(\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z)^\times$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since $2^n\equiv-1\pmod{3n}$, every $t+t^{-1}$ in
\((\mathsf S)\) is fixed by $2^n$ and lies in $\F_{2^n}$; it is
nonzero unless $t=1$.  The $n-1$ nonidentity elements of
$\mu_n$ give real cyclotomic elements in the lower field
$\F_{2^{n/3}}^\times$, on which $\rho$ is trivial.

The remaining $2n$ elements are the primitive $3n$-th roots.  Pairing
them by inversion leaves the $n$ Frobenius conjugates
$\gamma_k^{2^i}$, each twice.  This proves \((\mathsf P)\).  Since
$q-1$ is invertible modulo $\ell$,
$\chi_{k,\ell}(\eta_k)=1$ exactly when
$\rho(\gamma_k)=1$, which gives the forward implication in
\((\mathsf C)\).  Conversely every summand in \((\mathsf S)\) has
complex absolute value one.  Equality with the number of summands
$3n-1$ forces equality in the triangle inequality and hence forces
every summand to be $1$, in particular $\rho(\gamma_k)=1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[why a full Singer character sum is too coarse]
The Singer difference-set character sum controls a set of size $q+1$
and has the usual square-root magnitude.  Equation~\((\mathsf P)\)
instead localizes to only $3n-1$ cyclotomic terms, with the unknown
part supported on the order-$n$ subgroup $\langle2\rangle$ modulo
$\ell$.  Generic Weil or difference-set bounds do not evaluate this
localized scalar; an all-level proof must rule out its extremal value
in \((\mathsf C)\).
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[cyclotomic-secant saturation]
\label{prop:cubic-secant-saturation}
Retain $n=3^k$, put $m=3n$, and let
$K=\F_{2^{2n}}$ contain a primitive $m$-th root \(\zeta\).  For
every prime \(\ell\mid\Phi_{3^k}(2)\), the following are equivalent:

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item \(\eta_k\in U_k^\ell\);
\item \(\gamma_k\in(\F_{2^n}^\times)^\ell\);
\item \(1+\zeta\in(K^\times)^\ell\);
\item for every \(a\not\equiv b\pmod m\),
      \(\zeta^a+\zeta^b\in(K^\times)^\ell\).
\end{enumerate}

Thus, for an exact order-\(\ell\) character \(\chi\) of \(K^\times\),
failure is also equivalent to the extremal identity

\[
 \sum_{\substack{t\in\mu_m\\t\ne1}}\chi(1-t)=m-1.       \tag{\(\mathsf{CS}\)}
\]

It saturates every cyclotomic secant, not only the Frobenius orbit of
the distinguished Gaussian period.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The first equivalence follows from

\[
 \eta_k=\gamma_k^{2^{n/3}-1},
\]

because \(\ell\nmid2^{n/3}-1\).  Next,

\[
 \gamma_k=\zeta^{-1}(1+\zeta)^2.
\]

The order of \(\zeta\) is coprime to \(\ell\), and squaring is
invertible on the \(\ell\)-primary quotient.  Moreover the inclusion
\(\F_{2^n}^\times\subset K^\times\) preserves that quotient, since
\(\ell\nmid2^n+1\).  This proves the equivalence with (iii).

Write \(d=b-a\).  The factor \(\zeta^a\) is automatically an
\(\ell\)-th power.  If \(3\nmid d\), then (2) generates
\((\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z)^\times\), so \(d\equiv2^j\pmod m\) and

\[
 1+\zeta^d=(1+\zeta)^{2^j}.
\]

If \(3\mid d\), then \(1+\zeta^d\in\F_{2^{2n/3}}^\times\).  This
group has order coprime to \(\ell\), because
\(\ord_\ell(2)=n\), so all its elements are \(\ell\)-th powers in
\(K\).  This proves (iii)\(\Rightarrow\)(iv); the converse is the
case \(a=0,b=1\).

Finally, all terms in \((\mathsf{CS})\) have complex absolute value
one.  Part (iv) makes them all one; conversely equality with the
positive real number \(m-1\) forces every term to be one by equality
in the triangle inequality.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the mixed-Jacobi top line of the cubic obstruction]
\label{prop:cubic-mixed-jacobi-top}
Let \(k\geq1\), put \(n=3^k\), \(m=3n\), and let
\(\ell\mid\Phi_n(2)\) be prime.  Put \(Q=2^{2n}\), choose a
generator \(g\in\F_Q^\times\), and set
\[
 z=g^{(Q-1)/m}.
\]
Thus \(z\) is a primitive \(m\)-th root of unity.  Define
\(\operatorname{ind}_\ell(g^v)=v\pmod\ell\), and let
\(\chi_m,\chi_\ell\) be the multiplicative characters, extended by
zero at zero, determined by
\[
 \chi_m(g)=\zeta_m,\qquad \chi_\ell(g)=\zeta_\ell.
\]
For \(1\leq j<m\), form the mixed Jacobi sums
\[
 J_j=\sum_{x\in\F_Q}\chi_m(x)^j\chi_\ell(1-x).
\]
Fix a prime above \(\ell\), put \(\pi=\zeta_\ell-1\), and write
\(\omega\) for the reduction of \(\zeta_m\).  Then
\[
 J_j\equiv-1+\pi C_j\pmod{\pi^2},\qquad
 C_j=\sum_{a=1}^{m-1}\omega^{ja}L(a),
 \quad L(a)=\operatorname{ind}_\ell(1-z^a).           \tag{\(\mathsf{MJ}_1\)}
\]

There is a primitive even character
\[
 \lambda:(\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z)^\times\longrightarrow\F_\ell^\times,
 \qquad \lambda(\mathord\pm2^i)=2^i\quad(0\leq i<n),
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{MJ}_2\)}
\]
and
\[
 L(a)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&3\mid a,\\
  \lambda(a)L(1),&(a,m)=1.
 \end{cases}                                                \tag{\(\mathsf{MJ}_3\)}
\]
Consequently, if
\[
 G_j=\sum_{a\in(\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z)^\times}
       \lambda(a)\omega^{ja},
\]
then \(C_j=L(1)G_j\), and
\[
 G_j=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&3\mid j,\\
  \lambda(j)^{-1}G_1,&(j,m)=1,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad
 G_1G(\lambda^{-1})=\lambda(-1)m=m\ne0.             \tag{\(\mathsf{MJ}_4\)}
\]
Here
\(G(\lambda^{-1})=\sum_{a\in(\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z)^\times}
\lambda(a)^{-1}\omega^a\).
In particular \(G_1\ne0\), and
\[
\begin{split}
 \gamma_k\notin(\F_{2^n}^\times)^\ell
 &\iff 1-z\notin(\F_Q^\times)^\ell\\
 &\iff L(1)\ne0\\
 &\iff C_1\ne0\\
 &\iff J_1\not\equiv-1\pmod{\pi^2}.
\end{split}                                          \tag{\(\mathsf{MJ}_5\)}
\]
Thus the entire first-order Jacobi vector is supported on the
primitive top-\(\Phi_m\) Fourier coordinates, and every primitive
coordinate is a known nonzero multiple of the one selected coordinate
\(L(1)\).  The remaining cubic theorem is precisely the all-level
assertion
\[
 v_\pi(J_1+1)=1.                                    \tag{\(\mathsf{MJ}_6\)}
\]
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The cyclotomic-divisor criterion gives
\(\ord_\ell(2)=n\), while \(\ord_m(2)=2n\).  Hence
\(m\ell\mid Q-1\).

Expand
\(\zeta_\ell^v=(1+\pi)^v\equiv1+v\pi\pmod{\pi^2}\)
in the defining Jacobi sum.  Its constant term is \(-1\).  To obtain
the linear term, partition \(\F_Q^\times\) by the value of the
discrete logarithm modulo \(m\).  For a nonzero class \(a\),
\[
 \prod_{v\equiv a\, (m)}(1-g^v)=1-z^a;
\]
this is the polynomial identity for the coset of the subgroup
\(\langle g^m\rangle\), evaluated at one.  Taking
\(\ell\)-indices gives the displayed coefficient.  In the zero class,
the term \(x=1\) is omitted by the zero-extension of \(\chi_\ell\), and
\[
 \prod_{h\in\langle g^m\rangle\setminus\{1\}}(1-h)
 =\frac{Q-1}{m}=1\quad\hbox{in characteristic two};
\]
its index is therefore zero.  This proves \((\mathsf{MJ}_1)\).

The group \((\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z)^\times\) is generated by \(2\),
has order \(2n\), and satisfies \(2^n=-1\pmod m\).  Since \(2\) has
order \(n\) modulo \(\ell\), the rule in \((\mathsf{MJ}_2)\) is
well-defined and multiplicative.  It is even, and its kernel is
exactly \(\{\mathord\pm1\}\).  It cannot factor through modulus
\(m/3\): the kernel of reduction to
\((\mathbb Z/(m/3)\mathbb Z)^\times\) has order three, whereas
\(\ker\lambda\) has order two.  Thus its conductor is \(m\).

If \(3\mid a\), then \(1-z^a\in\F_{2^{2n/3}}^\times\), whose order
is coprime to \(\ell\), because
\(\ord_\ell(2)=n\nmid2n/3\).  Hence \(L(a)=0\).  If
\((a,m)=1\), write \(a\equiv\mathord\pm2^i\pmod m\).  Frobenius
gives
\[
 \operatorname{ind}_\ell(1-z^{2^i})
 =2^i\operatorname{ind}_\ell(1-z).
\]
For the negative sign use
\(1-z^{-2^i}=z^{-2^i}(1-z^{2^i})\); the \(m\)-torsion factor has
zero \(\ell\)-index.  This proves \((\mathsf{MJ}_3)\), and hence
\(C_j=L(1)G_j\).

For \((j,m)=1\), changing variables gives
\(G_j=\lambda(j)^{-1}G_1\).  If \(3\mid j\), the additive factor is
constant on the three lifts of a unit modulo \(m/3\), while
\(\lambda\) is nontrivial on that order-three kernel, so each fibre
sums to zero.  Finally the standard Gauss identity gives
\[
 G(\lambda)G(\lambda^{-1})=\lambda(-1)m=m.
\]
Since \(\ell\nmid m\), this proves \((\mathsf{MJ}_4)\) and
\(G_1\ne0\).

The equivalence between \(L(1)=0\) and \(1-z\) being an
\(\ell\)-th power is the definition of the index.  Up to a Frobenius
conjugate, the selected period satisfies
\[
 \gamma_k=z+z^{-1}=z^{-1}(1+z)^2.
\]
The \(m\)-torsion factor is an \(\ell\)-th power, squaring is
invertible on the odd \(\ell\)-Kummer quotient, and extension from
\(\F_{2^n}\) to \(\F_Q\) preserves that quotient because
\(\ell\nmid2^n+1\).  This proves \((\mathsf{MJ}_5)\).  Finally,
the indices coprime to \(m\) are exactly the primitive additive
characters of the cyclic group of order \(m\); the other indices are
divisible by three.  Thus \((\mathsf{MJ}_4)\) is precisely the claimed
top-component statement, and \((\mathsf{MJ}_6)\) is the surviving
nonvanishing condition.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[higher Jacobi congruences do not bootstrap the first line]
\label{rem:cubic-higher-jacobi-no-go}
Arul's higher congruence theorem~\cite[Theorem~1.5]{Arul21}, stated for
a second prime conductor, expands
\[
 J+1=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}\pi^iA_i\pmod{\pi^\ell}.
\]
The coefficient field is unramified at \(\ell\), so distinct nonzero
summands have distinct valuations \(i/(\ell-1)\) modulo the integers
and cannot cancel.  In particular
\[
 v_\pi(J+1)=1\iff A_1\ne0.
\]
Higher congruences impose additional power-residue conditions only
after \(A_1=0\); they cannot force \(A_1\ne0\) from \(A_2,A_3,\ldots\).
Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-mixed-jacobi-top} proves the required
composite-conductor first-order analogue directly, with
\(A_1=C_1=G_1L(1)\) and \(G_1\ne0\).  Hence this route again stops at
the selected Kummer index \(L(1)\), rather than providing an induction
from a deeper Jacobi congruence.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[the selected weighted Jacobi resolvent]
\label{cor:cubic-weighted-jacobi-resolvent}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-mixed-jacobi-top}, put
\(U=(\mathbb Z/m\mathbb Z)^\times\), and choose integer lifts
\(w_j\equiv\lambda(j)\pmod\ell\).  Then
\[
 \mathcal R_\lambda
 :=\prod_{j\in U}(-J_j)^{w_j}
 \equiv1-2n\pi C_1\pmod{\pi^2}.                     \tag{\(\mathsf{WR}_1\)}
\]
Consequently the cubic arm at \((k,\ell)\) is equivalent to the one
weighted congruence
\[
 \mathcal R_\lambda\not\equiv1\pmod{\pi^2}.         \tag{\(\mathsf{WR}_2\)}
\]
By contrast, the full unweighted primitive product is identically flat:
\[
 \prod_{j\in U}(-J_j)\equiv1\pmod{\pi^2}.           \tag{\(\mathsf{WR}_3\)}
\]
Thus an unweighted Hasse--Davenport relation necessarily loses the
selected first-order phase; a proof must evaluate a genuinely weighted
resolvent such as \(\mathcal R_\lambda\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since \(-J_j\equiv1-\pi C_j\pmod{\pi^2}\), first-order expansion and
Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-mixed-jacobi-top} give
\[
 \mathcal R_\lambda
 \equiv1-\pi\sum_{j\in U}w_jC_j
 =1-\pi\sum_{j\in U}\lambda(j)\lambda(j)^{-1}C_1
 =1-2n\pi C_1.
\]
Now \(\ell\nmid2n\), and \(C_1=G_1L(1)\) with \(G_1\ne0\), proving
\((\mathsf{WR}_2)\).  With every weight equal to one, the linear term
instead contains
\(C_1\sum_{j\in U}\lambda(j)^{-1}=0\), which proves
\((\mathsf{WR}_3)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[the first Jacobi phase is the selected current projection]
\label{cor:cubic-jacobi-current-projection}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-mixed-jacobi-top}.  Put

\[
 t=\frac{2^n-1}{\ell},\qquad s=\frac{m+1}{2},\qquad
 r\equiv st\pmod m,
\]

write \(I\) for the binary support of \(t\), and set

\[
 \rho=g^{(Q-1)/\ell},\qquad
 \Theta=z^{-r}\prod_{i\in I}(1+z^{2^i})
       =z^{-r}(1+z)^t.                                \tag{\(\mathsf{CJ}_1\)}
\]

Then

\[
 \Theta\in\mu_\ell,\qquad
 \Theta^2=\rho^{L(1)}.                               \tag{\(\mathsf{CJ}_2\)}
\]

Consequently, if \(\log_\rho:\mu_\ell\to\mathbb F_\ell\) is the
discrete logarithm with base \(\rho\), then

\[
 C_1=2G_1\log_\rho(\Theta),                          \tag{\(\mathsf{CJ}_3\)}
\]

and

\[
 v_{\zeta_\ell-1}(J_1+1)=1
 \iff \Theta\ne1.                                    \tag{\(\mathsf{CJ}_4\)}
\]

Thus the first Jacobi derivative is literally the discrete logarithm of
the selected current \(\ell\)-projection.  Its Fourier and binary-support
forms locate the missing scalar but do not replace its evaluation.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Since \(2^n\equiv-1\pmod m\),

\[
 (1+z)^{2^n-1}=z^{-1}.
\]

Also \(\ell t=2^n-1\) and \(2r\equiv t\pmod m\), whence
\(r\ell\equiv-1\pmod m\).  Therefore

\[
 \Theta^\ell
 =z^{-r\ell}(1+z)^{2^n-1}=1.
\]

On the other hand,

\[
 \Theta^{2^n+1}
 =z^{-r(2^n+1)}(1+z)^{t(2^n+1)}
 =(1+z)^{(Q-1)/\ell}
 =\rho^{L(1)}.
\]

The omitted torsion factor is one because \(m\mid r(2^n+1)\).
Since \(2^n+1\equiv2\pmod\ell\), this proves
\((\mathsf{CJ}_2)\).  Taking the \(\rho\)-logarithm gives
\(L(1)=2\log_\rho(\Theta)\); now
Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-mixed-jacobi-top} gives
\(C_1=G_1L(1)\) with \(G_1\ne0\), proving the remaining assertions.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[real square resultant and the failed size strategy]
\label{prop:cubic-real-resultant}
Retain Corollary~\ref{cor:cubic-jacobi-current-projection}, and put
\[
 K=\mathbb Q(\zeta_m),\qquad K^+=\mathbb Q(\zeta_m+\zeta_m^{-1}),
 \qquad s=\frac{m+1}{2},
\]
\[
 \beta=\zeta_m^{-s}(1+\zeta_m),\qquad
 x=\zeta_m^{-r}(1+\zeta_m)^t=\beta^t.
\]
Then \(\beta,x\in K^+\), and
\[
 \Norm_{K^+/\mathbb Q}(\beta)
 =\Norm_{K^+/\mathbb Q}(x)=-1.                    \tag{\(\mathsf{RN}_1\)}
\]
Let \(F_m(Y)\) be the degree-\(n\) minimal polynomial of \(\beta\),
and set
\[
 A_{k,\ell}=\Norm_{K^+/\mathbb Q}(x-1).
\]
Up to the standard harmless sign convention for the first resultant,
\[
 A_{k,\ell}=\operatorname{Res}(F_m(Y),Y^t-1),        \tag{\(\mathsf{RN}_2\)}
\]
while the full cyclotomic resultant is the exact square
\[
 \operatorname{Res}\!\left(
   \Phi_m(X),(1+X)^t-X^r
 \right)=A_{k,\ell}^{\,2}.                          \tag{\(\mathsf{RN}_3\)}
\]

The prime two is inert in \(K^+\), with residue degree \(n\).  If
\(\Theta\) is the selected phase in \((\mathsf{CJ}_1)\), then
\[
 \begin{split}
 \Theta=1
 &\iff x\equiv1\pmod{(2)}\\
 &\iff 2^n\mid A_{k,\ell}\\
 &\iff 2^{2n}\mid
 \operatorname{Res}\!\left(
   \Phi_m(X),(1+X)^t-X^r
 \right).
 \end{split}
 \tag{\(\mathsf{RN}_4\)}
\]
Thus a bound \(0<|A_{k,\ell}|<2^n\) would prove the cubic arm.
That proposed mechanism is false already in the second row.  For
\[
 k=2,\qquad n=9,\qquad m=27,\qquad \ell=73,\qquad t=7,\qquad r=17,
\]
one has
\[
 F_{27}(Y)=Y^9-9Y^7+27Y^5-30Y^3+9Y+1
\]
and the exact resultant
\[
 |A_{2,73}|=567780193>2^9,                            \tag{\(\mathsf{RN}_5\)}
\]
so the full resultant is \(567780193^2\).  Real descent and unit norm
therefore sharpen failure to an inert-prime divisibility statement,
but do not yield an archimedean contradiction; proving that
\(A_{k,\ell}\) is odd is exactly the original selected residue test.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(2s\equiv1\pmod m\),
\[
 \overline\beta
 =\zeta_m^s(1+\zeta_m^{-1})
 =\zeta_m^{s-1}(1+\zeta_m)=\beta.
\]
For representatives \(1\leq a<m/2\), \(3\nmid a\), of the real
embeddings,
\[
 \sigma_a(\beta)=(-1)^a\,2\cos(\pi a/m).           \tag{\(\mathsf{RN}_6\)}
\]
These values are distinct, so \(\beta\) generates \(K^+\).  The
number of odd representatives is
\((n+(-1)^k)/2\), which is odd because
\(3^k\equiv(-1)^k\pmod4\).  Equation
\((\mathsf{RN}_6)\) proves the first equality in
\((\mathsf{RN}_1)\).  The integer \(t\) is odd, proving the second.

Taking the product of \(\beta^t-1\) over the real embeddings proves
\((\mathsf{RN}_2)\).  Since \(x-1\in K^+\), its norm from \(K\) is
the square of its real norm.  Multiplication by \(\zeta_m^r\) does not
change the full rational norm, and this gives \((\mathsf{RN}_3)\).

The order identities
\[
 \operatorname{ord}_m(2)=2n,\qquad 2^n\equiv-1\pmod m
\]
show that two is inert in \(K\) and in \(K^+\), of residue degrees
\(2n\) and \(n\), respectively.  Thus for the unique prime
\(\mathfrak p\mid2\) in \(K^+\),
\[
 v_2(A_{k,\ell})=n\,v_{\mathfrak p}(x-1).
\]
The reduction of \(x\) is \(\Theta\), proving
\((\mathsf{RN}_4)\).

For the final exact obstruction, the displayed polynomial for
\(F_{27}\) follows by separating the primitive-
twenty-seven factor in the Dickson identity for
\[
 D_{27}(Y)-2=(Y-2)(Y+1)^2(Y^3-3Y+1)^2F_{27}(Y)^2.
\]
Reducing \(F_{27}\) modulo \(Y^7-1\) gives
\[
 27Y^5-30Y^3+Y^2+9Y-8.
\]
The determinant of multiplication by this element on
\(\mathbb Z[Y]/(Y^7-1)\) is
\[
\det\!\begin{pmatrix}
-8&0&27&0&-30&1&9\\
9&-8&0&27&0&-30&1\\
1&9&-8&0&27&0&-30\\
-30&1&9&-8&0&27&0\\
0&-30&1&9&-8&0&27\\
27&0&-30&1&9&-8&0\\
0&27&0&-30&1&9&-8
\end{pmatrix}
=-567780193.
\]
This proves \((\mathsf{RN}_5)\).  Squaring and using
\((\mathsf{RN}_3)\) gives the stated full resultant.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[cyclic parity form of the cubic obstruction]
\label{prop:cubic-cyclic-parity}
Put \(n=3^k\), \(m=3n\), with \(k\geq1\), and let the prime
\(\ell\) divide \(\Phi_n(2)\).  Define
\[
 t=\frac{2^n-1}{\ell},\qquad
 s=\frac{m+1}{2},\qquad
 r\equiv st\pmod m,\quad 0\leq r<m,
\]
and let \(R_{k,\ell}(X)\) be the degree-\(<m\) reduction of
\((1+X)^t\) modulo \(X^m-1\).  Then, independently of cubic failure,
\[
 \frac{X^n-1}{X-1}\mid R_{k,\ell}(X)+X^r.             \tag{\(\mathsf{CP}_1\)}
\]
Moreover, for a primitive \(m\)-th root \(\zeta\) and
\(\gamma_k=\zeta+\zeta^{-1}\),
\[
 \gamma_k\in(\mathbb F_{2^n}^{\times})^\ell
 \iff
 R_{k,\ell}(X)+X^r
 =1+X+\cdots+X^{m-1}.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{CP}_2\)}
\]

Equivalently, write the binary expansion
\[
 t=\sum_{i\in I}2^i.
\]
Then cubic failure at \(\ell\) is exactly the group-algebra identity
\[
 \prod_{i\in I}(1+X^{2^i})
 =\sum_{\substack{0\leq j<m\\j\ne r}}X^j
 \quad\hbox{in }\mathbb F_2[X]/(X^m-1).               \tag{\(\mathsf{CP}_3\)}
\]
Thus, if
\[
 c_j=\#\left\{J\subseteq I:
       \sum_{i\in J}2^i\equiv j\pmod m\right\}\pmod2,
\]
failure says
\[
 c_r=0,\qquad c_j=1\quad(j\ne r).                     \tag{\(\mathsf{CP}_4\)}
\]
The remaining cubic theorem is therefore the universal impossibility
of this complement-of-one parity pattern for the particular binary
set \(I\) coming from \((2^n-1)/\ell\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \(\xi\ne1\) be an \(n\)-th root of unity, of order
\(e=3^{a+1}\), and put \(d=3^a<n\).  The cyclotomic-divisor criterion
gives \(\ord_\ell(2)=n\), so \(\gcd(\ell,2^d-1)=1\).  Since \(d\mid n\),
it follows that
\[
 T:=\frac{t}{2^d-1}\in\mathbb Z.
\]
Now \(2^d\equiv-1\pmod e\), and hence
\[
 (1+\xi)^{2^d-1}=\xi^{-1}.
\]
Also \(t=T(2^d-1)\equiv-2T\pmod e\).  Because
\(2s\equiv1\pmod m\), this gives \(-T\equiv st\equiv r\pmod e\),
and therefore
\[
 R_{k,\ell}(\xi)=(1+\xi)^t=\xi^{-T}=\xi^r.
\]
The polynomial \((X^n-1)/(X-1)\) is squarefree, since \(n\) is odd,
and its roots are exactly these nonidentity \(n\)-th roots.  This
proves \((\mathsf{CP}_1)\).

At the primitive \(m\)-th root \(\zeta\), one has
\[
 \gamma_k=\zeta^{-1}(1+\zeta)^2.
\]
The element \(\gamma_k\) is an \(\ell\)-th power exactly when
\(\gamma_k^t=1\).  Since squaring is bijective in characteristic two,
this is equivalent to
\[
 (1+\zeta)^t=\zeta^{st}=\zeta^r,
\]
and hence to
\[
 \Phi_m(X)\mid R_{k,\ell}(X)+X^r.                    \tag{\(\mathsf{CP}_5\)}
\]
The two factors in \((\mathsf{CP}_1)\) and \((\mathsf{CP}_5)\) are
coprime and satisfy
\[
 \frac{X^n-1}{X-1}\Phi_m(X)
 =\frac{X^m-1}{X-1}=1+X+\cdots+X^{m-1}.
\]
The polynomial \(R_{k,\ell}+X^r\) has degree \(<m\).  Under failure it
is therefore a scalar multiple of the displayed degree-\(m-1\)
polynomial.  Evaluation at \(X=1\), which is preserved by reduction
modulo \(X^m-1\), gives
\[
 (R_{k,\ell}+X^r)(1)=(1+1)^t+1=1;
\]
the all-ones polynomial also has value \(m=1\) in characteristic two.
The scalar is consequently one, proving \((\mathsf{CP}_2)\); the
converse is immediate.

Finally, in the group algebra,
\[
 (1+X)^t=\prod_{i\in I}(1+X^{2^i}).
\]
The coefficient of \(X^j\) after cyclic reduction is the parity of
the subsets with exponent sum congruent to \(j\).  Rewriting
\((\mathsf{CP}_2)\) as
\(R_{k,\ell}=\sum_{j\ne r}X^j\) proves
\((\mathsf{CP}_3)\)--\((\mathsf{CP}_4)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[exceptional residue and primitive CRT isolation]
\label{cor:cubic-primitive-crt-isolation}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-cyclic-parity}, write
\(\ell=1+an\), and set
\[
 S_{k,\ell}(X)=R_{k,\ell}(X)+X^r.
\]
Then \(a\) is even and the exceptional residue is one of exactly three
lifts of \(-1\pmod n\):
\[
 r\equiv an-1\pmod m,
 \qquad
 r=
 \begin{cases}
 m-1,&a\equiv0\pmod3,\\
 n-1,&a\equiv1\pmod3,\\
 2n-1,&a\equiv2\pmod3.
 \end{cases}                                         \tag{\(\mathsf{CP}_6\)}
\]

Moreover, in the squarefree group algebra
\[
 \mathcal A_m=\F_2[X]/(X^m-1)
 \cong
 \F_2\times\prod_{j=1}^{k+1}\F_{2^{2\cdot3^{j-1}}},
\]
the CRT coordinates of \(S_{k,\ell}\) have the form
\[
 S_{k,\ell}=(1,0,\ldots,0,\sigma_{\mathrm{top}}).       \tag{\(\mathsf{CP}_7\)}
\]
Cubic failure is exactly \(\sigma_{\mathrm{top}}=0\).  Thus every
invariant which factors through a proper cyclic projection of
\(C_m\), or otherwise discards the top \(\Phi_m\)-component, is
identical on success and failure.  At a primitive \(m\)-th root
\(\zeta\), the surviving coordinate is precisely the original selected
Kummer test, since
\[
 \zeta^{-r}S_{k,\ell}(\zeta)
 =(1+\zeta)^t\zeta^{-r}+1.                            \tag{\(\mathsf{CP}_8\)}
\]

Finally let \(w\) be the binary weight of \(t\), and let \(C_j\) be the
integer number of binary submasks of \(t\) congruent to \(j\pmod m\).
Failure necessarily implies
\[
 2^w\geq m-1,
 \qquad
 C_r\equiv1-m\pmod4.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{CP}_9\)}
\]
In particular, \(w<\lceil\log_2(m-1)\rceil\) is an unconditional
nonfailure criterion.  These are exact screens, not an evaluation of
the remaining top component.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The equality \(\ord_m(2)=2n\) gives \(2^n\equiv-1\pmod m\).
Since \(\ell\) and \(n\) are odd, \(a\) is even.  Also
\[
 (1+an)^{-1}\equiv1-an\pmod{3n},
\]
because \(3n\mid a^2n^2\).  Hence
\[
 t\equiv-2\ell^{-1}\equiv2an-2\pmod m.
\]
As \(2r\equiv t\pmod m\), this proves
\((\mathsf{CP}_6)\).

The standard factorization
\[
 X^m-1=(X-1)\prod_{j=1}^{k+1}\Phi_{3^j}(X)
\]
is squarefree, and every displayed cyclotomic factor is irreducible
over \(\F_2\), because
\(\ord_{3^j}(2)=2\cdot3^{j-1}\).
Equation \((\mathsf{CP}_1)\) kills the coordinates for
\(1\leq j\leq k\), while \(S_{k,\ell}(1)=1\).  This proves
\((\mathsf{CP}_7)\); equation \((\mathsf{CP}_5)\) says exactly that its
last coordinate vanishes.  Equation \((\mathsf{CP}_8)\) is immediate
from the definition of \(S_{k,\ell}\).

Under failure, the \(2^w\) submasks must occupy all \(m-1\)
nonexceptional residues, proving the first inequality in
\((\mathsf{CP}_9)\).  Complementation gives \(C_j=C_{t-j}\).  The only
fixed residue of this involution is \(r\); every other residue occurs
in one of \((m-1)/2\) pairs, and failure makes the common count in each
pair odd.  Since \(2^w\equiv0\pmod4\) under the first inequality, summing
all counts modulo four gives
\[
 0\equiv C_r+m-1\pmod4,
\]
which completes the proof.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[augmentation-ideal normalization of cubic failure]
\label{cor:cubic-augmentation}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-cyclic-parity}, and put
\[
 A_m=\mathbb F_2[X]/(X^m-1),\qquad
 J_m=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}X^j,\qquad e_m=1+J_m.
\]
The element \(e_m\) is the identity of the augmentation ideal
\(e_mA_m\).  For \(0\leq i<n\), define
\[
 h_i=X^{-s2^i}(1+X^{2^i})
     =X^{s2^i}+X^{-s2^i}\in e_mA_m.
\]
If \(I\) is the binary support of \(t=(2^n-1)/\ell\), then cubic
failure at \(\ell\) is exactly
\[
 \prod_{i\in I}h_i=e_m.                               \tag{\(\mathsf{CA}_1\)}
\]
On the other hand, the full-product relation
\[
 \prod_{i=0}^{n-1}h_i=e_m                             \tag{\(\mathsf{CA}_2\)}
\]
holds unconditionally.  Thus failure makes both the proper digit
subset \(I\) and its complement multiply to the identity in
\(e_mA_m\).

Likewise, raising \((\mathsf{CP}_3)\) to the \(\ell\)-th power yields
only the unconditional identity
\[
 (1+X)^{2^n-1}=J_m+X^{-1}\qquad\text{in }A_m,          \tag{\(\mathsf{CA}_3\)}
\]
because \(r\ell\equiv-1\pmod m\).  Hence neither the full product nor
the strongest immediate exponent check can exclude failure.  The
remaining cubic theorem is precisely the absence of the extra
proper-subproduct relation \((\mathsf{CA}_1)\) for this selected
binary support.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
For \(y\in A_m\), one has \(J_my=\operatorname{aug}(y)J_m\).
Since \(m\) is odd, \(e_m\) has augmentation zero and acts as the
identity on the augmentation ideal.

Write \(P_I=\prod_{i\in I}(1+X^{2^i})\).  Under
\((\mathsf{CP}_3)\), \(P_I=J_m+X^r\), and therefore
\[
 \prod_{i\in I}h_i
 =X^{-st}P_I=X^{-r}(J_m+X^r)=e_m.
\]
Every step is reversible, proving \((\mathsf{CA}_1)\).

For the full product, let \(\xi\) be an \(m\)-th root of unity in an
algebraic closure.  If \(\xi\ne1\), then
\[
 \prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(1+\xi^{2^i})
 =(1+\xi)^{2^n-1}
 =\frac{1+\xi^{2^n}}{1+\xi}
 =\xi^{-1},
\]
because \(2^n\equiv-1\pmod m\).  Also
\(s(2^n-1)\equiv-1\pmod m\), so the monomial factor in the product
of the \(h_i\) contributes \(\xi\).  At \(\xi=1\) the product
vanishes.  These are exactly the values of \(e_m\); since \(m\) is
odd, \(X^m-1\) is squarefree, proving \((\mathsf{CA}_2)\).
It follows that every \(h_i\) is a unit of \(e_mA_m\), so
\((\mathsf{CA}_1)\) also holds for the complementary subset.

Finally \(J_m+X^r=X^re_m\), and \(e_m\) is idempotent.  Raising
\((1+X)^t=X^re_m\) to the \(\ell\)-th power gives
\[
 (1+X)^{2^n-1}=X^{r\ell}e_m=X^{-1}e_m=J_m+X^{-1},
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{CA}_3)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[cubic block compression and forced midpoint]
\label{prop:cubic-block-compression}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-cyclic-parity}.  Put
\[
 d=\frac n3,\qquad q=2^d,\qquad
 N=q^2+q+1,\qquad u=\frac N\ell,
\]
so that \(t=(q-1)u\), and set
\[
 B(X)=1+X+\cdots+X^{n-2}\in A_m,
 \qquad b=X^{-s(q-1)}B.
\]
Then
\[
 (1+X)^{q-1}=B(X)\quad\hbox{in }A_m,                 \tag{\(\mathsf{CB}_1\)}
\]
and cubic failure at \(\ell\) is exactly
\[
 B^u=X^{st}e_m,\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad b^u=e_m.       \tag{\(\mathsf{CB}_2\)}
\]
The normalized element satisfies, unconditionally,
\[
 b(X^{-1})=b(X),\qquad b^N=e_m.                       \tag{\(\mathsf{CB}_3\)}
\]
At a primitive \(m\)-th root \(\zeta\),
\[
 b(\zeta)^2=\eta_k.                                  \tag{\(\mathsf{CB}_4\)}
\]
Thus \((\mathsf{CB}_2)\) is the original selected current-factor
order question in compressed form, rather than a weaker consequence.

Moreover, if \(C_j\) is the number of binary submasks of \(t\) whose
sum is \(j\pmod m\), then
\[
 C_j=C_{t-j}.                                         \tag{\(\mathsf{CB}_5\)}
\]
The unique fixed residue of \(j\mapsto t-j\) is \(r=st\), and
\(C_r\) is even.  Hence complementation forces the exceptional zero
required by \((\mathsf{CP}_4)\) into exactly the right position; it
cannot contradict cubic failure.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The lifted congruence in Proposition~\ref{prop:eta-tower} gives
\[
 q\equiv n-1\pmod{3n},
 \qquad \frac{q-(n-1)}{3n}\equiv0\pmod2.
\]
Since \(q\) is a power of two,
\((1+X)^{q-1}=\sum_{a=0}^{q-1}X^a\).  Cyclic reduction modulo
\(X^m-1\) cancels the even number of complete residue blocks and leaves
exactly \(B\), proving \((\mathsf{CB}_1)\).  Substitution in
\((\mathsf{CP}_2)\), followed by multiplication by
\(X^{-st}\), proves \((\mathsf{CB}_2)\).

Because \(2s\equiv1\pmod m\) and \(q-1\equiv n-2\pmod m\), reversing
\(B\) proves \(b(X^{-1})=b(X)\).  Also
\((q-1)N=2^n-1\).  At every nonidentity \(m\)-th root \(\xi\),
\[
 (1+\xi)^{2^n-1}=\xi^{-1},\qquad
 s(2^n-1)\equiv-1\pmod m,
\]
so \(b(\xi)^N=1\); at \(\xi=1\), \(B(1)=n-1=0\).
Squarefree interpolation proves \(b^N=e_m\).  Finally
\((1+\zeta)^2=\zeta\gamma_k\), whence
\[
 b(\zeta)^2
 =\zeta^{-(q-1)}(1+\zeta)^{2(q-1)}
 =\gamma_k^{q-1}=\eta_k.
\]

The binary integer \(t\) is odd.  Complementing a submask is therefore
fixed-point-free and changes its sum from \(j\) to \(t-j\pmod m\),
which proves \((\mathsf{CB}_5)\).  Since \(m\) is odd, the involution
on residues has the unique fixed point \(r=st\); all submasks above
that point occur in complementary pairs.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[distinguished Wendt-factor obstruction]
\label{prop:cubic-wendt-factor}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-block-compression}, and let
\(P_{\eta,k}\) be the degree-\(n\) minimal polynomial of \(\eta_k\)
over \(\mathbb F_2\).  Define
\[
 G_u(X)=\gcd\bigl(X^u+1,(X+1)^u+1\bigr)\in\mathbb F_2[X].
\]
Then cubic failure at \(\ell\) is equivalent to
\[
 P_{\eta,k}\mid G_u.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{CW}_1\)}
\]
Under failure, the two distinct factors
\[
 P_{\eta,k}(X),\qquad P_{\eta,k}(X+1)                \tag{\(\mathsf{CW}_2\)}
\]
both divide \(G_u\), and the second is the reciprocal partner of the
first.  Every irreducible factor of \(G_u\) has degree exactly \(n\);
translation pairs those factors without fixed points.  Consequently
\[
 G_u=1\qquad\hbox{or}\qquad 2n\mid\deg G_u.           \tag{\(\mathsf{CW}_3\)}
\]
The usual six-element group generated by
\(X\mapsto X+1\) and \(X\mapsto X^{-1}\) acts freely on the roots of
\(G_u\).  Thus the surviving assertion is specifically that the
distinguished pair \((\mathsf{CW}_2)\), not merely some Wendt pair,
never occurs.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Failure says \(\eta_k^u=1\).  Since
\(\eta_k+1=\eta_k^{q+1}\), it also gives
\((\eta_k+1)^u=1\), and Frobenius gives the same two identities for
every conjugate.  This proves \((\mathsf{CW}_1)\) and
\((\mathsf{CW}_2)\); the converse uses the first factor.

The relative trace recursion in Proposition~\ref{prop:eta-tower}
gives
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{\mathbb F_{2^n}/\mathbb F_2}(\eta_k)=0.
\]
As \(n\) is odd, the trace of \(\eta_k+1\) is one, so the two factors
in \((\mathsf{CW}_2)\) are distinct.  Also
\(\eta_k^{-1}=\eta_k^q+1\); hence the translate is the minimal
polynomial of \(\eta_k^{-1}\), namely the reciprocal partner.

Let \(x\) be a root of \(G_u\).  The nontrivial orders of both \(x\)
and \(x+1\) divide \(u\mid\Phi_n(2)\).  Every prime \(p\mid u\)
satisfies \(\ord_p(2)=n\): the only prime dividing \(n\) is three,
and \(3\nmid\Phi_n(2)\).  Thus \(x\) has degree at least \(n\), while
its order divides \(2^n-1\), so its degree is exactly \(n\).
Translation changes the trace by one and therefore pairs the
degree-\(n\) irreducible factors without fixed points, proving
\((\mathsf{CW}_3)\).

Finally none of a root \(x\), its inverse, or its translate is fixed
by an involution: the only possible values would be zero or one.
A fixed point of the order-three map satisfies \(x^2+x+1=0\), hence
has order three, but \(3\nmid u\).  The six-element action is therefore
free.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Singer factorization of the Wendt gcd]
\label{prop:cubic-wendt-singer}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-wendt-factor}.  In
\(L=\mathbb F_{q^3}\), put
\[
 U=\ker\!\left(\Norm_{L/\mathbb F_q}\right)=\mu_N,
 \qquad H=U^\ell=\mu_u,
\]
and let
\[
 D=\{x\in U:x^{q+1}+x+1=0\}.
\]
Thus \(D\) is the planar Singer \((N,q+1,1)\) difference set from
Proposition~\ref{prop:eta-tower}.  Then
\[
 \operatorname{Roots}(G_u)
 =(D\cap H)\mathbin{\dot\cup}\bigl((D\cap H)+1\bigr). \tag{\(\mathsf{SW}_1\)}
\]
If \(\mathcal O\) runs over the absolute-Frobenius orbits in
\(D\cap H\), and \(P_{\mathcal O}\) is the corresponding minimal
polynomial, then
\[
 G_u(X)=\prod_{\mathcal O\subset D\cap H}
 P_{\mathcal O}(X)P_{\mathcal O}(X+1),
 \qquad
 \deg G_u=2|D\cap H|.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{SW}_2\)}
\]
Every orbit \(\mathcal O\) has size \(n\), and
\(P_{\mathcal O}(X+1)\) is its reciprocal partner.

More precisely, choose a generator \(g\) of \(U\), and put
\[
 a_j=|D\cap g^jH|\qquad(j\in\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z).
\]
The quotient intersection numbers obey the exact moment identities
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \sum_j a_j&=q+1,&
 \sum_j a_j^2&=q+u,\\
 \sum_j a_ja_{j+r}&=u\quad(r\ne0),&
 a_{2j}&=a_j.
\end{aligned}                                         \tag{\(\mathsf{SW}_3\)}
\]
In particular, if
\[
 \ell-1<\sqrt q+\frac1{\sqrt q},                      \tag{\(\mathsf{SW}_4\)}
\]
then every \(a_j\) is positive.  In that range
\(a_0\geq n\) and \(\deg G_u\geq2n\): Wendt factors of the correct
degree and symmetry are forced to exist even though the distinguished
factor may still be absent.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
A root \(x\) of \(G_u\) satisfies \(x,x+1\in H\subset U\).  Put
\(y=x^{q+1}\).  Since
\[
 x^q=\frac yx,\qquad x^{q^2}=\frac1y,
\]
the condition \(\Norm(x+1)=1\), after clearing \(xy\), factors as
\[
 (y+x+1)\bigl((x+1)y+x\bigr)=0.                       \tag{\(\mathsf{SW}_5\)}
\]
The first factor says \(x\in D\).  In the second case \(x+1\in D\).
Conversely, if \(x\in D\cap H\), then
\(x+1=x^{q+1}\in H\), so both \(x\) and \(x+1\) are roots of \(G_u\).
The two sets in \((\mathsf{SW}_1)\) are disjoint: if both \(x\) and
\(x+1\) lay in \(D\), their two Frobenius equations would give
\(x^2+x+1=0\), impossible because \(3\nmid N\).

Every nonidentity element of \(H\) has absolute degree \(n\), by the
cyclotomic-divisor argument in
Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-wendt-factor}.  The polynomial \(G_u\) is
squarefree, and \((\mathsf{SW}_1)\) therefore gives
\((\mathsf{SW}_2)\).  For \(x\in D\),
\[
 (x+1)^q=x^{-1},
\]
so translation of its absolute orbit is the inverse orbit, proving
the reciprocal assertion.

The first identity in \((\mathsf{SW}_3)\) is \(|D|=q+1\).
For the second, \(\sum a_j^2\) counts ordered pairs of points of \(D\)
whose quotient lies in \(H\).  The identity quotient contributes
\(q+1\), and each of the other \(u-1\) elements occurs once because
\(D\) has difference parameter one.  If \(r\ne0\), the corresponding
coset has \(u\) nonidentity quotients, proving the third identity.
Squaring stabilizes \(D\) and sends the \(j\)-th coset to the
\(2j\)-th, which proves the last.

If some \(a_j\) vanished, Cauchy's inequality applied to the remaining
\(\ell-1\) entries would give
\[
 (q+1)^2\leq(\ell-1)(q+u).                            \tag{\(\mathsf{SW}_6\)}
\]
But a direct simplification using \(u=N/\ell\) gives
\[
 \ell\bigl((q+1)^2-(\ell-1)(q+u)\bigr)
 =q\left[
 \left(\sqrt q+\frac1{\sqrt q}\right)^2-(\ell-1)^2
 \right],
\]
which is positive under \((\mathsf{SW}_4)\), a contradiction.
Finally \(D\cap H\) is a union of size-\(n\) orbits, so positivity of
\(a_0\) implies \(a_0\geq n\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the selected \(S_3\)-quotient of the Wendt pair]
\label{prop:cubic-wendt-s3}
For \(x\in D\), put
\[
 J(x)=\frac{(x^2+x+1)^3}{x^2(x+1)^2},
 \qquad a=\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathbb F_{q^3}/\mathbb F_q}(x).
\]
Then
\[
 J(x)=a^2+a+1.                                        \tag{\(\mathsf{SJ}_1\)}
\]
Let \(d=3^{k-1}\), let \(W_{k-1}\) be the degree-\(d\) minimal
polynomial of
\[
 z_{k-1}=\eta_{k-1}^2+\eta_{k-1}+1,
\]
and retain the selected polynomial \(P_{\eta,k}\).  Then
\[
\begin{split}
 P_{\eta,k}(X)P_{\eta,k}(X+1)
  ={}&[X(X+1)]^{2d}\\
    &{}\cdot
 W_{k-1}\!\left(
   \frac{(X^2+X+1)^3}{X^2(X+1)^2}
 \right).
\end{split}                                            \tag{\(\mathsf{SJ}_2\)}
\]
Thus quotienting by the free \(S_3\)-action lowers the selected
degree-\(2n\) pair to one degree-\(d\) trace factor.  Cubic failure is
still the assertion that this particular factor, rather than an
unselected Singer intersection with the same orbit structure, occurs
in the Wendt quotient.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The cubic for \(x\in D\) is
\[
 T^3+aT^2+(a+1)T+1.
\]
Substitution of \(x\) gives
\[
 a=\frac{x^3+x+1}{x(x+1)}.
\]
Putting this expression over a common denominator proves
\[
 a^2+a+1
 =\frac{(x^2+x+1)^3}{x^2(x+1)^2},
\]
which is \((\mathsf{SJ}_1)\).

The element \(\eta_{k-1}\) has odd degree \(d\), while it is at most
quadratic over \(z_{k-1}\).  Hence the two fields are equal and
\(W_{k-1}\) has degree \(d\).  The invariant \(J\) is constant on the
six-element orbit generated by \(X\mapsto X+1\) and
\(X\mapsto X^{-1}\).  On \(D\), its order-three subgroup is relative
Frobenius, while translation gives the reciprocal absolute orbit.
Consequently the roots of the two sides of
\((\mathsf{SJ}_2)\) are the same.  Both sides are monic of degree
\(6d=2n\), which proves the identity.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[reciprocal Conway selector and the exact order seam]
\label{cor:cubic-reciprocal-selector}
For \(j\geq1\), put
\[
 z_j=\eta_j^2+\eta_j+1,\qquad z_0=1.
\]
Then
\[
 z_j=\gamma_j^{-2}.                                   \tag{\(\mathsf{RS}_1\)}
\]
If \(q=2^{3^{j-1}}\), then \(z_j\) has the irreducible cubic
\[
 Z^3+z_{j-1}Z^2+z_{j-1}=0                             \tag{\(\mathsf{RS}_2\)}
\]
over \(\F_q\).  In particular
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{\F_{q^3}/\F_q}(z_j)
 =\Norm_{\F_{q^3}/\F_q}(z_j)=z_{j-1},\qquad
 z_{j-1}=\frac{z_j^3}{z_j^2+1}.                       \tag{\(\mathsf{RS}_3\)}
\]
If \(P_j\) is the minimal polynomial of \(\gamma_j\), the minimal
polynomial of \(z_j\) is its reciprocal
\[
 W_j(X)=X^{3^j}P_j(X^{-1}).                            \tag{\(\mathsf{RS}_4\)}
\]
Most importantly,
\[
 z_j^{q-1}=\eta_j^{-2},\qquad
 \ord(z_j)=\ord(z_{j-1})\,\ord(\eta_j).                \tag{\(\mathsf{RS}_5\)}
\]
Thus \(W_j\) is primitive exactly when \(P_j\) is.  For every current
prime \(\ell\mid q^2+q+1\),
\[
 z_j\in(\F_{q^3}^{\times})^\ell
 \iff \eta_j\in U_j^\ell.                             \tag{\(\mathsf{RS}_6\)}
\]
The selected \(S_3\)-quotient is therefore a lossless reciprocal
formulation of \(C_j\), not an independent induction from \(C_{j-1}\).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Put \(t=\zeta_j^2\) and
\(M(t)=(\omega+\omega^2t)/(1+t)=\eta_j\).  Direct calculation in
\(\F_4(t)\) gives
\[
 M(t)^2+M(t)+1=\frac{t}{(1+t)^2},
 \qquad
 \gamma_j^2=t+t^{-1}=\frac{(1+t)^2}{t},
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{RS}_1)\).

Write \(b=\eta_{j-1}\), \(c=b^2+b+1=z_{j-1}\), and let \(a_i\) be
the three relative conjugates of \(\eta_j\).  By
Proposition~\ref{prop:eta-tower},
\[
 e_1(a_i)=b,\qquad e_2(a_i)=b+1,\qquad e_3(a_i)=1.
\]
For \(w_i=a_i^2+a_i+1\), these identities give
\[
 e_1(w_i)=c,\qquad e_2(w_i)=0.
\]
Moreover, over \(\F_4\),
\[
 \prod_i w_i
 =\prod_i(a_i+\omega)\prod_i(a_i+\omega^2)
 =(b+\omega)(b+\omega^2)=c.
\]
This proves \((\mathsf{RS}_2)\).  Since \(\eta_j\) is at most
quadratic over \(z_j\), while its absolute degree \(3^j\) is odd, the
two elements generate the same field.  Thus the cubic is irreducible,
and its coefficients give \((\mathsf{RS}_3)\).

Squaring permutes the absolute conjugates of \(\gamma_j\), while
inversion sends them to the conjugates of \(z_j=\gamma_j^{-2}\).
The constant term of \(P_j\) is one, proving \((\mathsf{RS}_4)\).
The first equality in \((\mathsf{RS}_5)\) follows from
\(\eta_j=\gamma_j^{q-1}\).

Now
\[
 \F_{q^3}^{\times}\simeq
 \F_q^\times\times U_j,\qquad
 \gcd(q-1,q^2+q+1)=1.
\]
Relative norm detects the \(\F_q^\times\)-component of \(z_j\)
without changing its order, because it cubes that component and
\(3\nmid q-1\).  Raising to \(q-1\) detects the \(U_j\)-component
without changing its order.  Equations \((\mathsf{RS}_3)\) and
\((\mathsf{RS}_5)\), together with the oddness of \(|U_j|\), therefore
give the order product.  Finally \(\ell\)-powering is an automorphism
on \(\F_q^\times\), while both \(q-1\) and \(2\) are invertible modulo
\(\ell\); this proves \((\mathsf{RS}_6)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[fixed-norm iterated cubic fibre]
\label{prop:cubic-iterated-fibre}
Put
\[
 L_j=\F_{2^{3^j}},\qquad
 z_0=1,\qquad z_j=\eta_j^2+\eta_j+1=\gamma_j^{-2}.
\]
Fix \(k\geq1\) and a prime
\(\ell\mid\Phi_{3^k}(2)\).  For \(0\leq j<k\), let \(r_j\in L_j^\times\)
be the unique element such that
\[
 r_j^\ell=z_j.                                        \tag{\(\mathsf{IF}_0\)}
\]
Then \(r_0=1\), and the roots are norm coherent:
\[
 \Norm_{L_j/L_{j-1}}(r_j)=r_{j-1}\qquad(1\leq j<k).  \tag{\(\mathsf{IF}_1\)}
\]

For indeterminates \(C,D,E\), define generalized power sums by
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 T_0(C,D,E)&=1,&T_1(C,D,E)&=C,&T_2(C,D,E)&=C^2,\\
 T_n(C,D,E)&=CT_{n-1}+DT_{n-2}+ET_{n-3}&&&(n\geq3),
 \end{aligned}                                        \tag{\(\mathsf{IF}_2\)}
\]
and put
\[
 (A,B)=\Psi_{\ell,E}(C,D)
 =\bigl(T_\ell(C,D,E),T_\ell(D,EC,E^2)\bigr).
\]
If \(J=\det(\partial(A,B)/\partial(C,D))\), then in
\(\F_2[C,D,E]\),
\[
 \boxed{(CD+E)J=AB+E^\ell.}                          \tag{\(\mathsf{IF}_3\)}
\]
The same identity holds with \(\ell\) replaced throughout by every odd
integer \(n\), defining \(\Psi_{n,E}\) from the same recurrences.

Now specialize \(E\) to a nonzero field element.  For
\(1\leq j\leq k\), take \(K=L_{j-1}\), \(E=r_{j-1}\), and the
fixed-\(E\) selected fibre
\[
 T_\ell(C,D,E)=z_{j-1},\qquad
 T_\ell(D,EC,E^2)=0.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{IF}_4\)}
\]
It has exactly \(\ell^2\) reduced geometric points and is
\(\acute{e}\)tale.  Every \(K\)-rational point gives an irreducible
cubic \(X^3+CX^2+DX+E\).  For \(j<k\), the \(K\)-rational fibre is a
singleton, the minimal polynomial of \(r_j\).  At \(j=k\), its
cardinality is
\[
 \#\Psi_{\ell,r_{k-1}}^{-1}(z_{k-1},0)(K)
 =\begin{cases}
 0,&z_k\notin(L_k^\times)^\ell,\\
 \ell,&z_k\in(L_k^\times)^\ell.
 \end{cases}                                          \tag{\(\mathsf{IF}_5\)}
\]
In the failure case the \(\ell\) points correspond to the \(\ell\)
roots \(b^\ell=z_k\), and all of them have
\(\Norm_{L_k/K}(b)=r_{k-1}\).  Thus every bad top lift collapses to
the same complete selected lower norm ancestry.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put \(q=2^{3^{k-1}}\).  Since
\(\Phi_{3^k}(2)\equiv1\pmod3\), one has \(\ell\ne3\).
Also \(q^2+q+1\equiv0\pmod\ell\), while \(q\not\equiv1\pmod\ell\);
hence \(\ord_\ell(q)=3\) and \(\ord_\ell(2)=3^k\).  Therefore
\(\ell\nmid|L_j^\times|\) for \(j<k\), which proves existence and
uniqueness in \((\mathsf{IF}_0)\).  Taking norms and using
Corollary~\ref{cor:cubic-reciprocal-selector} gives
\[
 \Norm(r_j)^\ell=z_{j-1}=r_{j-1}^\ell.
\]
Powering by \(\ell\) is injective on \(L_{j-1}^\times\), proving
\((\mathsf{IF}_1)\).

For \((\mathsf{IF}_3)\), work first over \(\F_2(E)\), take
independent \(x,y\), and put \(z=E/(xy)\).  Then
\[
 C=x+y+z,\qquad D=xy+yz+zx,
\]
while \(A=x^\ell+y^\ell+z^\ell\).  The three pair-products
\(xy,yz,zx\) have elementary coefficients \(D,EC,E^2\), so the
second recurrence gives
\(B=(xy)^\ell+(yz)^\ell+(zx)^\ell\).  Logarithmic differentiation
gives
\[
 \det\frac{\partial(C,D)}{\partial(\log x,\log y)}=CD+E,
 \qquad
 \det\frac{\partial(A,B)}
          {\partial(\log x^\ell,\log y^\ell)}=AB+E^\ell.
\]
The power map has logarithmic determinant
\(\ell^2=1\) in characteristic two.  The chain rule proves
\((\mathsf{IF}_3)\) over \(\F_2(E)\), hence as a polynomial identity.

Label the three distinct roots \(y_1,y_2,y_3\) of the target cubic
\(X^3+z_{j-1}X^2+z_{j-1}\).  Choose \(\ell\)-th roots of
\(y_1,y_2\) independently; among the roots of \(y_3\), exactly one
makes the product equal to \(E\).  This gives \(\ell^2\) source
triples.  The target labels are recovered after powering, so distinct
choices do not coalesce on passing to coefficients.  On the selected
fibre, \((\mathsf{IF}_3)\) reads
\[
 (CD+E)J=E^\ell=z_{j-1}\ne0,
\]
which proves separability, reducedness, and \(\acute{e}\)taleness.

At a rational point, the powered source roots are the roots of the
irreducible selected target cubic.  A reducible source cubic over the
finite field \(K\) would have a \(K\)-root whose \(\ell\)-th power was
a \(K\)-root of that target, a contradiction.  Hence its roots lie in
the unique cubic extension \(L_j\), and the coefficient points are in
bijection with roots \(b^\ell=z_j\) having norm \(E\).  For \(j<k\),
powering is bijective on \(L_j^\times\), giving the singleton \(r_j\).
At the top there are no roots on success and exactly \(\ell\) on
failure.  Their norms all equal \(r_{k-1}\) by the same injectivity
argument in \(K^\times\), proving \((\mathsf{IF}_5)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the full selected trace-coefficient flag]
\label{prop:cubic-trace-fibre-flag}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:eta-tower}, fix \(k\geq1\), and let
\(\ell\mid\Phi_{3^k}(2)\) be prime.  For the generalized cubic power map
of Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-iterated-fibre}, define, for
\(1\leq j\leq k\),
\[
 \mathcal Y_j=\Psi_{\ell,1}^{-1}
   \bigl(\eta_{j-1},\eta_{j-1}+1\bigr)
 \subset\mathbb A^2_{L_{j-1}}.                       \tag{\(\mathsf{TA}_1\)}
\]
Thus \((C,D)\in\mathcal Y_j\) means that the \(\ell\)-th powers of
the roots of
\[
 X^3+CX^2+DX+1                                      \tag{\(\mathsf{TA}_2\)}
\]
have the selected reciprocal cubic
\[
 X^3+\eta_{j-1}X^2+(\eta_{j-1}+1)X+1.               \tag{\(\mathsf{TA}_3\)}
\]

Each \(\mathcal Y_j\) is finite reduced \'etale of geometric degree
\(\ell^2\), and every \(L_{j-1}\)-rational point gives an irreducible
reciprocal cubic.  If \(J\) is the Jacobian determinant of
\(\Psi_{\ell,1}\), then at every geometric point,
\[
 \boxed{
 (CD+1)J
 =\eta_{j-1}(\eta_{j-1}+1)+1
 =z_{j-1}\ne0.}                                      \tag{\(\mathsf{TA}_4\)}
\]
In particular the source cubic has nonzero discriminant
\[
 \operatorname{disc}(X^3+CX^2+DX+1)=(CD+1)^2.        \tag{\(\mathsf{TA}_5\)}
\]

For \(j<k\), let \(b_j\in L_j^\times\) be the unique element satisfying
\(b_j^\ell=\eta_j\).  Then
\[
 \Norm_{L_j/L_{j-1}}(b_j)=1,
 \qquad
 \#\mathcal Y_j(L_{j-1})=1\quad(1\leq j<k),          \tag{\(\mathsf{TA}_6\)}
\]
and the unique point is the coefficient pair of the minimal polynomial
of \(b_j\).  At the top,
\[
 \#\mathcal Y_k(L_{k-1})
 =
 \begin{cases}
 0,&\eta_k\notin(L_k^\times)^\ell,\\
 \ell,&\eta_k\in(L_k^\times)^\ell.
 \end{cases}                                         \tag{\(\mathsf{TA}_7\)}
\]
Consequently the complete edgewise flag
\(\mathcal Y_{\leq k}=\prod_{j=1}^k\mathcal Y_j(L_{j-1})\) has
\[
 \boxed{
 \#\mathcal Y_{\leq k}
 =
 \begin{cases}
 0,&\eta_k^{(2^{3^k}-1)/\ell}\ne1,\\
 \ell,&\eta_k^{(2^{3^k}-1)/\ell}=1.
 \end{cases}}                                        \tag{\(\mathsf{TA}_8\)}
\]
Thus the complete recursively selected trace ancestry, together with all
symmetric source coefficients, Jacobians, cubic discriminants, and their
ordinary lower-field norms, is compatible with failure.  Its sole
rationality obstruction is the original top Euler class.  This does not
exclude a nonsymmetric cross-edge construction using a chosen top root or a
mixed additive--multiplicative character correlation not determined by the
edge coefficient fibres.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let \(y_1,y_2,y_3\) be the roots of \((\mathsf{TA}_3)\).  Choose
\(\ell\)-th roots \(x_1,x_2\) independently and put
\(x_3=(x_1x_2)^{-1}\).  Since \(y_1y_2y_3=1\), one has
\(x_3^\ell=y_3\).  The target roots recover the labels after powering, so
this gives exactly \(\ell^2\) geometric coefficient points.

The universal identity
\[
 (CD+E)J=AB+E^\ell
\]
specializes at \(E=1\), \(A=\eta_{j-1}\), and
\(B=\eta_{j-1}+1\) to \((\mathsf{TA}_4)\).
Here \(z_{j-1}=\eta_{j-1}^2+\eta_{j-1}+1\ne0\).  Hence both
\(CD+1\) and \(J\) are nonzero, proving reducedness, \'etaleness, and
\((\mathsf{TA}_5)\).

A reducible source cubic over \(L_{j-1}\) would have a base-field root
whose \(\ell\)-th power was a root of the irreducible target cubic, an
impossibility.  For \(j<k\), one has
\(\ell\nmid|L_j^\times|\), so \(\ell\)-powering is bijective on
\(L_j^\times\).  The target roots therefore have one Frobenius orbit of
unique preimages, giving one coefficient pair.  Moreover
\[
 \Norm(b_j)^\ell=\Norm(\eta_j)=1,
\]
and injectivity of \(\ell\)-powering downstairs gives \(\Norm(b_j)=1\).

At \(j=k\), a rational point supplies an \(\ell\)-th root in \(L_k\)
of a conjugate of \(\eta_k\), hence one of \(\eta_k\) after Frobenius
conjugation.  Conversely, under failure the \(\ell\) roots of \(\eta_k\)
give \(\ell\) distinct coefficient pairs, as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-kummer-descent}.  This proves
\((\mathsf{TA}_7)\); all lower factors are singletons, so
\((\mathsf{TA}_8)\) follows.  Lean kernel-checks the selected Jacobian
numerator, the characteristic-two reciprocal-cubic discriminant, and the
norm-one uniqueness step.  The finite-field fibre count and \'etale
interpretation remain paper-level.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the selected cubic radicals span one Kummer-class line]
\label{prop:cubic-coupled-radicals}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:eta-tower}.  Fix \(k\geq1\), put
\[
 L=L_k=\F_{q^3},\qquad q=2^{3^{k-1}},\qquad N=q^2+q+1,
\]
and let \(\ell\mid N\) be prime.  Choose integers
\(1\leq r<\ell\) and \(s\geq0\) such that
\[
 r(q-1)=1+\ell s.                                    \tag{\(\mathsf{RK}_1\)}
\]
For a chosen root \(t^\ell=\eta_k\) in \(\overline L\), define
\[
 e=t^{q+1},\qquad b=t^{-r}\beta_k^{-s}.
\]
Then
\[
 \boxed{e^\ell=\varepsilon_k,\qquad b^\ell=\beta_k.} \tag{\(\mathsf{RK}_2\)}
\]
Moreover, for every \(\rho\in\mu_\ell\),
\[
 t\longmapsto\rho t,\qquad
 e\longmapsto\rho^{q+1}e,\qquad
 b\longmapsto\rho^{-r}b.                             \tag{\(\mathsf{RK}_3\)}
\]
All three weights \(1,q+1,-r\) are nonzero modulo \(\ell\).

Writing the Kummer quotient additively,
\[
 [\varepsilon_k]=(q+1)[\eta_k],\qquad
 [\beta_k]=-r[\eta_k]
 \quad\text{in }L^\times/(L^\times)^\ell.            \tag{\(\mathsf{RK}_4\)}
\]
Consequently every choice of the three radicals generates the same field:
\[
 L(\eta_k^{1/\ell})
 =L(\varepsilon_k^{1/\ell})
 =L(\beta_k^{1/\ell}).                               \tag{\(\mathsf{RK}_5\)}
\]
The same holds for \(\gamma_k=\beta_k^{-1}\).

For every \(1\leq j<k\), \(\ell\)-powering is bijective on
\(L_j^\times\).  Hence all lower selected
\(\eta_j,\varepsilon_j,\beta_j,\gamma_j\) have unique \(\ell\)-th
roots already in \(L_j\), and the full selected radical compositum is
\[
 \boxed{
 L_k\bigl(
   \eta_j^{1/\ell},\varepsilon_j^{1/\ell},
   \beta_j^{1/\ell},\gamma_j^{1/\ell}:1\leq j\leq k
 \bigr)
 =L_k(t).}                                           \tag{\(\mathsf{RK}_6\)}
\]
Its degree over \(L_k\) is \(\ell\) on \(C\)-success at this prime
\(\ell\), and one on \(C\)-failure at \(\ell\).

More intrinsically, the \(L\)-subalgebra generated by these chosen roots,
their Frobenius conjugates, and the full lower ancestry is the single Kummer
algebra
\[
 \mathcal A_{k,\ell}
 =L[T]/(T^\ell-\eta_k)
 =\bigoplus_{m=0}^{\ell-1}LT^m.                      \tag{\(\mathsf{RK}_7\)}
\]
The action \(T\mapsto\rho T\) preserves every selected coefficient and
every lower canonical root, and
\[
 \mathcal A_{k,\ell}^{\mu_\ell}=L.                   \tag{\(\mathsf{RK}_8\)}
\]
Thus the compatible chosen roots, their Frobenius conjugates, and all
cross-edge monomials supply no second radical torsor or canonical
orientation.  A remaining proof must establish a genuinely
nonzero-weight correlation uniformly on this one torsor, or evaluate a
weight-zero scalar in \(L\).  The proposition does not itself prove the
required nonvanishing and does not exclude external character-sum or
counting arguments.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(q\equiv-1\pmod3\),
\[
 \gcd(q-1,N)=\gcd(q-1,3)=1.
\]
Thus \(q\) has order three modulo \(\ell\);
in particular \(\mu_\ell\subset L\), and both \(q-1\) and \(q+1\)
are units modulo \(\ell\).  This gives \(r,s\) in
\((\mathsf{RK}_1)\).

The Singer and normalized-inverse identities give
\[
 \varepsilon_k=\eta_k^{q+1},\qquad
 \beta_k^{q-1}=\eta_k^{-1}.
\]
Therefore
\[
 e^\ell=(t^\ell)^{q+1}=\eta_k^{q+1}=\varepsilon_k,
\]
while
\[
 \eta_k^{-r}
 =\beta_k^{r(q-1)}
 =\beta_k^{1+\ell s},
\]
and hence
\[
 b^\ell
 =\eta_k^{-r}\beta_k^{-\ell s}
 =\beta_k.
\]
Replacing \(t\) by \(\rho t\) gives
\((\mathsf{RK}_3)\), and \((\mathsf{RK}_4)\) follows.  Since the
displayed weights are units modulo \(\ell\), B\'ezout recovers \(t\)
from either \(e\) or \(b\), proving \((\mathsf{RK}_5)\); arbitrary
choices differ from these compatible roots by elements of
\(\mu_\ell\subset L\).

Because \(q=2^{3^{k-1}}\) has order three modulo \(\ell\),
\(2\) has order \(3^k\) modulo \(\ell\).  Thus
\(\ell\nmid 2^{3^j}-1=|L_j^\times|\) for \(j<k\), proving the
lower-root assertion and \((\mathsf{RK}_6)\).  Standard prime-degree
Kummer theory gives its stated degree.

Finally, \(T\) is a unit in \(\mathcal A_{k,\ell}\), and every Laurent
monomial reduces uniquely by
\[
 T^{\ell a+m}=\eta_k^aT^m,\qquad0\leq m<\ell.
\]
In particular products of Frobenius conjugates reduce by
\[
 \prod_i(T^{2^i})^{w_i}
 =\eta_k^aT^m
 \quad\text{when}\quad
 \sum_iw_i2^i=\ell a+m.
\]
All lower selected roots are coefficients in \(L\), while \(e\) and
\(b\) have the weights in \((\mathsf{RK}_3)\).  Since
\(\mu_\ell\subset L\), the distinct characters of \(\mu_\ell\) on
the summands \(LT^m\) prove \((\mathsf{RK}_8)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[every nonzero cubic weight carries the Euler monodromy]
\label{prop:cubic-current-weight-collapse}
Retain Propositions~\ref{prop:cubic-coupled-radicals} and
\ref{prop:cubic-normalized-singer}.  Put
\[
 L=L_k=\F_{q^3},\qquad N=q^2+q+1=\ell u,\qquad
 \tau=\tau_k,\qquad \Omega=\tau^u,
\]
and choose \(v\in\overline L\) with \(v^\ell=\tau\).  Then
\[
 \boxed{v^{q^3}=\Omega^{q-1}v.}                       \tag{\(\mathsf{CWG}_1\)}
\]
For \(a\in L^\times\) and \(0<m<\ell\), the evaluated pure weight
\(F=a v^m\) satisfies
\[
 \boxed{F^{q^3}=\Omega^{m(q-1)}F.}                    \tag{\(\mathsf{CWG}_2\)}
\]
Since \(\ord_\ell(q)=3\), both \(m\) and \(q-1\) are units modulo
\(\ell\), and therefore
\[
 \boxed{
 F\in L
 \iff \Omega=1
 \iff C\text{-failure at }\ell.}                    \tag{\(\mathsf{CWG}_3\)}
\]
The nonvanishing of \(F\) itself is automatic.  Equivalently, on the
radical torsor a nonzero homogeneous regular function \(aV^m\) vanishes
nowhere.

The universal Kummer algebra is
\[
 \mathcal B=L[V]/(V^\ell-\tau)
 =\bigoplus_{m\bmod\ell}LV^m.
\]
For \(P(X)\in L[X]\) with \(\deg P<\ell\), separability gives the exact
orientation-free branch product
\[
 \boxed{
 \operatorname{Res}_X(X^\ell-\tau,P(X))
 =\prod_{\rho\in\mu_\ell}P(\rho v).}                 \tag{\(\mathsf{CWG}_4\)}
\]
It is independent of the chosen root \(v\), and is nonzero exactly when
\(P\) vanishes on no root branch.  In particular, for \(a,b\in L\) and
\(0<m<\ell\),
\[
 \boxed{
 \operatorname{Res}_X(X^\ell-\tau,a+bX^m)
 =a^\ell+b^\ell\tau^m.}                             \tag{\(\mathsf{CWG}_5\)}
\]

The coefficients may be any everywhere-defined ancestry expressions in
\(L\).  Pure nonzero weights therefore provide only an invertible power of
the original Euler bit, while root-choice-invariant regular functions reduce
to weight-zero arithmetic; the orbit product of one mixed evaluation is the
resultant above and forgets which branch could vanish.  This does not evaluate
a special selected weight-zero resultant and therefore does not prove \(C_k\).
External character sums, inequalities, and direct weight-zero evaluations
remain outside the conclusion.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(q^3-1=(q-1)N=\ell(q-1)u\),
\[
 v^{q^3}
 =v^{1+\ell(q-1)u}
 =v\,\tau^{(q-1)u}
 =\Omega^{q-1}v,
\]
proving \((\mathsf{CWG}_1)\); coefficients in \(L\) are fixed by
\(q^3\)-powering, so \((\mathsf{CWG}_2)\) follows.  If \(F\in L\), then
\((\mathsf{CWG}_2)\) and \(F\ne0\) give
\(\Omega^{m(q-1)}=1\).  The exponent is a unit modulo \(\ell\), while
\(\Omega\in\mu_\ell\), so \(\Omega=1\).  Conversely
\((\mathsf{CWG}_1)\) shows that \(\Omega=1\) makes \(v\) fixed by the
\(q^3\)-power Frobenius, hence \(v\in L\).  This proves
\((\mathsf{CWG}_3)\).

The power basis gives the weight decomposition of \(\mathcal B\).  Its
geometric roots are the distinct \(\rho v\), and the first polynomial in
the resultant is monic; the product formula for the resultant proves
\((\mathsf{CWG}_4)\).  Finally \(\rho\mapsto\rho^m\) permutes
\(\mu_\ell\), and characteristic two gives
\[
 \prod_{\rho\in\mu_\ell}(a+b\rho^mv^m)
 =a^\ell+b^\ell v^{m\ell}
 =a^\ell+b^\ell\tau^m,
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{CWG}_5)\).

Lean kernel-checks the full-exponent factorization and both monodromy
identities.  The finite-field fixed-point and Kummer/Euler criteria,
together with separability, the resultant formula, and the invariant-ring
interpretation, remain paper-level.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the top cubic fibre is one Kummer coordinate]
\label{prop:cubic-torus-torsor}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-iterated-fibre}, and put
\[
 K=L_{k-1}=\F_q,\qquad L=L_k=\F_{q^3},\qquad
 N=q^2+q+1,\qquad T=R^1_{L/K}\mathbb G_m.
\]
Let \(r=r_{k-1}\), let \(c\in K^\times\) be the unique cube root of
\(r\), and set
\[
 \alpha=\frac{z_k}{c^\ell}\in T(K).                  \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_1\)}
\]
Then
\[
 z_k\in(L^\times)^\ell
 \iff \alpha\in\ell T(K).                           \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_2\)}
\]
The fixed-norm coefficient fibre is naturally isomorphic to the finite
\(\acute{e}\)tale torsor
\[
 X=[\ell]^{-1}(\alpha)
\]
under \(T[\ell]\), of degree \(\ell^2\).  Its class
\[
 \kappa=\delta(\alpha)
 \in H^1(K,T[\ell])
 \simeq T(K)/\ell T(K)\simeq\F_\ell                 \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_3\)}
\]
vanishes exactly on failure; the desired \(C\)-conclusion is
\(\kappa\ne0\).

There is an exact Euler representative containing the full selected
lower ancestry:
\[
 \boxed{\Omega=\alpha^{N/\ell}
 =\frac{z_k^{N/\ell}}{r}\in T[\ell](K),}
 \qquad
 \kappa=0\iff\Omega=1.                               \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_4\)}
\]
Moreover
\[
 \Omega^{q-1}=\eta_k^{-2N/\ell},                     \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_5\)}
\]
so this is an invertible power of the original selected obstruction,
not a new lower invariant.

More precisely, identify
\[
 A=T[\ell](\overline K)
 \simeq\{(u_0,u_1,u_2)\in\F_\ell^3:
                u_0+u_1+u_2=0\}.
\]
Arithmetic \(q\)-Frobenius is \(F=qP\), with \(P\) a three-cycle; its
eigenvalues on \(A\) are \(1,q^2\).  Choosing a geometric origin makes
Frobenius on \(X\) the affine map \(x\mapsto Fx+v\), where the class of
\(v\) on the fixed line is \(\kappa\).  For every \(s\geq1\),
\[
 \#X(\F_{q^s})=
 \begin{cases}
 \ell^{1+\mathbf1_{3\mid s}},&\kappa=0,\\
 0,&\kappa\ne0\text{ and }\ell\nmid s,\\
 \ell^{1+\mathbf1_{3\mid s}},&\kappa\ne0\text{ and }\ell\mid s.
 \end{cases}                                          \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_6\)}
\]
Equivalently, the Frobenius cycle types and zeta functions are
\[
 \begin{array}{c|c|c}
 &\text{cycles}&Z(X,t)\\ \hline
 \kappa=0&
 \ell[1]+\dfrac{\ell^2-\ell}{3}[3]&
 (1-t)^{-\ell}(1-t^3)^{-(\ell^2-\ell)/3}\\[2mm]
 \kappa\ne0&
 [\ell]+\dfrac{\ell-1}{3}[3\ell]&
 (1-t^\ell)^{-1}(1-t^{3\ell})^{-(\ell-1)/3}.
 \end{array}                                          \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_7\)}
\]
Thus the entire fibre geometry differs only by translation along one
fixed line.  The torus \(T\) has no nontrivial \(K\)-rational
character.  More precisely, let \(B\) be a finite-dimensional
\(\F_\ell\)-representation of \(F\), let \(f:A\to B\) be an
\(F\)-equivariant linear homomorphism, and let \(v\in A^F\) represent
\(\kappa\).  The induced quotient map sends
\[
 \kappa\longmapsto[f(v)]\in B/(F-1)B.
\]
It kills \(\kappa\) exactly when \(f(v)\in(F-1)B\), in particular if
\(f\) kills \(A^F\).  When \(B=B^F\oplus(F-1)B\) is semisimple,
nonvanishing of \(f(v)\) is sufficient to retain only an image of the
same unresolved coordinate.  Thus scalar characters and the ordinary
norm/determinant routes are blind; a transfer to another anisotropic
torus must be checked through its particular module map.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Since \(3\nmid q-1\), the cube root \(c\) is unique.  The norm of
\(z_k\) is \(z_{k-1}=r^\ell=c^{3\ell}\), proving
\(\alpha\in T(K)\).  If \(b^\ell=z_k\), norm coherence gives
\(\Norm(b)=r=c^3\), so \(b/c\in T(K)\); the converse is immediate.
This proves \((\mathsf{KT}_2)\).  To identify \(X\) with the coefficient
fibre, let \(\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\sigma_2\) be the three embeddings of
\(L/K\).  Over \(\overline K\), a point
\(t=(t_0,t_1,t_2)\in X\) satisfies
\[
 t_i^\ell=\sigma_i(\alpha),\qquad t_0t_1t_2=1.
\]
Send it to
\[
 (C,D)=\left(
   c\sum_i t_i,\,
   c^2\sum_{i<j}t_it_j
 \right).                                             \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_8\)}
\]
The three source roots are \(ct_i\); their \(\ell\)-th powers are the
three distinct conjugates of \(z_k\), and their product is
\(c^3=r\).  Thus \((\mathsf{KT}_8)\) lands in the selected fixed-norm
coefficient fibre.  Conversely those distinct powered targets recover
the labeled \(t_i\), so the map is a bijection on geometric points.
It is \(K\)-defined, and both sides are finite \(\acute{e}\)tale;
hence it is an isomorphism.  The Kummer sequence, together with Lang's
theorem \(H^1(K,T)=0\), gives
\((\mathsf{KT}_3)\).

Modulo \(q-1\), one has \(N\equiv3\), hence \(c^N=c^3=r\).
The cyclic Euler criterion in the group \(T(K)\), of order \(N\),
now proves \((\mathsf{KT}_4)\).  Equation
\((\mathsf{KT}_5)\) follows from
\(z_k^{q-1}=\eta_k^{-2}\), since the base scalar \(c\) is killed by
raising to \(q-1\).

On the augmentation plane \(A\), the three-cycle has eigenvalues
\(q,q^2\), because \(q\) has order three modulo \(\ell\); therefore
\(qP\) has eigenvalues \(1,q^2\).  The cohomology quotient
\(A/(F-1)A\) is exactly its fixed line.  If \(v\in A^F\) represents
\(\kappa\), then over \(\F_{q^s}\) the exact restriction formula is
\[
 \operatorname{res}_{\F_{q^s}/\F_q}(\kappa)
 =[(1+F+\cdots+F^{s-1})v]=[sv]
 \in A/(F^s-1)A.                                     \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_9\)}
\]
Moreover,
\[
 |A^{F^s}|=\ell^{1+\mathbf1_{3\mid s}}.
\]
This proves \((\mathsf{KT}_6)\), and orbit enumeration gives
\((\mathsf{KT}_7)\).  Finally the character lattice of the norm-one
cubic torus is the \(C_3\)-augmentation lattice, whose invariant
subgroup is zero.  Functoriality of the quotient
\(H^1=A/(F-1)A\) sends \([v]\) to \([f(v)]\); the final assertions
follow, with the stated semisimple direct-sum condition when it is
invoked.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[one normalized Singer element carries every current class]
\label{prop:cubic-normalized-singer}
For \(k\geq1\), put
\[
 q=2^{3^{k-1}},\qquad K=L_{k-1}=\F_q,\qquad
 L=L_k=\F_{q^3},\qquad N=q^2+q+1.
\]
Let \(d_k\in K^\times\) be the unique cube root of \(z_{k-1}\), and
define
\[
 d_k^3=z_{k-1},\qquad s_k=d_k^2,\qquad
 \tau_k=\frac{z_k}{d_k}.                             \tag{\(\mathsf{NS}_1\)}
\]
Then \(\tau_k\in R^1_{L/K}\mathbb G_m(K)\), and its irreducible
cubic over \(K\) is
\[
 \boxed{X^3+s_kX^2+1.}                               \tag{\(\mathsf{NS}_2\)}
\]
In particular
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{L/K}(\tau_k)=s_k,\qquad
 \operatorname{Tr}_{L/K}(\tau_k^{-1})=0,\qquad
 \Norm_{L/K}(\tau_k)=1.                             \tag{\(\mathsf{NS}_3\)}
\]
Moreover
\[
 \tau_k^{q-1}=\eta_k^{-2},\qquad
 \ord(\tau_k)=\ord(\eta_k).                         \tag{\(\mathsf{NS}_4\)}
\]

The coefficients retain the complete selected ancestry.  For \(k\geq2\),
\[
 \Norm(d_k)=d_{k-1},\qquad
 \Norm(s_k)=s_{k-1},\qquad
 \boxed{s_k^3=s_{k-1}\tau_{k-1}^2.}                 \tag{\(\mathsf{NS}_5\)}
\]
Also \(\F_2(s_k)=L_{k-1}\); conditional on \(C_{k-1}\), the
coefficient \(s_k\) is primitive in \(K^\times\).

Conversely, the normalized recursion is Frobenius-rigid.  Starting from
\(s_1=1\), every finite sequence in the prescribed fields satisfying
\((\mathsf{NS}_2)\) and the boxed recursion in
\((\mathsf{NS}_5)\) is a compatible absolute-Frobenius conjugate of the
selected finite sequence.  An infinite compatible sequence is similarly a
conjugate under the procyclic group
\(\operatorname{Gal}(\bigcup_jL_j/\F_2)\simeq\mathbb Z_3\).  Thus retaining
the complete recursion does not replace the primitivity problem: it
reconstructs the same selected elements up to an order-preserving Galois
action.

Now retain a current prime \(\ell\mid N\), the lower root
\(r=r_{k-1}\) with \(r^\ell=z_{k-1}\), and the cube root
\(c^3=r\) from Proposition~\ref{prop:cubic-torus-torsor}.  Then
\[
 c^\ell=d_k,
 \qquad
 \boxed{\alpha_{k,\ell}=\tau_k,\qquad
 \Omega_{k,\ell}=\tau_k^{N/\ell}.}                  \tag{\(\mathsf{NS}_6\)}
\]
Thus the current torsors are the preimages
\([\ell]^{-1}(\tau_k)\) of one selected norm-one element, while
\(\Omega_{k,\ell}\in T[\ell](K)\) is their mod-\(\ell\) Euler
obstruction.  At this level,
\[
 C_k\iff C_{k-1}\ \hbox{and}\ \ord(\tau_k)=N.
\]
Consequently the universal cubic arm is the conjunction of these maximal-order
statements over all levels, not a separate prime-dependent family of torsors.

There is a corresponding selected critical-value criterion.  For odd
\(u\), use the generalized sums of \((\mathsf{IF}_2)\) and put
\[
 A_u(S)=T_u(S,0,1),\qquad
 B_u(S)=T_u(0,S,1),\qquad
 \Delta_u(S)=A_u(S)B_u(S)+1.                         \tag{\(\mathsf{NS}_7\)}
\]
If \(u=N/\ell\), then
\[
 \boxed{
 \Omega_{k,\ell}=1
 \iff \Delta_u(s_k)=0,}                             \tag{\(\mathsf{NS}_8\)}
\]
and more explicitly
\[
 \boxed{
 \Delta_u(s_k)
 =\Norm_{L/K}\!\left(1+\eta_k^{-2u}\right).}        \tag{\(\mathsf{NS}_9\)}
\]
If \(S_k\) is the minimal polynomial of \(s_k\) over \(\F_2\),
the desired current-\(\ell\) conclusion is therefore
\[
 \operatorname{Res}_X\!\left(S_k(X),\Delta_u(X)\right)=1.
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{NS}_{10}\)}
\]
