 0<2^x-3\leq2^{n+3}-3=8M-3<12M+1\leq p,
\]
again impossible.  This proves the exclusion.  The strict-factor part of
\((\mathsf{QJ}_{11})\) proves \((\mathsf{QJ}_{13})\).

For the recovered jet, \((\mathsf{QJ}_{5})\) and repeated doubling give
the polynomial identity
\[
 S_d^{[2R]}=T_sX^R S_t^{4R}=T_sX^R S_h^W.             \tag{*}
\]
Lucas's theorem gives
\[
 \binom{2R+W}{2R}
 \equiv \binom{2+2^{u+2}}2\equiv1\pmod2.
\]
Apply the order-\(W\) Hasse derivative to \((*)\).  The Hasse composition
rule and the displayed parity identify its left side with
\(S_d^{[2R+W]}\).  Since \(S_h^W\) is a \(W\)-th Frobenius power, the
Hasse--Frobenius and product rules, evaluated at \(a\) with \(S_h(a)=0\),
leave exactly
\[
 T_s(a)a^R\bigl(S_h'(a)\bigr)^W.
\]
The vanishing hypothesis and \((\mathsf{QJ}_{8})\) give
\(\delta_n\mid t\).  Since \(\delta_n\) is odd, \(\delta_n\mid h\).
Writing \(h=2m+1\), differentiation of
\(S_h=S_{m+1}^2+XS_m^2\) gives \(S_h'=S_m^2\).  If this vanished at
\(a\), the Fibonacci zero-set criterion would also give
\(\delta_n\mid m\); then \(\delta_n\mid h-2m=1\), impossible because
\(\delta_n>1\).  The other factors are nonzero by the proof of
\((\mathsf{QJ}_{6})\), proving \((\mathsf{QJ}_{14})\).

If \(t=0\), then \(g=3\) and \(d=1+3R=B\).  Trailing-zero compression
with \(S_3=1+X\) and \(S_4=1\) gives
\(S_d=1+T_s(1+X^R)\), whose degree is \(3R/2\).  This proves
\((\mathsf{QJ}_{15})\).

Lean kernel-checks the strengthened short-factor window, the upper-end size
contradiction, the odd-index divisibility contradiction, the Lucas parity and
Hasse-composition coefficient, and nonvanishing of the recovered product.
The selected Fibonacci identities and their Hasse specialization remain
paper-level, as in Propositions~\ref{prop:fermat-fixed-adic-jet-blocks}
and~\ref{prop:fermat-odd-jet-collision}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[a failed Fermat factor has a lower semiconjugacy flag]
\label{prop:fermat-semiconjugacy-flag}
Retain the notation of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-odd-jet-recovery}.  Thus
\[
 d=1+Rg,\qquad R=2^s,\qquad g\ \hbox{odd},
\]
and assume the hypothetical failure \(A_{n-1}\mid S_d\).  Put
\[
 v=v_2(s),\qquad \rho=n-v,\qquad e=(g-1)/2,
\]
and let \(\mathcal N\) be the quadratic resultant operator of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-norm-descent}.  Then
\[
 \boxed{
 \Norm_{E_{n-1}/E_{v-1}}\!\bigl(S_g(a_{n-1})\bigr)
 =\Norm_{E_{n-1}/E_{v-1}}(a_{n-1})^e.}
                                                        \tag{\(\mathsf{QJ}_{16}\)}
\]
Equivalently, the actual lower selected polynomial obeys
\[
 \boxed{
 A_{v-1}\mid
 \mathcal F_{n,\ell}(X):=
 \mathcal N^{\circ\rho}(S_g)(X)
       +X^{3^\rho e}.}
                                                        \tag{\(\mathsf{QJ}_{17}\)}
\]
Thus a single nonzero remainder
\(\mathcal F_{n,\ell}\bmod A_{v-1}\) excludes the original failure;
the test lives at degree \(2^v\), not at the top degree \(2^n\).

The odd-jet collision has a factor-independent specialization.  Define
\[
 P_0=X+1,\qquad P_{j+1}=\mathcal N(P_j),\qquad
 D_j=P_j+X^{3^j}.
\]
If \(S_d^{[2R]}(a_{n-1})=0\), then
\[
 \boxed{A_{v-1}\mid D_\rho.}
                                                        \tag{\(\mathsf{QJ}_{18}\)}
\]
Here \(P_j\) is monic of degree \(3^j\) with constant coefficient one.
Consequently \(D_j\ne0\), \(\deg D_j<3^j\), and
\[
 \boxed{
 3^{n-v}\leq2^v
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad S_d^{[2R]}(a_{n-1})\ne0.}
                                                        \tag{\(\mathsf{QJ}_{19}\)}
\]
The implication in \((\mathsf{QJ}_{19})\) is an unconditional exclusion
of the surviving odd collision, under the standing hypothetical-failure
context.  It is not a proof that \(A_{n-1}\nmid S_d\): a nonzero Hasse jet
is compatible with divisibility.  If \(v=0\), then
\(E_{v-1}=E_{-1}=\F_2\) and \(A_{v-1}=A_{-1}=X+1\);
both nonzero relative norms are one, so
\((\mathsf{QJ}_{18})\) is automatic and carries no new information.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write
\[
 w=w_n=\frac{c_n+1}{c_n},\qquad
 \phi(z)=\frac{z}{(z+1)^2},
\]
so that \(\ord(w)=\delta_n\) and \(a_{n-1}=\phi(w)\).
For every odd \(m\), the Binet formula gives
\[
 S_m(\phi(z))=\frac{1+z^m}{(1+z)^m},\qquad
 \phi(z^m)=\frac{\phi(z)^m}{S_m(\phi(z))^2}.          \tag{*}
\]
Failure gives \(\delta_n\mid d\), hence
\(Rg\equiv-1\pmod{\delta_n}\).  Let \(L=2^\rho\) and
\[
 x_j=a_{n-1}^{R^j}=\phi(w^{R^j})
 \qquad(j\in\mathbf Z/L\mathbf Z).
\]
Because \(s=2^v s_0\) with \(s_0\) odd, Frobenius \(z\mapsto z^R\)
has order \(L\) on \(E_{n-1}/E_{v-1}\); the \(x_j\) are exactly the
relative conjugates.  Since \(\gcd(R,\delta_n)=1\) and \(\delta_n>1\),
the denominators below are nonzero.  Moreover \(\phi(z^{-1})=\phi(z)\),
so \(Rg\equiv-1\pmod{\delta_n}\) gives
\[
 \phi\bigl((w^{R^{j+1}})^g\bigr)=\phi(w^{-R^j})=x_j.
\]
Applying \((*)\) to \(z=w^{R^{j+1}}\) gives
\[
 x_jS_g(x_{j+1})^2=x_{j+1}^g.
\]
Multiplication around the cycle yields
\[
 \Norm(a_{n-1})\Norm(S_g(a_{n-1}))^2
   =\Norm(a_{n-1})^g,
\]
where all three norms are from \(E_{n-1}\) to \(E_{v-1}\).
The first norm is nonzero.  Cancelling it and using injectivity of the
square map in a finite field proves \((\mathsf{QJ}_{16})\).

Propositions~\ref{prop:fermat-norm-descent} and
\ref{prop:fermat-ancestry-exhaustion} give
\[
 \mathcal N^{\circ\rho}(S_g)(a_{v-1})
 =\Norm(S_g(a_{n-1})),\qquad
 \Norm(a_{n-1})=a_{v-1}^{3^\rho},
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{QJ}_{17})\).

If the odd order-\(2R\) jet vanishes, \((\mathsf{QJ}_8)\) gives
\(\delta_n\mid3R+1\).  Repeating the cyclic argument with \(m=3\),
rather than \(m=g\), uses \(3R\equiv-1\pmod{\delta_n}\) and
\(S_3=X+1\); it gives
\[
 \Norm(a_{n-1}+1)=\Norm(a_{n-1}).
\]
The same selected norm descent is exactly
\(P_\rho(a_{v-1})=a_{v-1}^{3^\rho}\), proving
\((\mathsf{QJ}_{18})\).

Finally, if \(P\) is monic of degree \(h\) and \(P(0)=1\), then
\(\mathcal N(P)\) is monic of degree \(3h\), while
\(\mathcal N(P)(0)=P(0)^2=1\).  For the degree assertion, if
\(y,y'\) are the roots of
\(Y^2+XY+X^3\), then \(yy'=X^3\).  The leading product
\((yy')^h=X^{3h}\) is unique: every paired cross-term
\(y^iy'^j+y^jy'^i\), with \(i\leq h\), \(j<h\), has degree at most
\(2i+j\leq3h-1\).  At \(X=0\), the resultant is
\(\operatorname{Res}_Y(Y^2,P(Y))=P(0)^2\).
Induction gives the assertions about \(P_j,D_j\).  If
\(3^\rho\leq2^v=\deg A_{v-1}\), the nonzero polynomial \(D_\rho\)
has degree strictly below \(A_{v-1}\), contradicting
\((\mathsf{QJ}_{18})\).  This proves \((\mathsf{QJ}_{19})\).

Lean kernel-checks the denominator-cleared cubic identity, the general
cyclic-product reindexing, cancellation to the exact half power in
characteristic two, the selected quadratic-edge norm formula, and the
final degree-divisibility exclusion.  The Binet specialization,
finite-field orbit identification, and resultant degree bookkeeping
remain paper-level.
\end{proof}

The fast \texttt{--jet-only} mode of
\path{experiments/fermat_selected_screen.py} evaluates
\(\Gamma_{n,\ell}\) and the branch-appropriate gcd without constructing the
selected field.  Both are one on every published prime-factor row in the
pinned \(F_{12},\ldots,F_{18}\) table, so the first uncollapsed Hasse jet is
nonzero for all those coordinates even under hypothetical failure.  This is
an exact integer screen of a higher jet, not a replacement for the selected
residue computation and not an all-level proof.

\begin{proposition}[Dickson all-or-nothing factorization]
\label{prop:fermat-dickson-dichotomy}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-root-ancestry}.  Thus \(n\geq2\),
\[
 K=E_{n-1}=\mathbb F_q,\qquad L=E_n=\mathbb F_{q^2},
 \qquad a=a_{n-1},\qquad b=r_{n-1},\qquad b^\ell=a,
\]
where the prime \(\ell\) divides \(F_n=q+1\).  Put \(c=c_n\), so
that
\[
 c^2+c=a,\qquad c^q=c+1,
\]
and define
\[
 f_{\ell,n}(X)=\mathcal D_\ell(X,b)+1\in K[X].
\]
Then \(f_{\ell,n}\) is separable of degree \(\ell\), and exactly one
of the following occurs:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item \(c\in L^{\times\ell}\), in which case \(f_{\ell,n}\) splits
      into \(\ell\) distinct linear factors over \(K\);
\item \(c\notin L^{\times\ell}\), in which case \(f_{\ell,n}\) is
      irreducible over \(K\).
\end{enumerate}
Consequently the Dickson equation in \((\mathsf{RD})\) never has an
intermediate number of roots: it has either \(\ell\) roots in \(K\)
or none.

More explicitly, choose \(t\in\overline K\) with \(t^\ell=c\), and put
\[
 \vartheta=\frac{t^{q+1}}b\in\mu_\ell.
\]
This element is independent of the chosen \(\ell\)-th root, while the
standard finite-field Kummer symbol is
\[
 c^{(q^2-1)/\ell}=\vartheta^{-2}.
\]
With the convention
\(\operatorname{Res}(f,g)=\prod_{f(s)=0}g(s)\), one has
\[
 \operatorname{Res}_X
 \bigl(f_{\ell,n}(X),X^q-X\bigr)
 =(1+\vartheta)^\ell.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{DF}\)}
\]
Thus the resultant vanishes precisely in the Conway--Fermat failure
case.

Finally, if \(A_{n-1}\) is the minimal polynomial of \(a_{n-1}\) over
\(\mathbb F_2\), then
\[
 \mathcal C_n(Y):=A_{n-1}(Y^2+Y)
\]
is the degree-\(2^{n+1}\) minimal polynomial of \(c_n\).  The absolute
composition
\[
 \mathcal C_n(X^\ell)
 =A_{n-1}(X^{2\ell}+X^\ell)                            \tag{\(\mathsf{AC}\)}
\]
is irreducible of degree \(2^{n+1}\ell\) in case~\textup{(ii)}, while
in case~\textup{(i)} it is the product of exactly \(\ell\) distinct
irreducible polynomials, each of degree \(2^{n+1}\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Because \(\ell\mid q+1\), one has \(\mu_\ell\subset L\) and
\(q\equiv-1\pmod\ell\).  Fix a primitive \(\ell\)-th root
\(\zeta\), choose \(t^\ell=c\), and put \(\widetilde t=b/t\).  Since
\[
 \widetilde t^\ell=\frac{b^\ell}{c}
 =\frac ac=c+1,
\]
the \(\ell\) elements, indexed by \(\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z\),
\[
 s_i=t\zeta^i+\widetilde t\zeta^{-i}
\]
satisfy
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(s_i,b)
 =(t\zeta^i)^\ell+(\widetilde t\zeta^{-i})^\ell
 =c+(c+1)=1.
\]
They are distinct.  Indeed, equality \(s_i=s_j\) identifies the
unordered root pairs of the corresponding polynomials
\(X^2+s_iX+b\).  Besides \(i=j\), the only possible pairing would
identify \(t\zeta^i\) with \(\widetilde t\zeta^{-j}\); taking
\(\ell\)-th powers would give \(c=c+1\).  Hence the \(s_i\) are all
the roots of the monic degree-\(\ell\) polynomial \(f_{\ell,n}\).

There is a unique \(k\in\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z\) such that
\[
 t^q=\widetilde t\zeta^k.
\]
Since \(\zeta^q=\zeta^{-1}\), direct substitution gives
\[
 s_i^q=s_{i-k},                                        \tag{\(\mathsf{DF}_1\)}
\]
and applying \(q\)-Frobenius once more gives
\[
 t^{q^2}=t\zeta^{-2k}.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{DF}_2\)}
\]
As \(\ell\) is odd, \(k=0\) if and only if \(t\in L\).  Because all
\(\ell\)-th roots of \(c\) differ by an element of
\(\mu_\ell\subset L\), this is equivalent to
\(c\in L^{\times\ell}\).  If \(k=0\), every \(s_i\) is fixed by
\(q\)-Frobenius.  If \(k\ne0\), translation by \(-k\) is one
\(\ell\)-cycle because \(\ell\) is prime.  Equation
\((\mathsf{DF}_1)\) therefore proves the split/irreducible dichotomy.

The quotient \(t^{q+1}/b\) is \(\zeta^k\), and replacing \(t\) by
another \(\ell\)-th root does not change it because
\(\ell\mid q+1\).  Equation \((\mathsf{DF}_2)\) yields
\[
 c^{(q^2-1)/\ell}=t^{q^2-1}=\vartheta^{-2}.
\]
For the resultant, characteristic two and \((\mathsf{DF}_1)\) give
\[
 \operatorname{Res}(f_{\ell,n},X^q-X)
 =\prod_i(s_{i-k}+s_i).
\]
Moreover
\[
 s_{i-k}+s_i
 =(1+\vartheta)
 \left(\vartheta^{-1}t\zeta^i+\widetilde t\zeta^{-i}\right).
\]
After pulling out the factors \(\zeta^{-i}\), whose product is one,
the fact that multiplication by two permutes
\(\mathbb Z/\ell\mathbb Z\) gives
\[
 \prod_i
 \left(\vartheta^{-1}t\zeta^i+\widetilde t\zeta^{-i}\right)
 = (\vartheta^{-1}t)^\ell+\widetilde t^\ell
 =c+(c+1)=1.
\]
This proves \((\mathsf{DF})\).

The element \(a\) has absolute trace one, so \(Y^2+Y+a\) is
irreducible over \(K\).  Since \(a=c^2+c\) and
\([K:\mathbb F_2]=2^n\), the element \(c\) has degree \(2^{n+1}\)
over \(\mathbb F_2\); hence \(\mathcal C_n\) is its minimal
polynomial.  Capelli's lemma now says that
\(\mathcal C_n(X^\ell)\) is irreducible over \(\mathbb F_2\) if and
only if \(X^\ell-c\) is irreducible over \(L\).  Since
\(\mu_\ell\subset L\) and \(\ell\) is prime, this holds exactly when
\(c\notin L^{\times\ell}\).

In the other case every root \(x\) of \(\mathcal C_n(X^\ell)\) lies
in \(L\), because its \(\ell\)-th power is a Frobenius conjugate of
\(c\) and all its \(\ell\)-th roots lie in \(L\).  That conjugate has
degree \(2^{n+1}\), so
\[
 2^{n+1}\leq[\mathbb F_2(x):\mathbb F_2]\leq[L:\mathbb F_2]
 =2^{n+1}.
\]
Separability then partitions the \(2^{n+1}\ell\) roots into exactly
\(\ell\) distinct Frobenius orbits of length \(2^{n+1}\), proving
the final assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[reverse-Dickson trace divisor]
\label{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-dickson-dichotomy}, and put
\[
 m=2^n,\qquad q=2^m,\qquad K=\F_q.
\]
Let $A_{n-1}\in\F_2[T]$ be the degree-$m$ minimal polynomial of
$a=a_{n-1}$, and define
\[
 \begin{split}
 R_\ell(Z)&=Z^\ell\mathcal D_\ell(Z^{-1},1)
            =\mathcal D_\ell(1,Z)^2,\\
 P_{\ell,a}(Z)&=Z^\ell+aR_\ell(Z),\\
 H_m(Z)&=1+\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}Z^{2^j},\\
 \mathcal P_{\ell,n}(Z)
   &=\prod_{r=0}^{m-1}P_{\ell,a^{2^r}}(Z)
     =R_\ell(Z)^m
       A_{n-1}\!\left(\frac{Z^\ell}{R_\ell(Z)}\right).
 \end{split}
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{TD}\)}
\]
The displayed rational expression is a polynomial in \(\F_2[Z]\).
Moreover
\[
 R_\ell\in\F_2[Z^2],\qquad R_\ell(0)=1,
 \qquad [Z^{\ell-1}]R_\ell=1,
\]
and the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item $c_n\in E_n^{\times\ell}$, so the singleton-even arm fails at
      \(\ell\);
\item $P_{\ell,a}$ splits completely over $K$;
\item
\[
       \mathcal P_{\ell,n}(Z)\mid H_m(Z)
       \qquad\hbox{in }\F_2[Z].                         \tag{\(\mathsf{TD}_1\)}
\]
\end{enumerate}
In the failure case every root of $P_{\ell,a}$ has absolute trace
one, and
\[
 \sum_{P_{\ell,a}(z)=0}z
 =\prod_{P_{\ell,a}(z)=0}z=a.                          \tag{\(\mathsf{TD}_2\)}
\]
There is also an absolute all-or-nothing alternative:
\(
\mathcal P_{\ell,n}
\)
is irreducible over \(\F_2\) of degree $m\ell$ on success, while on
failure it is the product of exactly \(\ell\) distinct irreducible
polynomials of degree $m$.

Consequently failure forces
\[
 m\ell\leq\frac q2.
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{TD}_3\)}
\]
Equivalently, for the complementary divisor $d=(q+1)/\ell$, one
must have $d\geq2m+1$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Dickson polynomial \(\mathcal D_\ell(X,1)\) contains only odd
powers because \(\ell\) is odd.  Termwise comparison gives the first
identity in \((\mathsf{TD})\) and the three assertions about
\(R_\ell\).  In particular
\[
 P_{\ell,a}'(Z)=Z^{\ell-1};
\]
since $P_{\ell,a}(0)=a\ne0$, this polynomial is separable.

For $s\ne0$, set $z=b/s^2$.  Dickson homogeneity gives
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(1,z)
   =s^{-\ell}\mathcal D_\ell(s,b),
\]
and hence
\[
 P_{\ell,a}(z)
 =\frac{a}{s^{2\ell}}
   \left(1+\mathcal D_\ell(s,b)^2\right).              \tag{\(\mathsf{TD}_4\)}
\]
Thus $s\mapsto b/s^2$ is a Frobenius-equivariant bijection between
the roots of
\(\mathcal D_\ell(X,b)+1\) and the roots of $P_{\ell,a}$; its
inverse sends $z$ to the unique square root of $b/z$.  The
split/irreducible dichotomy in
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-dickson-dichotomy} therefore proves the
equivalence of \textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)}.

Suppose failure holds, and let $s\in K$ be one of the Dickson roots.
If $t,t'$ are the roots of $X^2+sX+b$, then
\[
 \{t^\ell,(t')^\ell\}=\{c_n,c_n+1\}.
\]
Neither right-hand element belongs to $K$, so the quadratic is
irreducible over $K$.  The Artin--Schreier criterion now gives
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(b/s^2)=1.
\]
Thus the \(\ell\) roots of $P_{\ell,a}$ are distinct trace-one
elements of $K$.

The Frobenius conjugates of $a$ are distinct.  The root sets of two
different conjugate polynomials $P_{\ell,a^{2^r}}$ are disjoint:
a common root $z$ would force $R_\ell(z)=0$, then $z^\ell=0$,
contrary to $R_\ell(0)=1$.  Therefore failure supplies $m\ell$
distinct trace-one roots of \(\mathcal P_{\ell,n}\).  The roots of
\(H_m\) are exactly the trace-one elements of $K$, proving
\textup{(iii)}.  Conversely, \textup{(iii)} puts every root of
\(P_{\ell,a}\) in $K$; equation \((\mathsf{TD}_4)\) then puts every
Dickson root in $K$, so Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-dickson-dichotomy}
gives failure.  The coefficient and constant terms of $P_{\ell,a}$
give \((\mathsf{TD}_2)\).

On success $P_{\ell,a}$ is irreducible over $K$.  If $z$ is one
of its roots, then
\[
 a=\frac{z^\ell}{R_\ell(z)}\in\F_2(z),
\]
so $K=\F_2(a)\subseteq\F_2(z)$, and $z$ has absolute degree
$m\ell$.  Hence \(\mathcal P_{\ell,n}\) is its irreducible
polynomial.  On failure every root $z\in K$ still has absolute
degree $m$, by the same displayed identity, and the disjoint
conjugate fibres form exactly \(\ell\) Frobenius orbits.  This proves
the factorization alternative.  Finally
\(\deg H_m=2^{m-1}=q/2\), giving \((\mathsf{TD}_3)\); the equivalent
form follows from $q\geq2m$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the Conway trace is the top-bit functional]
\label{prop:fermat-top-bit-trace}
For every \(n\geq0\), put
\(m=2^n\), \(q=2^m\), \(K=E_{n-1}=\F_q\), and
\(a=a_{n-1}=\prod_{j<n}c_j\), with the empty product
\(a_{-1}=1\).  In the ordinary binary
representatives of the nimbers in \(K\), one has
\[
 a=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}c_j=2^{m-1}=q/2.                  \tag{\(\mathsf{TB}_1\)}
\]
For the Conway bit basis \(e_i=2^i\), \(0\leq i<m\),
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(e_i)=
 \begin{cases}
  0,&i<m-1,\\
  1,&i=m-1.
 \end{cases}                                          \tag{\(\mathsf{TB}_2\)}
\]
Consequently absolute trace is literally the top-bit coordinate:
\[
 \ker\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}=\{0,\ldots,q/2-1\},
 \qquad
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}^{-1}(1)=\{q/2,\ldots,q-1\}.
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{TB}_3\)}
\]
In particular \(a\) is the least Conway ordinal of absolute trace one.

Now assume \(n\geq2\), let the prime \(\ell\mid F_n\), and retain
the polynomial \(P_{\ell,a}\) of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}.  Let
\[
 L_m(Z)=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}Z^{2^i}.
\]
Then the singleton-even arm fails at \(\ell\) exactly when
\[
 \boxed{P_{\ell,a}(Z+a)\mid L_m(Z)\quad\hbox{in }K[Z].}
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{TB}_4\)}
\]
Thus the bad reverse-Dickson fibre is centered, at the literal least
trace-one nimber, inside the trace-zero linearized polynomial.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Products of distinct Conway--Fermat generators require no quadratic
reduction, and therefore agree with their ordinary binary product.  Hence
\[
 a=\prod_{j<n}2^{2^j}
   =2^{\sum_{j<n}2^j}=2^{2^n-1}=2^{m-1},
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{TB}_1)\).

We prove \((\mathsf{TB}_2)\) by induction on \(n\).  The assertion for
\(K=\F_2\) is immediate.  For \(n\geq1\), put \(d=m/2\) and
\(K_0=E_{n-2}=\F_{2^d}\).  If \(i<d\), then \(e_i\in K_0\), so its
relative trace through the quadratic extension \(K/K_0\) is zero.  If
\(i\geq d\), disjoint Fermat support gives
\[
 e_i=c_{n-1}e_{i-d}.
\]
The two roots of the Conway equation for \(c_{n-1}\) differ by one;
hence \(\operatorname{Tr}_{K/K_0}(c_{n-1})=1\), and
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/K_0}(e_i)=e_{i-d}.
\]
Trace transitivity and the induction hypothesis leave the value one
only at \(i-d=d-1\), namely \(i=m-1\).  Linearity now proves
\((\mathsf{TB}_3)\).

By Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}, failure is equivalent
to complete splitting of the separable polynomial \(P_{\ell,a}\) over
\(K\), and every one of its roots has trace one.  Since \(a\) also has
trace one, translation by \(a\) puts all those roots in the trace-zero
space.  Its vanishing polynomial is the separable linearized polynomial
\(L_m\), proving the forward implication in \((\mathsf{TB}_4)\).
Conversely, the divisibility puts every root of \(P_{\ell,a}(Z+a)\),
and hence every root of \(P_{\ell,a}\), in \(K\).  The same proposition
then gives failure.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[additive top-bit countermodels]
\label{prop:fermat-top-bit-countermodel}
Let \(m=2^n\geq2\), put \(q=2^m\), and write
\[
 K=\F_q,\qquad L=\F_{q^2},\qquad
 U=\ker\!\left(\Norm_{L/K}\right)=\mu_{q+1}.
\]
Let the odd prime \(\ell\mid q+1\) satisfy \((q+1)/\ell>1\).
Choose
\[
 w\in U^\ell\setminus\{1\},\qquad u_0\in U,\quad u_0^\ell=w,
 \qquad \Phi(u)=\frac{u}{(u+1)^2},\qquad a=\Phi(w).
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_1\)}
\]
Then \(a\in K\) has absolute trace one and exact degree \(m\) over
\(\F_2\).  With
\[
 T_\ell(Z)=\mathcal D_\ell(1,Z),\qquad
 P_{\ell,a}(Z)=Z^\ell+aT_\ell(Z)^2,
\]
one has
\[
 \begin{split}
 G(Z):=P_{\ell,a}(Z+a)
 &=\prod_{v\in\mu_\ell}
   \bigl(Z+\Phi(u_0v)+a\bigr),\\
 G(Z)&\mid L_m(Z):=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}Z^{2^i}.
 \end{split}                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_2\)}
\]

More precisely, if
\[
 \mathcal T=\{\Phi(u_0v)+a:v\in\mu_\ell\},
 \qquad W=\operatorname{span}_{\F_2}(\mathcal T),
\]
and \(M_W(Z)=\prod_{w_0\in W}(Z+w_0)\) is the subspace
polynomial, then
\[
 \sum_{t\in\mathcal T}t=0,\qquad
 \lceil\log_2\ell\rceil
 \leq\dim_{\F_2}W\leq\min(m-1,\ell-1),                 \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_3\)}
\]
and
\[
 G\mid M_W\mid L_m.                                      \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_4\)}
\]
The trace operator is self-adjoint for
\(\langle x,y\rangle=\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(xy)\), with
\[
 (\ker\operatorname{Tr})^\perp=\F_2,                    \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_5\)}
\]
and the coefficient and derivative constraints are compatible as well:
\[
 [Z^{\ell-1}]G=0,\qquad G'(Z)=(Z+a)^{\ell-1}.          \tag{\(\mathsf{AT}_6\)}
\]

Finally, choose any basis of \(\ker\operatorname{Tr}\) and append
\(a\).  In the induced binary labeling of \(K\), the element \(a\)
is literally the top bit and the least trace-one label.  Hence the
finite-field structure, top-bit trace rule, centered linearized
divisibility, Moore bounds, and adjoint identities are jointly compatible
with failure.  This is not a Conway counterexample: the adapted basis
need not be the multiplicative Fermat basis.  It proves that an exclusion
must use that special multiplicative ancestry, not only additive trace or
a bare choice of binary labeling.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For \(u\in U\setminus\{1\}\), put \(c=(1+u)^{-1}\).  Since
\(u^q=u^{-1}\), one has
\[
 c^q=c+1,\qquad \Phi(u)=c^2+c.
\]
The Artin--Schreier criterion therefore gives
\(\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(\Phi(u))=1\).  In particular \(a\)
has trace one.  If \(a\) lay in a proper subfield
\(\F_{2^d}\subsetneq K\), then \(m/d\) would be an even power of
two and trace transitivity would give
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(a)
 =(m/d)\operatorname{Tr}_{\F_{2^d}/\F_2}(a)=0,
\]
a contradiction.  Thus \(a\) has degree \(m\).

The Dickson semiconjugacy calculation of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-coset-semiconjugacy} applies to every
\(w=u_0^\ell\) and gives the product in \((\mathsf{AT}_2)\).
All values \(\Phi(u_0v)\) have trace one, so their translates by
\(a\) lie in the trace kernel, whose vanishing polynomial is \(L_m\).
The roots are distinct by the inverse-pair calculation in
\((\mathsf{KC}_1)\): for distinct \(v,v'\), equality could only pair
\(u_0v\) with its
inverse, which after raising to \(\ell\) would force \(w=w^{-1}\),
and the odd-order group \(U\) would then force \(w=1\).  This proves
the divisibility.

The original roots have sum \(a\), while translating \(\ell\) of them
adds \(\ell a=a\); hence \(\sum_{t\in\mathcal T}t=0\).  Since
\(W\) contains \(\ell\) distinct points, its dimension is at least
\(\lceil\log_2\ell\rceil\).  The displayed nontrivial dependence
and \(W\subseteq\ker\operatorname{Tr}\) give the two upper bounds.
Root-set inclusion among separable split polynomials proves
\((\mathsf{AT}_4)\).

For the trace pairing,
\[
 \langle\operatorname{Tr}(x),y\rangle
 =\operatorname{Tr}(x)\operatorname{Tr}(y)
 =\langle x,\operatorname{Tr}(y)\rangle.
\]
The one-dimensional space \(\F_2\) is orthogonal to the trace kernel,
so nondegeneracy gives \((\mathsf{AT}_5)\).  Finally
\(P_{\ell,a}'(Z)=Z^{\ell-1}\), and translation cancels its
next-to-leading coefficient because \(\ell\) is odd.  This proves
\((\mathsf{AT}_6)\).  Extending a basis of the trace kernel by \(a\)
gives the final binary labeling.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[half-field additive saturation of a failure fibre]
\label{prop:fermat-additive-saturation}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}.  Write
\[
 q=Q^2=2^m,\qquad K=\F_q,\qquad K_0=\F_Q,
\]
and suppose that the Conway--Fermat condition fails at \(\ell\).
Let \(S\subset K\) be the \(\ell\)-element root set of
\(P_{\ell,a}\).  Then, for every \(t\in K_0\),
\[
 P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)\mid H_m(Z).                         \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_1\)}
\]
These translated polynomials are pairwise distinct; indeed,
\[
 [Z^{\ell-1}]P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)=a+t.                   \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_2\)}
\]
Define the relative-trace polynomial
\[
 \Theta_{\ell,a}(Y)
 =\prod_{z\in S}\bigl(Y+z^Q+z\bigr)\in K_0[Y].
\]
Then
\[
 \prod_{t\in K_0}P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)
 =\Theta_{\ell,a}(Z^Q+Z).                           \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_3\)}
\]
If \(\Theta_{\ell,a}\) is separable, then
\[
 \ell\leq\frac Q2.                                  \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_4\)}
\]
Equivalently, failure at a prime \(\ell>Q/2\) forces distinct roots
\(z,z'\in S\) with
\[
 z^Q+z=(z')^Q+z',
 \qquad\text{or equivalently}\qquad z+z'\in K_0.    \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_5\)}
\]
Thus separability of this one selected relative-trace fibre would give
a square-root-scale sieve strictly sharper than the unconditional degree
bound \((\mathsf{TD}_3)\).  It is an additional selected assertion, not
a consequence of one-step trace and norm data.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Every \(z\in S\) has absolute trace one by
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}.  For \(t\in K_0\),
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(t)
 =\operatorname{Tr}_{K_0/\F_2}(t+t)=0.
\]
Hence all roots \(z+t\) of \(P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)\) are again roots of
the trace-one polynomial \(H_m\), proving \((\mathsf{AS}_1)\).
Because \(R_\ell\) has degree \(\ell-1\) and leading coefficient
one, while \(\ell\) is odd, translation gives
\((\mathsf{AS}_2)\).

Interchanging the two products gives
\[
\begin{split}
 \prod_{t\in K_0}P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)
 &=\prod_{z\in S}\prod_{t\in K_0}(Z+t+z)\\
 &=\prod_{z\in S}\bigl(Z^Q+Z+z^Q+z\bigr),
\end{split}
\]
which is \((\mathsf{AS}_3)\).  If \(\Theta_{\ell,a}\) is separable,
its \(\ell\) relative-trace values are distinct.  The \(Q\) translates
of each root then give \(Q\ell\) distinct trace-one elements of \(K\).
There are exactly \(q/2=Q^2/2\) such elements, proving
\((\mathsf{AS}_4)\).  Negating this injectivity condition gives
\((\mathsf{AS}_5)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[full trace-kernel translation is exactly saturated]
\label{cor:fermat-full-translate-saturation}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-additive-saturation}, and put
\[
 \mathcal T_0=\ker\bigl(\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}\bigr),\qquad
 \mathcal H=\{z\in K:\operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(z)=1\}.
\]
Under the hypothetical failure,
\[
 \boxed{\qquad
 \prod_{t\in\mathcal T_0}P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)=H_m(Z)^\ell.
 \qquad}                                            \tag{\(\mathsf{AS}_6\)}
\]
The \(|\mathcal T_0|\) translated polynomials in this product are pairwise
distinct.  Thus the full additive translation attack is exactly saturated:
each point of the trace-one hyperplane occurs with multiplicity \(\ell\), so
averaging over the whole trace kernel cannot yield a counting inequality or
any Fourier coefficient beyond the two characters forced by the affine
trace hyperplane.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Let \(S\subset\mathcal H\) be the \(\ell\)-element root set of
\(P_{\ell,a}\).  Interchanging finite products gives
\[
 \prod_{t\in\mathcal T_0}P_{\ell,a}(Z+t)
 =\prod_{s\in S}\prod_{t\in\mathcal T_0}(Z+t+s).
\]
For each fixed \(s\in\mathcal H\), translation by \(s\) is a bijection
from \(\mathcal T_0\) to \(\mathcal H\), so the inner product is
\(H_m(Z)\).  This proves \(\mathsf{AS}_6\).  Distinctness follows from
\(\mathsf{AS}_2\).  Lean checks the underlying generic finite-group
product identity; the trace-hyperplane specialization is paper-level.
\end{proof}
\begin{proposition}[relative-trace collision factorization]
\label{prop:fermat-relative-trace-axes}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-additive-saturation}, and put
\[
 U=\ker\!\left(\Norm_{\F_{q^2}/K}\right),\qquad
 U_0=\ker\!\left(\Norm_{K/K_0}\right),\qquad
 \Phi(u)=\frac{u}{(u+1)^2}.
\]
Under the hypothetical failure, choose \(u_0\in U\) with
\(u_0^\ell=w=(c_n+1)/c_n\), so that, as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-coset-semiconjugacy},
\[
 S=\{\Phi(u_0v):v\in\mu_\ell\}.
\]
For \(x^\ell=w\) and \(v\in\mu_\ell\setminus\{1\}\), set
\[
 A=x^Q,\qquad B=v^Q,\qquad
 D=(1+x)(1+xv),\qquad D_Q=(1+A)(1+AB)
\]
and
\[
 \begin{split}
 N_0&=A(1+B)D+x(1+v)D_Q,\\
 N_1&=N_0+DD_Q.
 \end{split}
\]
All four factors in \(DD_Q\) are nonzero, and the collision equation
factors exactly as
\[
 \Phi(A)+\Phi(AB)+\Phi(x)+\Phi(xv)
 =\frac{N_0N_1}{(DD_Q)^2}.                            \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_1\)}
\]

There is a more intrinsic form.  Let \(s\in\mu_\ell\) be the unique
square root of \(v\), put \(y=xs\), and set
\[
 \alpha=\Phi(y),\qquad \beta=\Phi(s).
\]
Then \(y^\ell=w\), \(s^\ell=1\), and \(\alpha\ne\beta\).  The two
factors in \((\mathsf{AX}_1)\) give the disjoint alternatives
\[
 \begin{split}
 N_0=0&\iff \alpha/\beta\in K_0^\times,\\
 N_1=0&\iff \alpha/\beta\in U_0.
 \end{split}                                           \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_2\)}
\]
Consequently the selected polynomial \(\Theta_{\ell,a}\) is
separable if and only if
\[
 \frac{\Phi(y)}{\Phi(s)}
 \notin K_0^\times\cup U_0                            \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_3\)}
\]
for every \(y^\ell=w\) and every
\(s\in\mu_\ell\setminus\{1\}\).  Thus its separability is exactly
a selected two-axis transversality assertion, not merely a generic
property of a transported multiplicative coset.

Equivalently, put \(\sigma=s+s^{-1}\) and use the Dickson power sum
\(\mathcal D_\ell\) of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-root-ancestry}.  A collision exists if
and only if there are
\[
 s\in\mu_\ell\setminus\{1\},\qquad
 \tau\in K_0^\times\cup U_0
\]
such that
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(\sigma,1)=0,\qquad
 \mathcal D_\ell(\tau\sigma,1)=a^{-1}.                \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_4\)}
\]
Thus the Conway value is not discarded: the remaining assertion is
that \(a^{-1}\) avoids the two axis-restrictions of the Dickson map
through every fibotomic zero.  In particular, for
\(z\in K^\times\setminus K_0^\times\) put
\[
 J(z)=\frac{\Norm_{K/K_0}(z)}
            {\operatorname{Tr}_{K/K_0}(z)^2}
      =\Phi(z^{Q-1}),
\]
the selected ancestry \(J(a)=a_{n-2}\) makes every collision satisfy
\[
 J\!\left(\mathcal D_\ell(\tau\sigma,1)\right)=a_{n-2}. \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_5\)}
\]
This last equality is a necessary shadow of \((\mathsf{AX}_4)\), not
an exclusion by itself.

Indeed, generic separability is false even at square-root scale.
For every fixed \(s\in\mu_\ell\setminus\{1\}\), there are exactly
\(Q/2\) elements \(\lambda\in K_0^\times\) for which
\(\lambda\Phi(s)\) has absolute trace one, and hence exactly \(Q\)
elements \(y\in U\setminus\{1\}\) such that
\[
 \frac{\Phi(y)}{\Phi(s)}\in K_0^\times.              \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_6\)}
\]
If \(\ell\leq Q\), at least one of these \(y\)'s lies outside
\(\mu_\ell\).  For \(w'=y^\ell\ne1\), the nonselected transported
coset over \(w'\) then has a type-\(N_0\) relative-trace collision.
This construction includes the range \(Q/2<\ell\leq Q\); therefore
the square-root inequality alone cannot exclude the collision of the
actual Conway coset.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For \(t\in U\setminus\{1\}\), put \(c_t=(1+t)^{-1}\).  Then
\(\Phi(t)=c_t^2+c_t\).  Hence, with
\[
 h=c_x+c_{xv}
   =\frac{x(1+v)}{(1+x)(1+xv)},
\]
one has
\[
 \Phi(x)+\Phi(xv)=h^2+h.                              \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_7\)}
\]
The assumptions \(x^\ell=w\ne1\) and \(v^\ell=1\) show that neither
\(x\) nor \(xv\) is one; the same holds after \(Q\)-Frobenius, so the
displayed denominators are nonzero.  Moreover \(x,v\in U\), and
inversion fixes \(h\), so \(h^q=h\) and \(h\in K\).

The two relative traces collide precisely when
\[
 0=(h^2+h)^Q+(h^2+h)
   =(h^Q+h)^2+(h^Q+h).
\]
Now \(h^Q+h\in K_0\), and the roots of \(T^2+T\) are \(0,1\).
Thus a collision is exactly \(h^Q=h\) or \(h^Q=h+1\).  Directly,
\[
 h^Q+h=\frac{N_0}{DD_Q},
\]
so multiplying
\((h^Q+h)(h^Q+h+1)\) by \((DD_Q)^2\) proves
\((\mathsf{AX}_1)\) and identifies its two factors.

Because \(\ell\) is odd, squaring permutes \(\mu_\ell\), so \(s\)
is unique.  Substitution of \(x=y/s\) and \(v=s^2\) gives
\[
 h=\frac{y(1+s)^2}{(y+s)(1+ys)}
   =\frac{\Phi(y)}{\Phi(y)+\Phi(s)}
   =\frac{\rho}{\rho+1},\qquad
 \rho=\frac{\alpha}{\beta}.                          \tag{\(\mathsf{AX}_8\)}
\]
Here \(y^\ell=w\) and \(s^\ell=1\).  The standard identity
\[
 \Phi(r)=\Phi(t)\iff(r+t)(1+rt)=0
\]
shows that \(\alpha=\beta\) would force \(y=s^{\pm1}\), contrary to
\(w\ne1\).  Thus every denominator in \((\mathsf{AX}_8)\) is nonzero.
It follows that
\[
 h^Q=h\iff\rho^Q=\rho\iff\rho\in K_0^\times,
\]
whereas
\[
 h^Q=h+1
 \iff \frac{\rho^Q}{\rho^Q+1}=\frac1{\rho+1}
 \iff \rho^{Q+1}=1
 \iff \rho\in U_0.
\]
Since
\(K_0^\times\cap U_0\) has order
\(\gcd(Q-1,Q+1)=1\), and \(\rho\ne1\), these alternatives are
disjoint.  Centering an unordered pair of distinct elements of
\(u_0\mu_\ell\) reverses the construction uniquely up to replacing
\(s\) by \(s^{-1}\).  This proves \((\mathsf{AX}_2)\)--
\((\mathsf{AX}_3)\).

Since \(\Phi(t)^{-1}=t+t^{-1}\), take
\(\tau=\beta/\alpha=\rho^{-1}\).  Both axes are closed under
inversion, and
\[
 y+y^{-1}=\tau(s+s^{-1})=\tau\sigma.
\]
The Dickson power-sum identity and \(y^\ell=w\), \(s^\ell=1\) give
\((\mathsf{AX}_4)\).  Conversely, suppose \((\mathsf{AX}_4)\) holds
and let \(y,y^{-1}\) be the roots of
\[
 X^2+\tau\sigma X+1.
\]
Then
\[
 y^\ell+y^{-\ell}=a^{-1}=w+w^{-1},
\]
so \(y^\ell\in\{w,w^{-1}\}\).  The element \(y\) cannot lie in \(K\),
because then \(y^\ell\in K\), whereas
\(w\in U\setminus\{1\}\) and \(U\cap K^\times=\{1\}\).  As the
quadratic has coefficients in \(K\), it follows that \(y^q=y^{-1}\),
so \(y\in U\); replace \(y\) by \(y^{-1}\) if necessary to arrange
\(y^\ell=w\).  Now
\[
 \frac{\Phi(y)}{\Phi(s)}=\tau^{-1}
 \in K_0^\times\cup U_0,
\]
and \((\mathsf{AX}_2)\) gives a collision.  Finally,
\(J(z^{-1})=J(z)\), while the Conway trace and norm recursion gives
\(J(a)=a_{n-2}\), proving \((\mathsf{AX}_5)\).

For the final assertion fix \(s\ne1\) and write \(\beta=\Phi(s)\).
The element \(\beta\) has absolute trace one.  Therefore
\(b_0=\beta^Q+\beta\) is nonzero: if \(\beta\in K_0\), its absolute
trace from the quadratic extension \(K/K_0\) would vanish.  For
\(\lambda\in K_0\), transitivity of trace gives
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{K/\F_2}(\lambda\beta)
 =\operatorname{Tr}_{K_0/\F_2}(\lambda b_0).
\]
This nonzero \(\F_2\)-linear functional takes the value one on exactly
\(Q/2\) elements of \(K_0\), none of them zero.  Every trace-one
element of \(K\) has exactly the inverse pair of lifts under
\(\Phi:U\setminus\{1\}\to K\), so these scalars produce exactly
\(Q\) values of \(y\) satisfying \((\mathsf{AX}_6)\).  At most
\(\ell-1\) of them lie in \(\mu_\ell\).  If \(\ell\leq Q\), choose
one outside, put \(w'=y^\ell\), and center the pair \(y/s,ys\).
It consists of distinct members of the same \(\mu_\ell\)-coset and
has a collision by \((\mathsf{AX}_2)\), completing the construction.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the reverse-Dickson divisor is a transported Kummer coset]
\label{prop:fermat-coset-semiconjugacy}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-trace-divisor}, put \(N=q+1\), and
write
\[
 U=\ker\!\left(\Norm_{\F_{q^2}/\F_q}\right)=\mu_N.
\]
For odd \(r\), define
\[
 T_r(Z)=\mathcal D_r(1,Z),\qquad
 F_r(Z)=\frac{Z^r}{T_r(Z)^2},\qquad
 \Phi(u)=\frac{u}{(u+1)^2}.
\]
Then \(\Phi\) induces a bijection
\[
 (U\setminus\{1\})/(u\sim u^{-1})
 \xrightarrow{\;\sim\;}
 \{z\in\F_q:\operatorname{Tr}_{\F_q/\F_2}(z)=1\},       \tag{\(\mathsf{KC}_1\)}
\]
and one has the semiconjugacy
\[
 T_r(\Phi(u))=\frac{1+u^r}{(1+u)^r},\qquad
 F_r(\Phi(u))=\Phi(u^r).                               \tag{\(\mathsf{KC}_2\)}
\]
Consequently
\[
 H_m(Z)=T_N(Z)
 =\prod_{\substack{\{u,u^{-1}\}\\u\in U\setminus\{1\}}}
   (Z+\Phi(u)).                                       \tag{\(\mathsf{KC}_3\)}
\]

For the selected Conway point put
\[
 w=\frac{c_n+1}{c_n}\in U,\qquad a=\Phi(w)=a_{n-1}.
\]
Failure at \(\ell\) is equivalent to \(w\in U^\ell\).  If
\(u_0^\ell=w\), then
\[
 P_{\ell,a}(Z)
 =Z^\ell+aT_\ell(Z)^2
 =\prod_{v\in\mu_\ell}\bigl(Z+\Phi(u_0v)\bigr).
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{KC}_4\)}
\]
Thus the reverse-Dickson fibre is exactly one multiplicative
\(\mu_\ell\)-coset transported through \(\Phi\).  Furthermore
\[
 T_\ell\mid H_m,\qquad
 \gcd(\mathcal P_{\ell,n},T_\ell)=1,
\]
so failure gives the sharper factor statement
\[
 \mathcal P_{\ell,n}(Z)T_\ell(Z)\mid H_m(Z).           \tag{\(\mathsf{KC}_5\)}
\]
There are exactly
\[
 \frac{N/\ell-1}{2}
\]
distinct trace-one values \(\Phi(w)\) with
\(w\in U^\ell\setminus\{1\}\), each realizing the same transported
coset factorization.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For \(u\in U\setminus\{1\}\), put \(c=(u+1)^{-1}\).  Since
\(u^q=u^{-1}\),
\[
 c^q=c+1,\qquad c^2+c=\Phi(u).
\]
The Artin--Schreier trace criterion gives
\(\operatorname{Tr}_{\F_q/\F_2}(\Phi(u))=1\).  Direct simplification
also gives
\[
 \Phi(u)=\Phi(v)\iff(u+v)(1+uv)=0,
\]
so the fibres are precisely inverse pairs.  Both sides of
\((\mathsf{KC}_1)\) have \(q/2\) elements.

The roots of \(X^2+X+\Phi(u)\) are
\((1+u)^{-1}\) and \(u(1+u)^{-1}\).  The Dickson power-sum identity
therefore proves the first formula in \((\mathsf{KC}_2)\), and the
second follows on squaring the denominator.  Taking \(r=N\) shows
that the monic degree-\(q/2\) polynomials \(T_N\) and \(H_m\) have
the same roots, proving \((\mathsf{KC}_3)\).

One has \(w=c_n^{q-1}\).  In the coprime decomposition
\(\F_{q^2}^{\times}\simeq\F_q^\times\times U\), the
\(\ell\)-power map is an automorphism on \(\F_q^\times\).  Hence
\(c_n\) is an \(\ell\)-th power exactly when \(w\in U^\ell\), by
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-root-ancestry}.
For \(v\in\mu_\ell\), semiconjugacy gives
\[
 F_\ell(\Phi(u_0v))=\Phi((u_0v)^\ell)=\Phi(w)=a.
\]
These \(\ell\) values are distinct.  Indeed, an inverse collision
inside \(u_0\mu_\ell\) would imply \(w^2=1\), hence \(w=1\) because
\(|U|\) is odd; but \(w=1\) is impossible.  The monic degree-\(\ell\)
polynomial \(P_{\ell,a}\) therefore has precisely the roots in
\((\mathsf{KC}_4)\).

The same argument on \(\mu_\ell\setminus\{1\}\) gives
\(T_\ell\mid T_N\).  Also
\[
 P_{\ell,a}\equiv Z^\ell\pmod{T_\ell},\qquad T_\ell(0)=1,
\]
and the same congruence holds for every Frobenius conjugate.  This
proves coprimality and \((\mathsf{KC}_5)\).  Finally the image
\(U^\ell\) has order \(N/\ell\); its nonidentity elements form
\((N/\ell-1)/2\) inverse pairs, and \((\mathsf{KC}_1)\) proves the
count.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[exact fibotomic support of a hypothetical failure]
\label{rem:fermat-coset-support}
Use the fibotomic polynomials \(\mathfrak F_s\) of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-fibotomic}.  Put
\[
 e=v_\ell(N),\qquad
 \delta=\ord(w)=\ell^f\delta_0,\qquad
 (\ell,\delta_0)=1.
\]
Under failure \(0\leq f\leq e-1\).  If \(f=0\), the coset
\(u_0\mu_\ell\) contains exactly one element of order \(\delta\) and
\(\ell-1\) elements of order \(\ell\delta\).  Accordingly
\(\mathcal P_{\ell,n}\) has exactly one degree-\(m\) irreducible factor
in \(\mathfrak F_\delta\) and exactly \(\ell-1\) in
\(\mathfrak F_{\ell\delta}\).  If \(f\geq1\), every element of the
coset has order \(\ell\delta\), and all \(\ell\) factors lie in
\(\mathfrak F_{\ell\delta}\).  This includes the cases
\(v_\ell(N)>1\).

These support constraints do not close the arm.  Every trace-one
element of \(\F_q\) has absolute degree \(m\): if it lay in a proper
subfield \(\F_{2^d}\), then \(m/d\) would be even and its absolute
trace would vanish.  Hence the only resulting capacity inequality is
\[
 \varphi(\delta)\geq2m.
\]
It is automatic, because \(\delta>1\) divides \(2^m+1\), while \(m\)
is a power of two; therefore \(\ord_\delta(2)=2m\).
The full divisor, derivative, Hasse-derivative, Newton-sum, coefficient,
and fibotomic-support packages are thus realized by transported
nonselected cosets.  Excluding the selected one still requires the
recursive Conway ancestry.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[the reverse-Dickson gap is selected but not yet excluded]
\label{rem:fermat-trace-gap}
Put \(e=v_2(\ell-1)\).  For \(e\geq2\), substitution
\(X=W+W^{-1}\) gives
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(X,1)+X
 =(W^{\ell-1}+1)(W+W^{-\ell}).
\]
At \(W=1\) the two factors vanish to orders \(2^e\) and \(2\), while
\(X\) has order \(2\).  Therefore
\[
 \mathcal D_\ell(X,1)\equiv X
 \pmod{X^{2^{e-1}+1}}.                                 \tag{\(\mathsf{TG}\)}
\]
Every prime divisor of the Fermat number \(F_n=q+1\) has
\(e\geq n+2\).  Hence
\[
 \begin{split}
 P_{\ell,a}(Z)
 &=Z^\ell+aZ^{\ell-1}
   +\text{terms of degree at most }\ell-(2m+1),\\
 \mathcal P_{\ell,n}(Z)
 &=Z^{m(\ell-1)}A_{n-1}(Z)
   +\text{terms of degree at most }m\ell-(2m+1).
 \end{split}
                                                               \tag{\(\mathsf{TG}_1\)}
\]
This long leading gap still does not contradict
\((\mathsf{TD}_1)\).  For \(n>2\), every nontrivial complementary
divisor \(d\mid F_n\) satisfies \(d\equiv1\pmod{4m}\), hence
\(d\geq4m+1\) and \(m\ell<q/4\).  A hypothetical quotient
\(H_m/\mathcal P_{\ell,n}\) is therefore long enough to absorb the
leading gap.  The divisor criterion retains the actual selected
polynomial \(A_{n-1}\); its elementary degree and coefficient
consequences alone do not decide it.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Elliptic and generalized-Jacobian reformulations}

\begin{proposition}[supersingular elliptic form of the Conway transition]
\label{prop:fermat-elliptic}
For $i\geq0$ put
\[
 u_i:=\frac{c_i+1}{c_i}.
\]
Fix $n\geq1$, set $Q=2^{2^{n-1}}$, $v=u_{n-1}$ and $u=u_n$.
Then the exact Conway recursion is
\[
 \frac{u}{(u+1)^2}=\frac{v}{(v+1)^3},                    \tag{\(\mathsf E\)}
\]
or equivalently
\[
 u(v+1)^3=v(u+1)^2,
 \qquad
 u+u^{-1}=v^2+v+1+v^{-1}.
\]
The normalization of this correspondence is the fixed supersingular
elliptic curve over $\F_2$
\[
 \mathcal E:\qquad y^2+y=x^3+x^2.                       \tag{\(\mathsf W\)}
\]
The maps to the $v$- and $u$-lines have degrees two and three,
respectively; in particular the transition has no rational section
recovering $u$ from $v$.

On the original $(u,v)$ correspondence, the rational map
\[
 \mathcal A(u,v)=\left(\frac{u+v}{uv+1},v^{-1}\right)
\]
extends to an automorphism of order four, with
\[
 \mathcal A^2(u,v)=(u^{-1},v).
\]
For the distinguished Conway point $P_n=(u_n,u_{n-1})$ one has
\[
 \Frob_Q(P_n)=\mathcal A(P_n)
 \quad\hbox{or}\quad
 \mathcal A^{-1}(P_n).                                   \tag{\(\mathsf Q\)}
\]
Thus, at \(P_n\), relative Frobenius is one of the two quarter-turns
\(\mathcal A^{\pm1}\) on the elliptic correspondence.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $a_i=\prod_{j\leq i}c_j$.  The identities
\[
 a_{n-1}=\frac{u}{(u+1)^2},\qquad
 a_{n-1}=a_{n-2}c_{n-1}
 =\frac{v}{(v+1)^2}\frac1{v+1}
\]
give \((\mathsf E)\); inversion gives the second displayed form.

Put $U=u+1$, $V=v+1$, and blow up the affine singularity
\((U,V)=(0,0)\) by
$X=U/V$, $Y=V$.  After division by $V^2$ the equation becomes
\[
 XY^2+(X^2+1)Y+X^2=0.
\]
Its homogenization
\[
 H=XY^2+(X^2+Z^2)Y+X^2Z
\]
has partial derivatives $(Y^2,X^2+Z^2,X^2)$ and is therefore smooth.
The point $[1:1:0]$ is a flex.  The linear change
\[
 (x,y,z)=(Z,X,X+Y+Z)
\]
takes $H=0$ to
$y^2z+yz^2=x^3+x^2z$, which is \((\mathsf W)\).  This curve has
five \(\F_2\)-points, hence trace \(-2\), and is supersingular.  The original
equation has degrees two in $u$ and three in $v$, proving the degrees
of the two projections.  A rational section of the nontrivial
degree-two finite cover would induce a retraction of its degree-two
function-field extension, impossible for a proper finite field
extension.

Direct substitution in \((\mathsf E)\) gives
\[
 \frac{\mathcal A_u}{(\mathcal A_u+1)^2}
 =v\frac{u}{(u+1)^2}
 =\frac{v^{-1}}{(v^{-1}+1)^3},
\]
so $\mathcal A$ preserves the correspondence.  A second substitution
gives $\mathcal A^2(u,v)=(u^{-1},v)$, proving the order assertion.
Thus this birational self-map and its birational inverse extend over
the smooth projective normalization.

Finally $v^Q=v^{-1}$ and
\[
 \left(\frac{u}{(u+1)^2}\right)^Q
 =\left(\frac{v}{(v+1)^3}\right)^Q
 =v\frac{u}{(u+1)^2}.
\]
The fibres of $z\mapsto z/(z+1)^2$ are the inverse-pairs
$\{z,z^{-1}\}$.  The first coordinate of $\Frob_Q(P_n)$ is therefore
either $\mathcal A_u(P_n)$ or its inverse, while the second coordinate
is $v^{-1}$, proving \((\mathsf Q)\).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[\(X_0(11)\) reduction, but not a modular tower]
\label{rem:fermat-x011}
The elliptic curve \(\mathcal E\) is the good reduction modulo two of
\[
 y^2+y=x^3-x^2-10x-20,
\]
the standard \(X_0(11)\) model (Cremona \(11\mathrm{a}1\), LMFDB
\(11.\mathrm{a}2\))~\cite{LMFDB11}.  Its discriminant is \(-11^5\),
and reduction gives \((\mathsf W)\).  This observation does not turn
the Conway recursion into a modular-curve tower.  The two Conway
coordinate maps have degrees two and three, whereas
\(X_0(11)\to X(1)\) has degree twelve and the standard degeneracy maps
\(X_0(11^{r+1})\to X_0(11^r)\), \(r\geq1\), have degree eleven.
Moreover
\[
 j=-\frac{2^{12}31^3}{11^5}
\]
is nonintegral, so the characteristic-zero curve has no complex
multiplication.  The order-four endomorphism \(i=\pi+1\) in
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-elliptic-kummer} is extra supersingular
structure created after reduction; it does not lift to an endomorphism
of this characteristic-zero \(X_0(11)\) model.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[the successive-point overlap has zero CM component]
\label{prop:fermat-overlap-zero}
On \(\mathcal E\), the two Conway coordinates are
\[
 u=\frac{x^2+y}{x},\qquad v=\frac{y+1}{x},
\]
of degrees three and two respectively.  Let
\[
 \mathcal C=
 \widetilde{\mathcal E\times_{v,\mathbf P^1,u}\mathcal E}
\]
be the normalization of the fibre product, and write
\(p,q:\mathcal C\to\mathcal E\) for the first and second
projections.  Then \(\mathcal C\) is integral,
\[
 \deg p=3,\qquad \deg q=2,                             \tag{\(\mathsf{OV}_1\)}
\]
and the correspondence induced on \(\operatorname{Pic}^0(\mathcal E)\)
vanishes in both directions:
\[
 q_*p^*=0,\qquad p_*q^*=0.                            \tag{\(\mathsf{OV}_2\)}
\]
Equivalently, its numerical class has bidegree \((3,2)\) and zero
\(\operatorname{End}(\mathcal E)\)-component.  In particular the
overlap, when specialized to \(n\geq2\),
\[
 v(P_n)=u(P_{n-1})
\]
is not the graph of an isogeny and has no nonzero isogeny or CM
component; in particular it is not a Hecke correspondence acting
nontrivially on \(\operatorname{Pic}^0(\mathcal E)\).

This failure persists at the Kummer level.  Put
\[
 \mathfrak c=\frac1{u+1},\qquad
 \mathfrak d=\frac1{v+1}.
\]
On \(\mathcal C\),
\[
 q^*\mathfrak c=p^*\mathfrak d,                       \tag{\(\mathsf{OV}_3\)}
\]
For \(n\geq2\), put
\(\widetilde P_n=(P_n,P_{n-1})\in\mathcal C\).  Then
\[
 (p^*\mathfrak c)(\widetilde P_n)=c_n,\qquad
 (q^*\mathfrak c)(\widetilde P_n)
  =(p^*\mathfrak d)(\widetilde P_n)=c_{n-1}.
\]
For every prime \(\ell>3\), the class of \(p^*\mathfrak c\) is not in the span
of \(p^*\mathfrak d\) in
\(\overline{\F}_2(\mathcal C)^\times/
 \overline{\F}_2(\mathcal C)^{\times\ell}\).
Thus the correspondence yields no monomial Kummer relation between the
new function \(p^*\mathfrak c\) and the transported old function
\(p^*\mathfrak d=q^*\mathfrak c\).  This global divisor statement does
not decide the specialized class \([c_n]\), including when
\(\ell\mid F_n\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The \(v\)-map is the separable Galois double cover whose deck
involution is \(P\mapsto T-P\); the \(u\)-map is separable of odd
degree three.  Their function fields over
\(\overline{\F}_2(\mathbf P^1)\) have coprime degrees and hence are
linearly disjoint.  This proves integrality and
\((\mathsf{OV}_1)\).  Norm--conorm compatibility in the compositum of
these function fields gives
\[
 q_*p^*=u^*v_*
\]
on degree-zero divisor classes.  But
\(\operatorname{Pic}^0(\mathbf P^1)=0\), so this map vanishes; the
transpose argument proves the other equality in
\((\mathsf{OV}_2)\).

Equation \((\mathsf{OV}_3)\) is the defining equality
\(v\circ p=u\circ q\).  The divisors on \(\mathcal E\) are
\[
\begin{split}
 \operatorname{div}(\mathfrak c)
   &=[-T]+2[O]-3[-2T],\\
 \operatorname{div}(\mathfrak d)
   &=[O]+[T]-2[-2T].
\end{split}
\]
At a point of \(\mathcal C\) above \(-T\) under \(p\), the valuation
of \(p^*\mathfrak c\) is the ramification index, hence one, two, or
three, while the valuation of \(p^*\mathfrak d\) is zero.  Since every
prime divisor of \(F_n\), \(n\geq1\), is larger than three, no relation
\[
 p^*\mathfrak c=(p^*\mathfrak d)^r h^\ell
\]
is possible.  This proves the Kummer assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[new-prime character neutrality and the exact Kummer target]
\label{prop:fermat-kummer}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-elliptic} and let
$\ell$ be a prime divisor of $Q^2+1=F_n$.  If
$\chi:E_n^\times=\F_{Q^4}^\times\to\mu_\ell$ has exact order
$\ell$, and
\[
 \alpha=\chi(u),\qquad \beta=\chi(u+1),
\]
then
\[
 \alpha=\beta^2,\qquad \beta^{Q^2}=\beta^{-1}.           \tag{\(\mathsf K\)}
\]
The second identity is tautological because
$Q^2\equiv-1\pmod\ell$; in particular the exact lower transition and
the quarter-turn Frobenius symmetry do not by themselves exclude
$\beta=1$.

The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)}]
\item the order $\delta_n$ contains the full $\ell$-primary part of
      $F_n$;
\item $u_n\notin U_n^\ell$, where
      $U_n=\ker\Norm_{E_n/E_{n-1}}$;
\item $c_n\notin(E_n^\times)^\ell$;
\item $X^\ell-c_n$ is irreducible over $E_n$.
\end{enumerate}
Consequently the all-level Conway--Fermat theorem is exactly the
nontriviality, for every $\ell\mid F_n$, of this new Kummer symbol at
the distinguished point of \((\mathsf W)\).  No lower-field
multiplicative character can supply its value.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The lower field has order $Q^2$, and
$\gcd(\ell,Q^2-1)=1$, so $\chi$ is trivial on all of
$E_{n-1}^\times$, in particular on every expression in $v$.  Applying
$\chi$ to \((\mathsf E)\) gives $\alpha=\beta^2$.  Since
$u^{Q^2}=u^{-1}$,
\[
 (u+1)^{Q^2}=u^{-1}+1=\frac{u+1}{u},
\]
which gives the other identity in \((\mathsf K)\).  It reduces to
$\beta^{-1}=\beta^{-1}$ modulo $\ell$.

In the cyclic group $U_n$ of order $F_n$, nonmembership in
$U_n^\ell$ is equivalent to having the full $\ell$-primary order.
Here
\[
 U_n^\ell=U_n\cap(E_n^\times)^\ell.                    \tag{\(\mathsf K_0\)}
\]
Indeed, if \(u=x^\ell\in U_n\), then
\(\Norm_{E_n/E_{n-1}}(x)^\ell=1\); because
\(\gcd(\ell,Q^2-1)=1\), this forces \(\Norm(x)=1\), so \(x\in U_n\).
The reverse inclusion is immediate.
Moreover \((\mathsf E)\) lies in the lower field, hence is an
$\ell$-th power in $E_n$.  In
$E_n^\times/(E_n^\times)^\ell$ it therefore says
\[
 [u]=2[u+1].
\]
Since $\ell$ is odd, $u$ is an $\ell$-th power exactly when $u+1$ is.
But $u+1=c_n^{-1}$, proving the equivalence of the first three
assertions.  Finally $\mu_\ell\subset E_n$, so the prime-degree Kummer
criterion proves the equivalence with irreducibility of
$X^\ell-c_n$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[a Fermat Kummer coordinate is born once and transported by \(3/2\)]
\label{prop:fermat-kummer-tail}
Fix \(n\geq1\), let \(M=\ell^e\parallel F_n\), and, in additive
notation, put
\[
 \mathcal H_j=E_j^\times/(E_j^\times)^M.
\]
Then
\[
 \mathcal H_j=0\quad(j<n),\qquad
 \mathcal H_j\cong\mathbf Z/M\mathbf Z\quad(j\geq n).       \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_1\)}
\]
For \(j>n\), inclusion induces an isomorphism
\(\iota_j:\mathcal H_{j-1}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal H_j\).
If
\[
 \kappa_j=[a_j]_M,
\]
then
\[
 \kappa_n=[c_n]_M=-\frac12[w_n]_M,
 \qquad
 2[c_j]_M=\iota_j(\kappa_{j-1}),
 \qquad
 \boxed{\ \kappa_j=\frac32\,\iota_j(\kappa_{j-1})\ }
 \quad(j>n).                                           \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_2\)}
\]
Thus, under the canonical identifications along the tail,
\[
 \boxed{\ \kappa_j=(3/2)^{j-n}\kappa_n\quad(j\geq n).\ } \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_3\)}
\]
The multiplier \(3/2\) is a unit modulo \(M\).  Consequently the exact
\(\ell\)-adic divisibility of the selected class is invariant along the
whole future tower, and for every \(j\geq n\),
\[
 c_n\in(E_n^\times)^\ell
 \iff a_j\in(E_j^\times)^\ell.                         \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_4\)}
\]

For \(j>n\), the current driver \(w_j\) has order dividing \(F_j\), which
is coprime to \(M\), and hence
\[
 [w_j]_M=0,
 \qquad
 [w_j+1]_M=-\frac12\,\iota_j(\kappa_{j-1}).             \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_5\)}
\]
In particular a failure born at \((n,\ell)\) is equivalent to every---or
any---future selected translate \(w_j+1\) being an \(\ell\)-th power.
These are not independent future conditions.

Let \(A_j\) be the minimal polynomial of \(a_j\) over \(\F_2\).  The same
transport has the exact arboreal form
\[
\begin{split}
 \kappa_n\notin\ell\mathcal H_n
 &\iff A_j(X^\ell)\text{ is irreducible over }\F_2,\\
 \kappa_n\in\ell\mathcal H_n
 &\iff A_j(X^\ell)\text{ is a product of exactly \(\ell\) distinct
 irreducibles of degree }[E_j:\F_2]
\end{split}                                             \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_6\)}
\]
for one, equivalently every, \(j\geq n\).  Hence the infinite future
resultant tree supplies no accumulating obstruction: its irreducible or
completely split behavior is an exact copy of the single Kummer coordinate
born at level \(n\).

The transport is equally explicit on the selected Euler symbols.  Put
\[
 \mathfrak h_j
 =a_j^{(|E_j|-1)/\ell}\in\mu_\ell
 \qquad(j\geq n).
\]
Then
\[
 \mathfrak h_n=w_n^{F_n/\ell},
 \qquad
 \boxed{\ \mathfrak h_j=\mathfrak h_{j-1}^3
       =\mathfrak h_n^{3^{j-n}}\quad(j>n).\ }        \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_7\)}
\]
Since \(\ell\ne3\), cubing is an automorphism of \(\mu_\ell\).  Thus the
selected Euler residue remains nontrivial or trivial along the entire tail.
Motivated by the birth Hilbert-symbol normalization, put
\(\widehat{\mathfrak h}_j=\mathfrak h_j^{-2}\).  Then
\[
 \widehat{\mathfrak h}_n=H
 \quad\text{in \((\mathsf{GL}_6)\)},
 \qquad
 \widehat{\mathfrak h}_j=\widehat{\mathfrak h}_{j-1}^3.
\]

There is also a simultaneous direct-product diagonalization.  At the edge
\(E_{j-1}\subset E_j\), put \(q=|E_{j-1}|\), let
\(s_j\in E_{j-1}\) be the unique square root
of \(a_{j-1}\), and put \(g_j=c_j/s_j\).  Then
\[
 \Norm(g_j)=1,
 \qquad
 c_j=s_jg_j,
 \qquad
 a_j=s_j^3g_j,
 \qquad
 w_j=g_j^{-2}.                                      \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_8\)}
\]
Because \(q-1\) and \(q+1\) are coprime, multiplication gives the canonical
split
\[
 E_j^\times\cong E_{j-1}^\times\times U_j,
 \qquad
 a_j\longleftrightarrow
 \bigl(a_{j-1}^{3/2},w_j^{-1/2}\bigr).              \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_9\)}
\]
Consequently, for \(1\leq m\leq j\), the \(U_m\)-coordinate of \(a_j\) is
\[
 w_m^{-\frac12(\frac32)^{j-m}},                     \tag{\(\mathsf{KT}_{10}\)}
\]
where the exponent is read in \(\mathbf Z/F_m\mathbf Z\).  Every displayed
exponent is a unit.  Thus multiplicative CRT diagonalizes the complete
ancestry into independently born Fermat coordinates; only the additive
Conway equation can couple them, and evaluating that coupling is the original
selected birth-symbol problem.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The cyclic group \(E_j^\times\) has order
\(\prod_{r=0}^jF_r\).  Pairwise coprimality of the Fermat numbers gives
\((\mathsf{KT}_1)\).  For \(j>n\), suppose that a lower element \(x\)
becomes an \(M\)-th power in \(E_j\).  Taking the quadratic norm shows that
\(x^2\) is an \(M\)-th power in \(E_{j-1}\).  Since \(M\) is odd,
multiplication by two is invertible on \(\mathcal H_{j-1}\), so \(x\) was
already an \(M\)-th power.  Thus \(\iota_j\) is injective; its source and
target both have order \(M\), so it is an isomorphism.

At the birth edge every lower element is uniquely \(M\)-divisible, whence
\(\kappa_n=[c_n]_M\).  The relation
\(c_n(c_n+1)=a_{n-1}\) gives
\([w_n]_M=[c_n+1]_M-[c_n]_M=-2[c_n]_M\).
For \(j>n\), the norm identity
\[
 \Norm_{E_j/E_{j-1}}(c_j)=a_{j-1}
\]
and the fact that norm of an included lower element is its square imply
\(2[c_j]_M=\iota_j(\kappa_{j-1})\).  Since
\(a_j=a_{j-1}c_j\), multiplying its class by two gives
\[
 2\kappa_j
 =2\iota_j(\kappa_{j-1})+2[c_j]_M
 =3\iota_j(\kappa_{j-1}),
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{KT}_2)\)--\((\mathsf{KT}_3)\).  No Fermat number
\(F_n\), \(n\geq1\), is divisible by two or three, so both denominators and
the multiplier are units modulo \(M\).  This proves \((\mathsf{KT}_4)\).
For \(j>n\), \(w_j\) lies in the norm-one group of order \(F_j\), coprime
to \(M\), while \(w_j+1=c_j^{-1}\); this proves \((\mathsf{KT}_5)\).

Finally \(\mu_\ell\subset E_j\) for \(j\geq n\).  Prime-degree Kummer
theory and Capelli's lemma say that \(A_j(X^\ell)\) is irreducible exactly
when \(a_j\) is not an \(\ell\)-th power in \(E_j\).  In the power case
\(X^\ell-a_j\) splits into \(\ell\) distinct linear factors over \(E_j\).
Every root retains full absolute degree, because its \(\ell\)-th power is
the full-degree element \(a_j\).  This gives exactly \(\ell\) distinct
irreducible factors of degree \([E_j:\F_2]\), proving
\((\mathsf{KT}_6)\).

For the birth identity in \((\mathsf{KT}_7)\), put
\(q=|E_{n-1}|\).  The lower factor in \(a_n=a_{n-1}c_n\) disappears at
exponent \((q^2-1)/\ell\), and the Conway-unit Euler identity gives
\[
 \mathfrak h_n
 =c_n^{(q-1)(q+1)/\ell}=w_n^{(q+1)/\ell}.
\]
For \(j>n\), put \(Q=|E_{j-1}|\) and \(h=(Q-1)/\ell\).  Frobenius
fixity and \(c_j^{Q+1}=a_{j-1}\) give
\[
 \mathfrak h_j
 =(a_{j-1}c_j)^{(Q+1)h}
 =(a_{j-1}^{h})^2a_{j-1}^{h}
 =\mathfrak h_{j-1}^3.
\]
For \((\mathsf{KT}_8)\), the norm of both \(c_j\) and the included lower
element \(s_j\) is \(a_{j-1}=s_j^2\), so \(g_j\) has norm one.  Since
\(g_j^q=g_j^{-1}\) and \(c_j^q=c_j+1\), one has
\[
 w_j=\frac{c_j^q}{c_j}=\frac{g_j^q}{g_j}=g_j^{-2}.
\]
The remaining identities are immediate.  Coprimality of \(q-1\) and
\(q+1\) proves \((\mathsf{KT}_9)\), and iteration multiplies every old
coordinate exponent by \(3/2\), proving \((\mathsf{KT}_{10})\).
Lean independently checks this generic norm/Frobenius cube identity and the denominator-free
transport identities \(2[c_j]=\iota_j(\kappa_{j-1})\) and
\(2\kappa_j=3\iota_j(\kappa_{j-1})\).
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Dickson--Conway resultant and parallel rays]
\label{prop:fermat-dickson-conway-resultant}
For odd \(\ell\), let
\(\mathcal D_0(S,R)=0\), \(\mathcal D_1(S,R)=S\), and
\[
 \mathcal D_{r+2}(S,R)
 =S\mathcal D_{r+1}(S,R)+R\mathcal D_r(S,R)
\]
in characteristic two.  With the monic convention
\(\operatorname{Res}_S(f,g)=\prod_{f(s)=0}g(s)\), one has the universal
identity
\[
 \boxed{\quad
 \operatorname{Res}_S\!\left(
   \mathcal D_\ell(S,R)+1,\ Z^2+SRZ+R^3
 \right)
 =Z^{2\ell}+R^\ell Z^\ell+R^{3\ell}.
 \quad}                                                \tag{\(\mathsf{DCR}_1\)}
\]

Now let \(\ell\) be prime and fix \(M=\ell^e\parallel F_n\).  At the
birth edge put \(a=a_{n-1}\) and \(Q_a(T)=T^2+aT+a^3\).  If the
coordinate born at \((n,\ell)\) fails, the unique lower root
\(R^\ell=a\) lies in \(E_{n-1}\), and
\[
 Q_a(Z^\ell)
 =\prod_{\mathcal D_\ell(s,R)=1}
     \bigl(Z^2+sRZ+R^3\bigr),                         \tag{\(\mathsf{DCR}_2\)}
\]
where the \(\ell\) factors are distinct irreducible quadratics over
\(E_{n-1}\).  This is the coefficient-exact birth-edge refinement of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-dickson-dichotomy}.

At a later edge \(j>n\), use the normalized roots below: put
\(R=r_{j-1}\), \(T=r_j\), \(q=|E_{j-1}|\), and
\(d=T+T^q\in E_{j-1}\).  Since now
\(\mu_\ell\subset E_{j-1}\), one instead has
\[
 Q_{a_{j-1}}(Z^\ell)
 =\prod_{i\bmod\ell}
   \bigl(Z^2+\zeta^i dZ+\zeta^{2i}R^3\bigr).          \tag{\(\mathsf{DCR}_{2a}\)}
\]
These are again \(\ell\) distinct irreducible quadratics.  If the born
coordinate succeeds, \(Q_{a_{j-1}}(Z^\ell)\) is irreducible of degree
\(2\ell\) at every \(j\geq n\).  Thus relative Frobenius is respectively
\(\ell\) disjoint transpositions or one \(2\ell\)-cycle.  Both patterns are
closed under every later Conway transition; factor counts, symmetric
coefficients, and relative arboreal monodromy add no new obstruction.

More precisely, in the failure case choose the unique lower root
\(r_{n-1}^\ell=a_{n-1}\).  Every one of the \(\ell\) choices
\(r_n^\ell=a_n\) automatically satisfies
\[
 \Norm_{E_n/E_{n-1}}(r_n)=r_{n-1}^3.                 \tag{\(\mathsf{DCR}_3\)}
\]
Given one such \(r_n\), there is for each \(j>n\) a unique continuation
\[
 r_j^\ell=a_j,
 \qquad
 \Norm_{E_j/E_{j-1}}(r_j)=r_{j-1}^3.                \tag{\(\mathsf{DCR}_4\)}
\]
All normalized infinite rays therefore form one \(\mu_\ell\)-torsor, not
an \(\ell\)-branching tree.  If two rays differ by
\(r'_j=\zeta_jr_j\), then only after the birth edge,
\[
 \zeta_j^2=\zeta_{j-1}^3,
 \qquad
 \zeta_j=\zeta_n^{(3/2)^{j-n}}\quad(j>n).            \tag{\(\mathsf{DCR}_5\)}
\]
Hence the full compatible root tower is precisely the root-level form of
the \(3/2\)-transport in \((\mathsf{KT}_3)\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The Dickson polynomial is monic of \(S\)-degree \(\ell\).  Work over an
algebraic closure of \(\F_2(R)\).  Choose
\(\alpha+\beta=1\), \(\alpha\beta=R^\ell\), then choose
\(x^\ell=\alpha\), put \(y=R/x\), and let \(\zeta\) be a primitive
\(\ell\)-th root of unity.  The \(\ell\) elements
\[
 s_i=x\zeta^i+y\zeta^{-i}\qquad(i\bmod\ell)
\]
satisfy \(\mathcal D_\ell(s_i,R)=x^\ell+y^\ell=1\).  They are distinct:
an equality identifies the unordered root pairs of
\(T^2+s_iT+R\); the crossed identification would give
\(\alpha=\beta\), impossible because \(\alpha+\beta=1\).  Moreover
\[
 Z^2+s_iRZ+R^3
 =(Z+Rx\zeta^i)(Z+Ry\zeta^{-i}).
\]
Multiplying over \(i\) gives
\[
 (Z^\ell+R^\ell\alpha)(Z^\ell+R^\ell\beta)
 =Z^{2\ell}+R^\ell Z^\ell+R^{3\ell},
\]
which proves \((\mathsf{DCR}_1)\).  At the birth edge, substituting
\(R^\ell=a\) gives the right side \(Q_a(Z^\ell)\), while
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-dickson-dichotomy} puts every \(s_i\) in
\(E_{n-1}\).  A displayed quadratic cannot have a root there, since its
\(\ell\)-th power would be one of the two roots of \(Q_a\), both outside
\(E_{n-1}\).  This proves \((\mathsf{DCR}_2)\).

At a future edge Frobenius fixes \(\zeta\) and pairs
\(T\zeta^i\) with \(T^q\zeta^i\), which gives
\((\mathsf{DCR}_{2a})\) directly from
\(TT^q=R^3\).  Their constants are distinct because squaring permutes
\(\mu_\ell\), and the same root argument proves that the quadratics are
irreducible.  Under success, an \(\ell\)-th root of \(a_j\) has relative
degree \(2\ell\), proving the asserted irreducibility at every edge.

For \((\mathsf{DCR}_3)\), the quotient of
\(\Norm(r_n)\) by \(r_{n-1}^3\) lies in
\(\mu_\ell\cap E_{n-1}^\times=\{1\}\).  At a later edge an arbitrary
\(\ell\)-th root has norm \(\xi r_{j-1}^3\) for some
\(\xi\in\mu_\ell\); multiplying it by the unique
\(\eta\in\mu_\ell\) with \(\eta^2=\xi^{-1}\) gives
\((\mathsf{DCR}_4)\), and uniqueness follows because squaring is bijective
on odd-order \(\mu_\ell\).  Comparing two continuations proves
\((\mathsf{DCR}_5)\).  The single quadratic factorization underlying
\((\mathsf{DCR}_1)\) is independently kernel-checked in
\texttt{Ogdoad.Excess}; the root enumeration and resultant are paper-level.
Base-rationality of the constant-term Dickson factors uses the birth
congruence \(|E_{n-1}|\equiv-1\pmod\ell\); later edges require
\((\mathsf{DCR}_{2a})\).  These identities prove coefficient and monodromy
compatibility under hypothetical failure, not the value of the initial
selected Kummer coordinate.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[exact cyclotomic lift of the selected Fermat symbol]
\label{prop:fermat-cyclotomic-symbol}
Put \(m=2^n\), \(q=2^m\), and \(N=q+1=F_n\).  Choose a generator
\(\omega\) of the norm-one group
\[
 U_n=\ker\!\left(\Norm_{\F_{q^2}/\F_q}\right)
\]
independently of the Conway element, and write
\[
 w_n=\frac{c_n+1}{c_n}=\omega^{r_n},
 \qquad r_n\in\mathbf Z/N\mathbf Z.                 \tag{\(\mathsf{CS}_1\)}
\]
Let \(\mathfrak P_\omega\mid2\) be the prime of
\(\mathbf Q(\zeta_N)\) specified by
\(\zeta_N\bmod\mathfrak P_\omega=\omega\), and define the
cyclotomic unit
\[
 \varepsilon_{r_n}=(1+\zeta_N^{r_n})^{-1}.
\]
Its residue is \(c_n\).  For every prime \(\ell\mid N\), the
\(\ell\)-th power-residue symbol, identified with its reduction in
\(\mu_\ell\subset\F_{q^2}^{\times}\), is
\[
 \boxed{
 \left(\frac{\varepsilon_{r_n}}{\mathfrak P_\omega}\right)_\ell
   =\omega^{r_nN/\ell}=w_n^{N/\ell}.}               \tag{\(\mathsf{CS}_2\)}
\]
Consequently
\[
 \left(\frac{\varepsilon_{r_n}}{\mathfrak P_\omega}\right)_\ell
 \ne1
 \iff \ell\nmid r_n
 \iff v_\ell(\delta_n)=v_\ell(N)
 \iff A_{n-1}\nmid S_{N/\ell}.                     \tag{\(\mathsf{CS}_3\)}
\]
Thus the all-level Popovych problem is exactly the assertion that the
Conway-selected discrete logarithm \(r_n\) is a unit modulo \(F_n\) at
every level.

This description is noncircular only with the independent generator
\(\omega\).  Defining the prime above two by
\(\zeta_N\bmod\mathfrak P=w_n\) would already assume that \(w_n\) has
order \(N\).  With an honest primitive \(\omega\), the undetermined
coordinate \(r_n\bmod\ell\) remains exactly the original obstruction.
The ambient presentation in \(\mathbf Q(\zeta_N)\), the local logarithm,
and the principal ideal of the circular unit do not determine it.  The
minimal odd cyclotomic conductor is not blind: since
\[
 \zeta_N^{r_n}=\varepsilon_{r_n}^{-1}-1,
 \qquad
 \mathbf Q(\varepsilon_{r_n})
   =\mathbf Q(\zeta_{\delta_n}),
 \qquad
 \delta_n=\frac{N}{\gcd(N,r_n)},
\]
proving that this conductor is \(N\) is equivalent to the original
maximal-order assertion.  Under \(\ell\)-failure it drops to a divisor
of \(N/\ell\), but every nontrivial divisor \(d\mid F_n\) still has
\(\ord_d(2)=2m\), so residue-field degree does not exclude that drop.
The principal-unit part at two is uniquely \(\ell\)-divisible and the
logarithm kills the Teichmuller torsion where \((\mathsf{CS}_2)\) lives;
every \(\varepsilon_{r_n}\) also has unit principal ideal.  Circular-unit
distribution and reciprocity can transport the value in
\((\mathsf{CS}_2)\), but without an additional ancestry-sensitive
congruence they do not determine \(r_n\bmod\ell\).
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The elementary Fermat congruence gives \(\ord_N(2)=2m\), so
\(\mathfrak P_\omega\) has residue field \(\F_{q^2}\).  The element
\(w_n\ne1\).  Put
\(d=\ord(w_n)=N/\gcd(N,r_n)\).  Then \(d>1\) is odd,
\(\zeta_N^{r_n}\) is a primitive \(d\)-th root, and
\(\Phi_d(-1)=1\).  Hence \(1+\zeta_N^{r_n}\) is a cyclotomic unit of
absolute norm one.  Reduction gives
\[
 \varepsilon_{r_n}\bmod\mathfrak P_\omega
  =(1+w_n)^{-1}=c_n.
\]
Put \(h=N/\ell\).  In the residue field \(w_n^q=w_n^{-1}\), and hence
\[
 (1+w_n)^{q-1}
   =\frac{1+w_n^q}{1+w_n}=w_n^{-1}.
\]
Euler's definition of the power-residue symbol now gives
\[
 \varepsilon_{r_n}^{(q^2-1)/\ell}
  =(1+w_n)^{-(q-1)h}
  =w_n^h,
\]
which is \((\mathsf{CS}_2)\).  In the cyclic group of order \(N\),
\(\omega^{r_nN/\ell}\ne1\) exactly when \(\ell\nmid r_n\), and this
is equivalent to retaining the full \(\ell\)-primary part of the
order.  Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-fibotomic} supplies the last
equivalence in \((\mathsf{CS}_3)\).  The Euler-exponent identity used
here is independently kernel-checked in \texttt{Ogdoad.Excess}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the canonical global lift leaves a free cyclotomic torsor]
\label{prop:fermat-canonical-global-lift}
Define a characteristic-zero lift of the complete Conway chain by
\[
 \mathcal K_{-1}=\mathbf Q,\qquad \mathcal A_{-1}=1,
\]
and, for $i\geq0$,
\[
 C_i^2+C_i+\mathcal A_{i-1}=0,
 \qquad
 \mathcal A_i=\mathcal A_{i-1}C_i,
 \qquad
 \mathcal K_i=\mathcal K_{i-1}(C_i).               \tag{\(\mathsf{GL}_1\)}
\]
At two, choose the Hensel branch reducing successively to the Conway
elements $c_i$.  Then $2$ is inert and unramified through
$\mathcal K_n/\mathbf Q$.  On the other hand, $3$ is totally ramified,
with a unique prime at every level.  More precisely, if
\[
 D_i=1-4\mathcal A_i,\qquad Y_i=2C_i+1,
\]
where $D_{-1}=-3$, then
\[
 Y_i^2=D_{i-1},
 \qquad
 2D_i=Y_i^3-Y_i^2-Y_i+3,                            \tag{\(\mathsf{GL}_2\)}
\]
and $D_i$ is a uniformizer at the prime above three.

Put
\[
 W_i=-\frac{C_i+1}{C_i}.
\]
Then, characteristic-freely,
\[
 \mathcal A_{i-1}=\frac{W_i}{(W_i+1)^2},
 \qquad
 \mathcal A_i=-\frac{W_i}{(W_i+1)^3}.
\]
Every $C_i$, $C_i+1$, $\mathcal A_i$, and $W_i$ is an algebraic unit;
in particular
\[
 \operatorname{div}(W_i)=0,
 \qquad
 \Norm_{\mathcal K_i/\mathbf Q}(W_i)=1.             \tag{\(\mathsf{GL}_3\)}
\]
At the prime above three, $C_i\equiv W_i\equiv1$.  Hence, for every
prime $\ell\mid F_n$ with $n\geq1$, $W_n$ is an $\ell$-th power in the
full $3$-adic completion.

At two, all lower-field units are $\ell$-th powers.  The first
reparametrization therefore gives
\[
 [W_n]=-2[C_n]
 \quad\text{in }\mathcal K_{n,\mathfrak p}^{\times}/
                    \mathcal K_{n,\mathfrak p}^{\times\ell}.  \tag{\(\mathsf{GL}_{3a}\)}
\]
For the cyclotomic unit $\varepsilon_{r_n}$ of
Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-cyclotomic-symbol}, the compatible Hensel lifts
satisfy $C_n/\varepsilon_{r_n}\in1+2\mathcal O$.  This principal-unit group
is uniquely $\ell$-divisible, so
\[
 [W_n]=-2[\varepsilon_{r_n}].                       \tag{\(\mathsf{GL}_{3b}\)}
\]
Thus higher two-adic precision retains exactly the same selected Kummer
class, up to an invertible scalar.

Nevertheless global reciprocity does not determine its selected symbol at
two.  Put $M=\mathcal K_n(\zeta_\ell)$.  Then
\[
 \mathcal K_n\cap\mathbf Q(\zeta_\ell)=\mathbf Q,    \tag{\(\mathsf{GL}_4\)}
\]
and the unique prime $\mathfrak p\mid2$ of $\mathcal K_n$ splits completely
in $M/\mathcal K_n$.  Label the primes above it by
\[
 \mathfrak q_a=\tau_a\mathfrak q_1,
 \qquad \tau_a(\zeta_\ell)=\zeta_\ell^a,
 \qquad a\in(\mathbf Z/\ell\mathbf Z)^\times.
\]
For the tame Hilbert-symbol orientation
\[
 (x,y)_{\mathfrak q,\ell}
 =\left(
   (-1)^{v(x)v(y)}\frac{x^{v(y)}}{y^{v(x)}}
  \bmod\mathfrak q\right)^{(|k(\mathfrak q)|-1)/\ell},
\]
write $H=(W_n,2)_{\mathfrak q_1,\ell}$.  Equivariance gives
\[
 (W_n,2)_{\mathfrak q_a,\ell}=H^a,
 \qquad
 \prod_{a\in(\mathbf Z/\ell\mathbf Z)^\times}H^a=1 \quad
 \text{for every }H\in\mu_\ell.                    \tag{\(\mathsf{GL}_5\)}
\]
If $q=2^{2^n}$ and $N=q+1=F_n$, reduction at $\mathfrak q_1$ gives
\[
 H=\left(w_n^{N/\ell}\right)^{-2}.                 \tag{\(\mathsf{GL}_6\)}
\]
Thus $H\ne1$ is exactly the desired full $\ell$-primary order condition.
All finite places away from $2\ell$ are tame-trivial, the place above three
is already power-trivial, and there are no real places.  Global reciprocity
therefore says only
\[
 \prod_{\lambda\mid\ell}(W_n,2)_{\lambda,\ell}=1;   \tag{\(\mathsf{GL}_7\)}
\]
the product over the complete orbit above two is identically one for every
possible $H$.  In fact, for \(n\geq2\) the stronger pointwise statement is
\[
 (u,b)_{\lambda,\ell}=1
 \qquad
 (u,b\in\mathcal K_n^\times,\ \lambda\mid\ell).     \tag{\(\mathsf{GL}_{7a}\)}
\]
Consequently the canonical lift, its principal divisor,
its total $3$-adic ramification, and unweighted global reciprocity do not
select the Conway residue symbol.  Any reciprocity proof must add a genuinely
weighted $\ell$-adic ray or Jacobi coefficient that breaks the torsor in
\((\mathsf{GL}_5)\); proving that coefficient nonzero is a new arithmetic
input, not a consequence of the lifted ancestry alone.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The reductions of the quadratics in \((\mathsf{GL}_1)\) are precisely the
successive irreducible Conway quadratics.  Hensel lifting therefore makes
each local extension at two the unramified quadratic extension and proves
the first assertion.

The identities \((\mathsf{GL}_2)\) follow directly from
$C_i^2+C_i=-\mathcal A_{i-1}$.  Starting from the uniformizer
$D_{-1}=-3$, the first identity is Eisenstein at the unique prime over three.
In the resulting ramified quadratic extension, the linear term $-Y_i$ in
the second identity has strictly smallest valuation, so $D_i$ is again a
uniformizer.  This proves total ramification inductively.

The relative norm of each of $C_i$ and $C_i+1$ is
$\mathcal A_{i-1}$.  Induction therefore makes them, and hence
$\mathcal A_i$ and $W_i$, units.  The nontrivial relative conjugation sends
$W_i$ to $W_i^{-1}$, proving \((\mathsf{GL}_3)\).  At three,
$Y_i\equiv0$ gives $C_i\equiv1$ and then $W_i\equiv1$.  Since
$\ell$ is odd and different from three, powering by $\ell$ is an
automorphism of both the order-two residue-unit group and the pro-$3$
principal units.

Solving $W_i=-(C_i+1)/C_i$ for $C_i$ and using
$C_i^2+C_i=-\mathcal A_{i-1}$ gives the two displayed rational
reparametrizations.  At two, $\ell\mid q+1$ is coprime to the lower
residue-group order $q-1$, proving \((\mathsf{GL}_{3a})\).  The two
characteristic-zero units $C_n$ and $\varepsilon_{r_n}$ have the same
reduction $c_n$; their ratio is therefore a principal unit, on which odd
$\ell$-powering is an automorphism.  This proves \((\mathsf{GL}_{3b})\).

Every nontrivial subfield of $\mathcal K_n$ is ramified at three, whereas
$\mathbf Q(\zeta_\ell)$ is not; since the latter has abelian Galois group,
this proves \((\mathsf{GL}_4)\).  The residue field at $\mathfrak p$ is
$\F_{q^2}$ and contains $\mu_\ell$, so $\mathfrak p$ splits completely in
$M$.  Hilbert-symbol equivariance proves the first equality in
\((\mathsf{GL}_5)\), and the second follows because
$\sum_{a=1}^{\ell-1}a$ is divisible by $\ell$.

Finally $W_n\bmod\mathfrak p=w_n$, and the displayed tame convention gives
\[
 H=w_n^{(q^2-1)/\ell}
   =\left(w_n^{N/\ell}\right)^{q-1}
   =\left(w_n^{N/\ell}\right)^{-2},
\]
since $w_n^N=1$.  The standard tame local formula makes all other places
outside $\ell$ trivial as stated.  The product formula now yields
\((\mathsf{GL}_7)\), while \((\mathsf{GL}_5)\) proves that it imposes no
condition on $H$.  For \((\mathsf{GL}_{7a})\), let
\(\mathfrak l=\lambda\cap\mathcal K_n\).  Every prime divisor
\(\ell\mid F_n\), \(n\geq2\), satisfies
\(\ell\equiv1\pmod{2^{n+2}}\), whereas
\([\mathcal K_{n,\mathfrak l}:\mathbf Q_\ell]\leq2^{n+1}\).
Thus \(\mathcal K_{n,\mathfrak l}\) cannot contain
\(\mathbf Q_\ell(\zeta_\ell)\), and the decomposition stabilizer of
\(\lambda\) in \(\Delta\) contains some \(\tau_a\ne1\).  Since
\(\tau_a\) fixes \(u\), \(b\), and \(\lambda\), equivariance gives
\[
 (u,b)_{\lambda,\ell}
 =\tau_a\bigl((u,b)_{\lambda,\ell}\bigr)
 =(u,b)_{\lambda,\ell}^{a}.
\]
The symbol has prime order dividing \(\ell\), so it is one.

Lean independently checks the characteristic-free reparametrization, the
two identities \((\mathsf{GL}_2)\), and the corresponding odd-Kummer
square-class equivalence in \texttt{Ogdoad.Excess}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[the semiprimitive Gauss table remembers only the selected label]
\label{prop:fermat-semiprimitive-label}
Retain Proposition~\ref{prop:fermat-canonical-global-lift}.  Put
\[
 m=2^n,\qquad q=2^m,\qquad
 k_0=\F_q,\qquad k=\F_{q^2},
\]
and identify the residue copy of \(\mu_\ell\subset k^\times\) with the
fixed global copy by inverse reduction at \(\mathfrak q_1\).  Define
\[
 \chi(x)=x^{(q^2-1)/\ell}\in\mu_\ell,\qquad
 \psi(x)=(-1)^{\operatorname{Tr}_{k/\F_2}(x)}.
\]
Thus \(\chi\) has order \(\ell\) and is trivial on \(k_0^\times\).
For \(a\in(\mathbf Z/\ell\mathbf Z)^\times\) and \(b\in k^\times\), put
\[
 G_a(b)=\sum_{x\in k^\times}\chi(x)^a\psi(bx).
\]
Then
\[
 \boxed{
 G_a(1)=q,\qquad
 G_a(b)=q\chi(b)^{-a},\qquad
 G_a(w_n)=qH^{-a}.}                                  \tag{\(\mathsf{SG}_1\)}
\]

For \(\xi\in\mu_\ell\), define the labelled signed period
\[
 P_\xi(b)=
 \sum_{\substack{x\in k^\times\\ \chi(x)=\xi}}\psi(bx)\in\mathbf Z.
\]
Its complete table is
\[
 \boxed{
 P_\xi(b)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\frac{(\ell-1)q-1}{\ell},
      &\xi=\chi(b)^{-1},\\[2mm]
 \displaystyle-\frac{q+1}{\ell},
      &\xi\ne\chi(b)^{-1}.
 \end{cases}}                                         \tag{\(\mathsf{SG}_2\)}
\]
Both values are integers because \(\ell\mid q+1\).  In particular,
\[
 H=1
 \iff
 P_1(w_n)=\frac{(\ell-1)q-1}{\ell},                 \tag{\(\mathsf{SG}_3\)}
\]
whereas for \(H\ne1\), one has
\(P_1(w_n)=-(q+1)/\ell\).

Consequently the unordered period multiset, every symmetric function of
that multiset, all Gauss absolute values, and all principal-ideal or
Stickelberger data are independent of \(H\).  Explicitly,
\[
 |G_a(w_n)|=q,\qquad
 (G_a(w_n))=(q),\qquad
 G_a(w_n)G_{-a}(w_n)=q^2,
\]
and
\[
 \prod_{a=1}^{\ell-1}G_a(w_n)=q^{\ell-1}.            \tag{\(\mathsf{SG}_4\)}
\]
The datum which remains is exactly the label of the unique exceptional
period, equivalently the root-of-unity phase
\[
 \frac{G_a(w_n)}q=H^{-a}.
\]
Thus the semiprimitive Gauss table gives a two-valued integral
reformulation of the selected problem, but not a nonvanishing theorem.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For \(y\in k^\times\), consider the \(k_0^\times\)-line
\(yk_0^\times\).  For \(t\in k_0\),
\[
 \psi(ty)=(-1)^{
  \operatorname{Tr}_{k_0/\F_2}
  (t\operatorname{Tr}_{k/k_0}(y))}.
\]
Because \(k/k_0\) is quadratic of characteristic two,
\[
 \operatorname{Tr}_{k/k_0}(y)=0
 \iff y^q=y
 \iff y\in k_0.
\]
Hence
\[
 \sum_{t\in k_0^\times}\psi(ty)=
 \begin{cases}
 q-1,&yk_0^\times=k_0^\times,\\
 -1,&yk_0^\times\ne k_0^\times.
 \end{cases}                                          \tag{\(\mathsf{SG}_5\)}
\]
The second row follows because the corresponding nontrivial additive
character sums to zero on \(k_0\), and deleting \(t=0\) removes one.

The character \(\chi^a\) is constant on these lines and is a nontrivial
character of the cyclic quotient \(k^\times/k_0^\times\).  Its sum over
that quotient is zero.  The exceptional line has character value one, so
\[
 G_a(1)
 =(q-1)-\sum_{\substack{C\in k^\times/k_0^\times\\C\ne k_0^\times}}
       \chi(C)^a
 =(q-1)-(-1)=q.
\]
Changing variables \(y=bx\) gives
\(G_a(b)=\chi(b)^{-a}G_a(1)\).  Moreover
\[
 \chi(w_n)
 =w_n^{(q^2-1)/\ell}
 =\left(w_n^{(q+1)/\ell}\right)^{q-1}
 =\left(w_n^{(q+1)/\ell}\right)^{-2}
 =H,
\]
because \(q\equiv-1\pmod\ell\).  This proves
\((\mathsf{SG}_1)\).

Each fibre of \(\chi\) consists of \((q+1)/\ell\)
\(k_0^\times\)-lines.  In \(P_\xi(b)\), the unique line on which the
additive character is trivial is \(b^{-1}k_0^\times\), whose
\(\chi\)-label is \(\chi(b)^{-1}\).  If that line belongs to the fibre,
its contribution is \(q-1\) and the remaining \((q+1)/\ell-1\) lines
contribute \(-1\); otherwise all \((q+1)/\ell\) lines contribute
\(-1\).  This proves \((\mathsf{SG}_2)\).  Its two values differ by
\(q\), proving \((\mathsf{SG}_3)\).

Finally \(H\) is a root of unity, so multiplication by \(H^{-a}\)
changes neither absolute values nor principal ideals.  Pairing \(a\) with
\(-a\), or using
\(\sum_{a=1}^{\ell-1}a\equiv0\pmod\ell\), proves
\((\mathsf{SG}_4)\).

More generally, let \(\rho_0\) be a multiplicative character of
\(k_0^\times\) and put
\(\rho=\rho_0\circ\Norm_{k/k_0}\).  Since
\(\Norm(w_n)=1\), the same change of variables gives
\[
